The Great King of Tantras Called Glorious Vajraḍāka (Śrīvajraḍākanāmamahātantrarāja)

The Great King of Tantras called Glorious Vajraḍāka, known in Sanskrit as Śrī Vajraḍāka Nāma Mahātantrarāja and in Tibetan as Gyü kyi Gyalpo Chenpo Pal Dorjé Khandro, is a major esoteric scripture centered on the deity Vajraḍāka. After an homage to the noble Avalokiteśvara, the Great Compassionate One, it praises Vajraḍāka as the self-arisen Blessed One and the very reality of all ḍākas, a deity of supreme bliss who is neither passion nor free of passion and who embodies nondual wisdom. The tantra goes on to present teachings meant for practitioners who keep the tantric commitments, a domain that its opening verses describe as encompassing sources, modes of sound, and ways of release. It is Toh 370 in the Kangyur, where it belongs to the tantra section and is traditionally associated with the Cakrasaṃvara cycle of the highest class of yoga tantras, often regarded as one of that cycle's explanatory tantras.

In the language of India: śrī vajraḍākanāma mahātantrarājā. In the language of Tibet: The Great King of Tantras called Glorious Vajraḍāka.

Homage to the Blessed One, the Great Compassionate One, the noble Avalokiteśvara.

The hero who is the very reality of all ḍākas,
Vajraḍāka, supreme in bliss,
in the delight of the supreme secret
abides forever as the self of all.
He is the self-arisen Blessed One,
the one and only deity above all.
The sources, the modes of sound, and the ways of release
are the domain of those who practice the commitments.

Hard to find in all three worlds,
he truly abides at the beginning, middle, and end.
He is not passion, nor is he free of passion,
and he cannot be found even in the middle.
He is the net of illusion of all women;
he is the supreme nondual wisdom.
Since all incline toward gain and pleasure,
even those of evil conduct will reach accomplishment.

Because she has mastered moving throughout the sky,
she is renowned as the ḍākinī.
The mudrā, varied in every way,
through the binding of all that is varied,
joined in union with all the ḍākinīs,
is established as the one called Vajraḍāka.

Yogins whose minds are without suchness,
who take a deity as their object,
their minds fixed everywhere on images,
cast figures and the like, do not give rise to yoga.
In the yoga of beings bent on awakening,
one's own self, lord of yogins, is the chief.
This mind of awakening is the vajra;
it is the nature of all the buddhas.

Since by its nature it abides as the kin of beings,
cloaked in a variety of forms,
therefore, with every effort,
one should worship it in a variety of ways.
Oneself is all the buddhas
and all the heroes as well;
through the union with one's own deity
one should fully accomplish oneself.

"Through this one will become all the buddhas,
all the heroes as well,
and all the vajra holders,
in this very life."
So spoke Vajraḍāka, supreme in bliss,
joined in union with all the ḍākinīs,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the glorious Blessed One.

All things have the characteristic of space,
yet space itself has no characteristic.
All beings of the three realms,
without exception, are like illusions.
The beings, both the stable and the moving,
cannot be found as objects;
all things that are seen and touched
are exactly like an illusion.
That by which fools are bound,
by that very thing the wise are here set free.
Through this union of the mudrā
the three worlds without exception should be consumed.
If, however, one practices union otherwise,
one passes away like a lamp that lacks its oil.

Form is the Buddha Vairocana,
feeling is Vajrasūrya,
perception is Padmanarteśvara,
formations are Vajrarāja,
consciousness is Vajrasattva.
These, the nature of all things,
are renowned as the one called Vajraḍāka.
The one deity of the great reality,
who bestows every bliss,
by the nature of the great illusion
is called passion free of passion.

The earth element is Pātanī,
likewise the water element is Māraṇī;
the fire element is Ākarṣaṇī,
likewise wind is Narteśvarī;
the space element is Mahāmāyā,
and she herself is called the Goer.
Joined in union with all the buddhas,
the ḍākinī is the supreme of the blazing ones.
Through the nature of the five elements
she abides as the vajra queen.

The two eyes are declared the vajra of delusion,
hearing is called the vajra of aversion,
the nose is the vajra of avarice,
the mouth is said to be the vajra of attachment,
the body is the vajra of jealousy.
Joining all the sense bases in union
is Vajraḍāka, supreme in bliss.

All these forms are emptiness.
With the eye that sees in the manner of emptiness,
the supreme meditation, free of doubt,
one should look without conceptualization.
With a mind that is in the manner of space,
there all things are the reality of space;
endowed with meditation free of doubt,
nonconceptuality will arise.
So spoke Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder.

Then the Blessed One entered into the equipoise of the samādhi of the stage called "the unobstructed and unfailing commitment of great bliss of all the tathāgatas," and spoke these words:

As long as the supreme wise one
remains in the abode of saṃsāra,
for that long, for the incomparable benefit of beings,
he does not pass into nirvāṇa.
In this way the holy beings,
unafraid of the noose of craving,
with untiring diligence
always work for the benefit of beings.
To the very limit of the expanse of space
they carry out their aspirations,
and with a mind that strives for the benefit of beings
the hero purifies existence.
For these sons of the gods,
who are always intoxicated with desire,
and for other people full of attachment as well,
he purifies all attachment.
In order to fully accomplish great bliss,
bestowing the extensive kalpa,
the supreme one without beginning or end
taught all the kalpas first.
In time past, what arose before
was the first kalpa, great bliss.
There dwelt the single deity,
the transcendent one of the great vajra mind.
So that benefit might arise for all beings
of the infinite world systems,
through dance with song, music and the rest,
he became the lord of all existence.
I am the perfect gathering of joy
from which all beings arise.

Then, having abandoned the attachment of desire
by which beings come to be born,
and knowing that it is by this that they are tamed,
having abandoned whatever arises from letters,
selfless like space,
he then passed into purity itself.
Then, because he dwelt always in emptiness,
which has no power for the unsurpassed aim
of beings and of the buddhas,
he saw all beings, ruined by emptiness,
all falling into hell;
and once again, through devotion,
he became respectful toward the aim of self and others.
Then all the buddhas dwelling
in all the world systems gathered,
and swiftly they roused me:
oṃ mahāsukha vajrasatva jaḥ hūṃ baṃ hoḥ surata stvaṃ
By merely being roused with this,
it bestows every pure bliss,
accomplishes every desired aim,
and brings forth bliss for all beings.

I am the supreme form of desire,
blessed by all the buddhas.
I myself am the supreme Dharma body,
I am the lord of all the buddhas.
I am the one who steals the union of all the buddhas,
the great bliss of all the buddhas,
the great body of all the buddhas,
the melodious speech of all the buddhas,
the great mind of all the buddhas,
the great offering of all the buddhas,
the great king of all the buddhas,
the lord of all vajra holders,
the chief of all lords of the world,
the master of all jewel holders.
Through equal union with all the ḍākas
one becomes equal to all the buddhas.
Because one is equal to all the buddhas,
one is established as the one called Vajraḍāka.

Then, by the procedure of the great maṇḍala,
one should draw the whole maṇḍala.
In its center, in the proper way,
one should place Vajrasattva,
moon-colored and smiling,
beautiful with a garland of buddhas
emerging from swirling red rays of light,
and fully adorned with every ornament;
with four faces, four arms and three eyes,
adorned with a topknot of matted hair,
accompanied by four goddesses,
and adorned with images of the buddhas.
His main face is peaceful, the right is not white,
and likewise the other, the left, is red;
the back face is yellow like refined gold.
Seated on a skull, dancing,
he terrifies the Thirty-Three gods.
He holds a vajra and a sword,
a bell and a skull cup.
Filled with a circle of ḍākinīs,
the various protectors should be drawn.
"Vajraḍāka Saṃvara,
in equal union with all the ḍākas":
so spoke Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder.

Mother, sister and daughter,
and likewise female relatives:
one who fully accomplishes all siddhis
should take passion with them through the union of desire.
Beef, horse meat and the like
should be eaten without hesitation.
In the world one should always act with the left.
Since all beings, the stable and the moving,
have arisen from the left,
one should eat with the left and worship the left.
Apart from the messenger (dūtī), toward others
one should not go, deluded by desire.
Always devoted to the messenger,
one will surely become liberated, without doubt.
The practitioner, making supreme effort,
abandons what is not the observance and perfects the observance.
Practicing with the family of the commitment,
one should eat the caru together.
Practicing everything with the five families,
one should regard them as a single family.
Honey and blood, together with camphor,
combined with red sandalwood
and mixed with vajra water:
these become the five nectars
that accomplish all siddhis.
Joining the thumb and the ring finger,
the one who knows yoga should always consume them.
If one tastes them like a peaceful drink,
one will always obtain siddhi.
These are the sacred commitments,
explained to you, O lovely-faced one.

Being endowed with method in all actions,
whatever one practices without hesitation,
and meditating without conceptualization:
this is the supreme of supreme observances.
In all actions, at all times,
meditating without conceptualization,
and acting everywhere without hesitation:
that is the supreme of austerities.
With a mind free of doubt,
relying on all the objects without exception
of hair, moon and the rest, the things that follow:
this austerity is very hard to surpass.

Whatever actions there may be,
one joins them all with wisdom,
and that too one applies to the state of emptiness.
That, here, is the austerity of the great self.

With a nonconceptual mind,
toward a form equal to wisdom,
to engage in conduct without hesitation:
that is the austerity of the great self.

When, apart from wisdom and means,
the mind does not stir toward anything else,
then great awakening
is declared to be surely bestowed.

In following nonconceptuality
the yogin strives intently.
That is the perfection of wisdom
from which all things arise.

When the modes of conditioned things
are fully known by a nonconceptual mind
to be like a mirror,
that is relying on wisdom.

When the modes of conditioned things,
ripening like lightning and like bubbles,
are seen by someone just as they are,
that is relying on wisdom.

Whoever sees them as like a reflection in a mirror,
a dream, an illusion, a city of gandharvas,
or a magical display,
that one is declared to be foremost.

That one has no clinging anywhere,
and no abiding anywhere.
Even so, things do truly arise:
illusion-like entities
give rise to superior enjoyments,
and since they continually turn into bliss,
why should one not desire
to attain the superior fruit?

There are no stable or moving entities,
yet their characteristic is mere appearance.
When the power of familiarization
in some special meditation is complete,
the pleasing fruit of bliss,
the unsurpassed power of a clear mind,
comes about. If, seeking the fruit, one accomplishes its cause,
O goddess, who could prevent it?
Therefore, with every effort,
one should at all times worship the Goer.

Through harsh austerities and restraints
the body withers and suffers.
If the mind is distracted by suffering,
through distraction the accomplishment turns elsewhere.
If body and mind become stable,
all bliss becomes stable.
When it is shaken by suffering,
it goes to utter cessation.
Therefore, if body, speech and mind
are made stable, one attains awakening.
Otherwise there is untimely death,
and one will surely burn in hell.

So spoke Vajraḍāka, the supreme bliss,
united with all the ḍākas,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder.

From the great king of tantras, the glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the first chapter, on the wisdom that enters the sublime domain where suchness appears.

Then the queens once more made offerings to the Blessed One, the Buddha, bowed to him, and made this request: "Blessed One, may the deity please explain the essence that utterly destroys all hostility, the essence by which the wicked ones, gods and others who tear apart the teaching of all the tathāgatas, and other wicked people as well, are tamed, crushed, made dispirited, brought down, and pacified, so that they will cause no harm until one reaches the heart of awakening."

Then the Blessed One, lord of the vajra body, speech and mind of all the tathāgatas, sang out in song and spoke these words:

First, beginning from Vairocana,
there, at the ends of the letter classes,
the end of dha and bha of the first ta,
adorned on top with the third of the vowels;
the end of sa one views by oneself.
The end of sa, seated on a seat between la and sa,
is to be joined.
It is purified by the two ends.
The end of sa resting on an a seat;
again, the end of sa shown with the five arrows,
adorned with a crescent moon and a drop.
The seed that dwells between la and sa
is fully purified with the ten arrows.
To what dwells between śa and sa,
adorned with three faces,
and to the seed dwelling in the direction of the wind,
adorned with ru ta:
having taken hūṃ hūṃ ha,
fill it completely with the ninth vowel.
Place what is called phaṭ.
Fill the remaining foot of ha.
That very one dwelling in the middle of the fifth
is called ha jo.
The end of sa resting on a ra seat
is joined with the arrow called i.
Again, the end of what is called pa;
the arrow is fully purified by sarba.
There the letter ra mounts the end of sa,
with what is called e as an ear ornament,
adorned with the vowel i, a crescent moon and a drop.
The end of sa mounted by the letter ra;
again, the end of sa endowed with five sounds,
its crown adorned with a crescent moon and a drop.
Again, recite that very protector.
The last of the jewel is adorned with phaṭ.
With the variegated Vairocana
all the phrases are to be filled.

So spoke Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder.

The binding of the net of ḍākinīs
united with the six heroes:
on a sun disk upon a variegated lotus,
there, dwelling in the center of a karma vajra
that has arisen from hūṃ,
one meditates on the reality of hūṃ.
From its blazing light rays of many colors
the host of ḍākinīs is sent forth.

With one face, two arms and three eyes,
red in color, she is greatly fierce,
spear-like in shape, of terrifying form,
with red eyes and tawny hair standing on end.
She is endowed with perfect youth
and clad in a garment of tiger skin.
With her curling tongue she is most terrifying;
she terrifies even the terrifying.
"Vajraḍāka, renowned, in union with all the ḍākinīs":
thus Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One (the Buddha), the Vajra Holder, proclaimed.

That which ends the karma accumulated before,
together with fire, is recited twice.
kuru kuru, apart from the sattva;
matted locks, twisted, and bandha, made clear:
that is to be recited once again.
That which is free of the world's own form
is called trāsaya and is uttered;
that sublime one is recited twice.
The one called kṣa, joined with the sound o,
with bhaya properly placed at its side:
that too is to be recited again.
The last letter, after sa, rests on a sun seat,
filled with ten arrows, a crescent moon and a dot:
that too is to be said again.
The last letter after ṣa, purified by the maker of light,
with "please depart" placed at its side,
is to be doubled.
The one called phe, mounted on the moon and bright with a dot:
that too is to be recited again.
The one called phaṭ is proclaimed with a drawn-out cry.
The fuel of all conceptual thought:
daha daha, the vow of the ḍākinīs,
joined with the very first of the five groups:
that chief of the group is to be recited again.
bhakṣa bhakṣa it is called, and
basa rudhira antra,
mālāpalaṃbine, and
gṛhṇa gṛhṇa sapta, and
pātalagata bhujaṃga;
sarbbaṃ bātarjaya is to be doubled.
The letter ā and the letter ka,
joined to the third of the third,
with the fourth, resting on the wind:
these are to be recited twice.
The last letter, after ṣa, rests on the sun
and is to be filled with the fourth letter:
that, again, is recited twice.
Seated on the seat of the last of the five groups,
ka, ṣa and a, endowed with a dot:
that seed is to be recited twice.
The last letter after sa, with two arrows;
again the last after sa, joined with three arrows;
likewise the last after sa, on a seat of five arrows:
all of these are crowned
with the crescent moon and the dot,
and they are to be repeated again and again.
ka, sa, ca and dha
are held by the supreme of the sound i,
and the last of li is joined to each one:
these too are repeated again and again.
The last letter after sa, mounted on the sound u,
again refined with crescent moon and dot:
accomplish the jewel called phaṭ.
Fully held by many flowers
of various kinds, of parts and not parts,
these are the goddess Mahāmāyā,
who grants every accomplishment.

By merely speaking this,
all the world-systems were shaken,
all the tathāgatas were frightened,
and then again all the tathāgatas
became fully awakened into enlightenment.
All the mountains trembled and split apart.
The hosts of mātṛkās and the hosts of bhūtas,
grahas, fevers and venoms,
yakṣas, rākṣasas, vināyakas and the rest,
all the hostile ones were tamed and destroyed.
Pacified, they were summoned and placed
in the center of the maṇḍala,
bound by the pledge,
and brought under control.
"All the evil of the hell-beings
is torn apart, utterly destroyed and crushed":
thus Vajraḍāka, supreme bliss,
in union with all the ḍākinīs,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder, proclaimed.

A square with four doors,
complete with gatekeepers;
in its center a lotus is to be drawn,
radiant and shining with great red rays of light,
and encircled outside by a round line.
In its center is the Blessed One,
the great wrathful one, adorned with four faces.
In his mouths the fangs are slightly bared;
one face is laughing, together with wrath.
The right is white and peaceful,
the left is red and most terrifying,
the back is the color of yellow light.
He is adorned with four arms:
in his left hands the hero holds the ṭvāṃ staff
and likewise a skull cup;
in his right, a blazing vajra,
and he is adorned especially with an ornament.
He is endowed with the four knowledges
and closely adorned with forms of buddhas.
The hero's consort, Lovely Joy,
holds an arrow and a bow.
The sattva is blue, blazing in nature,
with a red maṇḍala of light.
Towering like the fire of a charnel ground,
fierce as the time of destruction,
on his hair blazing like fire
he binds a crown of skulls.
He sports together with the vetālas
and moves in the dance of Heruka.
In the delightful city of the mātṛkās
the lord of the earth sports.
Everywhere, by all,
in all things, the self of all,
Vajraḍāka, supreme bliss,
in union with all the ḍākinīs:
through the practice of meditative concentration and mantra recitation
he is accomplished here, without doubt.
By practicing at the four times,
having meditated on Saṃvara the unsurpassed,
if one recites the great vajra a hundred thousand times,
one becomes equal to the king of the wrathful ones.
As holder of the great vajra, one gains life,
complexion, youth, strength and prowess,
accomplishments and great miraculous powers,
and becomes formidably able to work for the good of beings.

Moreover, once it has been accomplished, the mantra practitioner, by reciting it a single time, will perform a full hundred actions, namely: subjugating all that moves and does not move, causing to fall, driving away, terrifying, sealing, stupefying, paralyzing, turning back, deluding, binding, splitting, scattering, destroying, driving mad, chopping up, banishing, sowing division, annihilating without trace, stealing wealth, gathering, striking dumb, binding, making one beat oneself, causing to kill, blinding, destroying the seed, seizing with fever, poison and venom, shaking the earth, sending to hell, transferring into an animal womb, turning a man into a woman, expelling what dwells in the womb, damming water, making a eunuch, arousing lustful desire, killing, causing to be killed by another, binding weapons, dulling the hearing, making naked, the iron fetter of the bhaga and anus, talk of the bhaga and anus, causing laughter, the disease of discharging blood and semen, crushing, producing diseases of the blood, leprosy and the like, grief, suffering, staking, bonds, cutting into pieces, grinding the whole body to powder, stabbing pain, attachment to everything, summoning, causing to enter, agitating, dancing, singing, causing laughter,

causing to babble, displaying ill omens, applying it to all rites of acquiring and of bringing under control, employing as servants, subduing all gods, nāgas, yakṣas, rākṣasas, vināyakas, tamers, bhūtas, mātṛkās, ḍākinīs and the rest, granting boons, performing the rite of knowing all of earth, water, fire, wind and space, victory over Brahmā, Viṣṇu and Maheśvara (Śiva), destroying all attachments, release from fevers, poison, venom and ḍākinīs, rejoicing in the freedom from desire of all the tathāgatas, and removing the weariness with saṃsāra of all the bodhisattvas.

For accomplishing all rites,
this, O goddess, has been taught.
"This very king of mantras
has not been before and will not come to be."
Thus spoke the Blessed Vajra Holder,
the Tathāgata Vajrasattva,
renowned as the ḍākinī
united with all illusions.

From the Rite of All Actions of the great king of tantras, the Glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the second, the division into stages of the mantra rite magically displayed by Mahāmāyā.

Next, the secret is to be explained,
in brief and not at length.
From the union with all illusions
all rites are magically displayed;
through the miraculous power of all illusions
all lordships are accomplished,
the great knowledge of bhūtas and grahas,
by which one gathers in and sends forth.

himavantasya uttareṇa bhabatsivo namaḥ mahāsamo sovi addhama sihaṃ behimasehimasehi batipujvi isonido mahinīdo kumbhakaṇḍa sannido sāgara sasadyapaḍabesina sannidaṃ baṃdhāmi kukurasassapiḍalasa tuṇḍa bandhāmi javasuru udani jabaradi byabhaba adhi atato barṇṇa anubadena sādhunmamacariya asarbbasiddhana svāhā

Thus spoke the Blessed Vajra Holder,
the Tathāgata Vajrasattva.

If this mantra is recited seven times and placed on the roof of a house, the people of the household will be deprived of mindfulness.

basahatolimijña a basabajanamohi esabijjacahi namanti indrana ha a bā i jabena dinnanidha tampiha mahāsvaba anena bādhosa rījakula khaṇḍaḍaṇiḍa jebā ibadha tadhiṇataṇa namaḥ sidhana namaḥ pupacajja pābadana

This is called the Ox Face.

masarabaṇe tadyathāre mahādaṇḍedahaheduna hehe svāhā

If this mantra is consecrated seven times over sand and the sand is scattered over a field, no creatures, such as wild animals, will eat from it. Merely by its command, clouds will be turned back.

ratnarati oṃ alli bili barculli cora baggha kanda sabāmuha bandhāmi svāhā

If this mantra is recited seven times over a stick four finger-widths long and the stick is set in the right ear, all thieves will be bound.

indasa atiṇi jaha a kali lohidhi piṅgalima mekabhani ahaṃ sila usarimi abarbhaṇi ahaṃ bāda usarami abara bhaṇami ahandheha mibhitoba jajapa mi lolo kali lohidi piṅgali hūṃ phaṭ

If one takes earth that has stuck to the tip of a bull's horn and has not fallen to the ground, and recites this mantra over it seven times, then no harm of any kind will arise on the frontier, on the roads through pastures and wildernesses, or even in the king's palace. This same is also the rite of the Ox Face. By this rite of reciting the mantra over a shoot of oleander, wind, rain and hail will be turned back.

oṃ jaṃ bili a bijeja sutithadasi isubhadeo saṇa o acalahi suṇṇapatane hisilabada barasapajahe svāhā

If one pours water into fine flour, recites this mantra seven times, and scatters it into the sky toward the quarter of the wind, wind, rain, hail and the like will be turned back.

nayamoṃdhe bhagavate kibhikureṇa śatraya thamakehathakarvākenitasaṃrubitibibhatuya ruchareṭaramaṭakuketoliṭakuketoliṭluya ludaṃ hiṣṭhale kasirale lotigrale bhiṣiṣalaṇe simulekha ayathe sarbbahadesrana bhujakubhanasutheraṣaya rayatiṣudhayan sopabyatitakasuselakakhaseḍagheghasedhagharisini mabyāhaghaghajisenaghaṃbasara ghadhasersa aha a oṃ hapiruga aṃ bralāndhakṣaka ara hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ paphapuṭaṣa

With o bearing the crescent and the drop,
one should beautify the eight-footed mantra;
it is adorned by adding svāhā,
and at end after end one adds the supreme phaṭ.
Merely by its being spoken,
the nāgas are terrified and faint.

The application of this king of mantras is this. The mantra practitioner should meditate on himself as Vajrahūṃkara, dwelling on the maṇḍalas of wind and fire, four-faced and four-armed, the color of a blue lotus, his head adorned with a garland of the flowers of the five buddhas, blazing like the fire at the end of the eon, sending forth vajraḍākinīs from every part of his limbs, and from his four faces proclaiming hūṃ four times.

Then take two bowls. On the bottom of both bowls, on maṇḍalas of wind and fire marked with their own emblems and blazing, draw a maṇḍala of the eight nāgas with nine squares. In the center of the maṇḍala draw the nāga Śeṣa, his head adorned with seven hoods, in a slightly drawn-in half-crossed posture. Seated on his left thigh, draw a nāginī with three hoods, embracing him with her left arm. In the same way, in the outer compartments of the maṇḍala draw Ananta and the others, and on their hoods set phuḥ interspersed with hūṃ. All of them hold a sword in the right hand. On their foreheads too place hūṃ phuḥ hūṃ, and on the soles of their feet draw jaḥ. Their feet are bound by other nāgas, and on the hoods of these too draw hūṃ phuḥ hūṃ.

Then, with this mantra,

oṃ dhri anilatarabegadithibibarṣeṣeyahiru sarbanāgakulabyanaya phuḥ samaye prabeśaya ābeśaya phuḥ sarbbanasādhaya phuḥ phuḥ phuḥ phuḥ

encircle the maṇḍala; and again, with this mantra and the mudrā, summon them and make them enter their respective bodies. The mudrā is this: join the thumbs together, bind the hands and place them facing outward, and make the remaining fingers like a serpent's hood.

One recites jaḥ hūṃ vaṃ hoḥ at the end of the mantra, and performs the summoning, the absorbing and so on. Then one fills that bowl with pungent oil, lights the wick, and covers it with another bowl. One visualizes that bowl as Mount Meru, and on top of it, from hūṃ, visualizes the maṇḍala of the wrathful deities. One recites the mantra of Vajrahūṃkāra one hundred and eight times and sets the bowl down according to the rite. Then one imagines that the water streaming from the bodies of the deities of the wrathful maṇḍala, Vajrahūṃkāra and the rest, visualized upon the bowl, is scattered over the maṇḍala of Vajrasūrya. Oneself, possessed of the yoga of Vajrahūṃkāra, roars four hūṃs from four faces, and visualizes two hūṃs at the tips of the index fingers of the bound mudrā of Vajrahūṃkāra. One imagines that the garlands of flame arising from them drive back the clouds. Alternatively, one may recite the mantra of glorious Vajrahūṃkāra. By this practice one should repel the clouds entirely to a distance of eight cubits outside the maṇḍala.

By the method of the great maṇḍala,
One should draw the outer maṇḍala.
In its center is a lotus,
White in color and beautiful;
Dwelling in the center of a sun disk upon it,
One should visualize the seed of wisdom.
Dwelling in the center of wind and fire,
One should visualize the maṇḍala of water.
In the center of the maṇḍala of Mahendra,
Having clearly visualized the radiant maṇḍala,
Through the union of moon and vajra
One should visualize Vajraḍāka:
He has one face and four arms,
Sits on an elephant seat with the right leg extended,
Has three eyes, is adorned with loosened hair,
Of beautiful form, youthful, perfect in every way,
Adorned with a garland of skulls,
Red in color, with radiant luster,
Wearing exceedingly beautiful garments.
He holds a blazing sword and a skull cup,
And draws a bow and arrow.
Delighting in blood, arisen from hrīḥ,
Garlands of red light swirling about him,
He terrifies even the terrifying.
Four-sided, with four doors,
Visualize the wheel of the ḍākinīs.
A fine canopy is spread above,
Banners stirred by the wind are raised,
And cloths of many kinds are hung.
With five kinds of colored powder,
Such as charnel-ground brick and the like,
To a measure of just four cubits,
A maṇḍala for merely wholesome activities and the like.
Then, to make the pond,
One spreads blue powder as the ground,
And draws waves, ripples and the like.
With a form filled with great colors,
One draws creatures of many kinds,
Such as fish and makaras,
And adorns the outside with crows and the like.
Then, according to this rite,
One sets out the great vidyā and the eight nāgas.
At the four doors their two heads are crossed,
Their tails coil round the four corners.
In the east is the nāga king Ananta,
His color like a blue lotus,
His seven hoods spread wide;
He is set out together with his nāginī,
Holding a lotus in his hand.
In the north is Vāsuki, yellow;
In the west is Takṣaka, red;
In the south is Karkoṭa, pale green;
In the northeast is Śaṅkhapāla.
In the northwest Padma, in the southwest
Mahāpadma should be set out.
In the southeast is Kulika:
Draw them by the rite given before.
All eight of the nāgas
Wear lovely jeweled crowns
And are adorned with every ornament.
With the light of the jewels on their hoods
They dispel all darkness.
Palms joined, and frightened,
They let streams of water fall from their hoods.
The lords of the clouds, the kings,
Should be summoned from the eight directions:
Thunderer and Gloom,
And likewise Fierce Summoner;
Victor, Excellent, Glorious Joyful One,
Rain-Pourer and Lovely One,
Swift One and Sarasvatī,
Fierce, Pourer, Full and Mover.
These are called the eight cloud kings,
So said Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
United with all the ḍākinīs,
The Blessed One, the Vajra Holder.

On the skulls of two women, marked with the name, one draws two fire maṇḍalas with gorocana and blood. At the corners of the fire maṇḍala one places the letters kṣuṃ and hriḥ, and in the center one writes the name of the girl who is the object of the rite. One encircles it with three lines and surrounds the maṇḍala in three layers with the seed syllable of Moja or with the letter ra. Having ascertained which is the top and which the bottom of the skulls, one heats them over a fire of khadira wood at the three times of day. She will then come that very day, or else she will waste away.

In a solitary place without a roof, one smears an image of Vajrasattva with pungent oil and sets it on a beautiful wooden stand. One offers it bathing water and dries it. Together with one's companions in practice, one concentrates on bringing down rain and faces the northeast. If one recites the mantra, Śakra (Indra) sends down rain. If not, one recites it interspersed with the mantra of glorious Vajrapāṇi, and the rain falls without interruption. Or else, on a bowl, one draws as before with the flesh and blood of a two-headed snake and sets it up in the air where the flames do not touch it. If one recites the mantra for bringing down rain, the rain will fall. If one wishes to stop the rain, one stays on a rooftop with flowers that have been offered to the Vajra Holder as incense, goes naked, fumigates the clouds and recites the mantra, and the rain will be turned back.

On an unfired bowl one draws with poison and the like the three maṇḍalas of wind and the rest, and imagines that they arise from their seed syllables. Above the water one also draws an eight-petaled lotus, and at its center one draws nāṃ pressed by two hūṃs. On the eight petals one draws the heart mantras of the eight nāgas, likewise pressed. With the mantra of Mahābala and nine hūṃs one encircles the lotus according to the rite, recites the mantra, and makes offerings with whatever is at hand. Then one joins the bowl mouth to mouth with another and places it in an abode of nāgas, and all the nāgas will pour down streams of rain. If, despite this, hostile nāgas do not send down rain, one should then apply this practice: one recites the seed syllable of Caṇḍa one hundred and eight times and performs a fire offering at the nāga lake, and rain will fall. If even that does not bring rain, then one grinds together white mustard seed, pungent oil, honey and karavīra flowers, makes them into pills, recites over them one hundred and eight times, and puts them into the nāga lake, and rain will fall. If the nāgas, out of hostility, still do not send down rain, their heads will split. If it still does not fall, one takes ash from a charnel ground, steeps it with roots until it is sour, recites the mantra and puts it into the lake. The nāgas become intoxicated and pour down streams of rain.

If even that does not bring rain, then one makes pills the size of a juniper berry from lotus, blue utpala, orpiment, copper powder, white mustard seed, Indra's hand (a salep orchid) and palāśa. One consecrates each pill with seven recitations, and keeps throwing the pills into the lake until the rain falls. Then rain will fall, day and night alike.

Then the Blessed One, the great Vajradhara, spoke this king of mantras:

oṃ namo nīla aṃbara dharāya vajrapāṇaye mahāyakṣa senapataye anekagaṃgānādīpālukopamābuddhakoṭi śatasahasraparibṛdtāya vikṛtaṃ daṃśṭa karālarūpāya aganirāsi nayā nayā jvalita viṣasasvāya bhīmanādāya vasukināgarājābhibhūṣitakaracaraṇāya kriṣṇabhujaṃgāya yajñopavītrāya jvalitabhujamaṇimukuṭāya puritaṭapavādanāya sakrucitābhulatāyadeśanaṣaṇḍaroṣaṇāya kritāntarūpāyavidyujjihvāyamahoragavakṣaṇāya kārasphoṭatrāsanakarāya suvarṇakanakarājamekhalāya hūṃkāranādatrāsanakarāya aśītiyojanashasragaṃbhīrasāgarajānumaṇḍalāya cakrādityaskandhavahanāya daṃśṭākarālāya hāsita aṭṭāṭāhāsanādanādāya mahāsaṃkṣobhakarāya kālarūpāya trailokyabhakṣaṇakaraṇāla nāgānāṃ mṛtyukālāya imaṃ gaṃgānadīvālukasambuddhabhagavadbibhāṣitaṃ mantramāvarttayiṣyāmi bhodhopannāga addhivataye idannīlāṃbardharaśarīraṃ vajrapāṇihṛdayāya samātikramatha mā kulotsādanaṃ bhapiṣyaṃti tadyathā buddha buddha vibuddha vibuddha mahābalokapālinī jahara jahara nāgānāṃ phrahara prahara sarvva nāgānāṃ cchinda cchin nāgānṝ hṛdayāni bhinda bhinda nāgamarmāṇi piśiryantu nāgahṛdayāni sphoṭaya sphoṭaya nāganāyanāni viśiryantu nāgaśarīrāṇi tadyathā hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ nāgavidāraya phaṭ nāgacchindanāya phaṭ nīlavasanāya phaṭ abratihatabalaparakramāya phaṭ kṛtānatarūpāya phaṭ roṣaṇāya phaṭ trāsanāya phaṭ kuṣmaṇḍotsādanakarāya phaṭ bretapiśācapināśakarāya phaṭ yakṣa apasmara vināśakarāya phaṭ hasa hasa dar dar māraya māraya nāśaya nāśaya sarvva vighnaṃ vināśakarāya phaṭ nīlāṃbaradharo vajrapāṇirājñāpayati svāhā

For someone seized by yakṣas, rākṣasas and the like, one recites over water and sprinkles it, and they will be freed. To turn back rain, one should recite over ash, look at the clouds and scatter it. If one recites over water and sprinkles it, rain will fall. For epidemic fever, one recites over a blue thread and ties it to the hand, and the sick person will be freed. When the moon or the sun is seized by Rāhu, one recites a thousand times over gorocana and makes a mark with it on the forehead, and one becomes invisible. Fixing the mind on one's aim, being clean and wearing clean clothes, one burns kunduru incense day and night and recites eight hundred times, and in a dream all that is good and evil will be shown. One recites seven times over earth, looks at robbers and blows it at them, and one will not be seen by the robbers, and they will be turned back. One recites seven times over a flower or a fruit, and whoever it is given to comes under one's power.

Thus:
The supreme bliss of Vajraḍāka,
Joined in union with all the ḍākas,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
The Blessed Vajra Holder, has proclaimed.

From the great king of tantras, the glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the third chapter, on summoning all the nāgas.

Then the goddesses again worshipped the Blessed One with offerings, prostrated three times, and made this request: "Blessed One, divine one, please explain the whole collection of rites."

Then the Blessed One graciously took up this king of vidyās and declared:

ghili ghili varṣaya varṣaya vajada ini variśa variśa paya hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ ho ho ho bhasaṭha ṭha ṭha hasa hasa haha

The syllable o, adorned with crescent and drop,
Abides within the eight clouds.
It is the vidyā-mantra of the ḍākinīs,
The very nature of Mahāmāyā.
As soon as it is uttered,
All the nāgas fall into a swoon.
So Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
The Blessed Vajra Holder, proclaimed.

namaḥ śmaśānavāsini mahākāla anucarāya tadyathā oṃ cale cale pracale lalā lalāya devaliṃga svāhā

Having done ten thousand recitations of this mantra as the approach, at midnight, beside the effigy, one recites until the person comes. This is the rite for summoning someone in bodily form.

oṃ camuṇḍe drumakeśi vidyujihe hana hana daha daha vaca vaca devadatta māraya svāhā

On the sole of one's own left foot one draws Mount Meru with nail dye, and inside it, at the four corners, writes four syllables hrīḥ. In the center one writes the words of the mantra. If one presses down with the foot while reciting, the person is summoned and comes under one's power.

On a peg made from the bone of a man's left shin, one forms in its upper part Gaṇapati, four-armed and looking downward, holding in his right and left hands a hammer, an axe, a noose and a trident. One boils that peg in black butter together with poison, blood, kétsé mustard, salt and clematis, and recites the mantra of Mahābala. The one at whose house door it is hidden will be struck by a great wasting disease; if it is taken out, he will be freed.

oṃ vajrakrodhā mahābala hanadha pāca vidhvansaya udsādāya mārāya hūṃ phaṭ

With earth from an anthill mixed with the five products of the cow, one makes an image of the nāga Vāsuki about a span high and covers it with karavīra shoots. One keeps guggulu incense burning without interruption, offers a torma of the three white substances, and, while reciting, sprinkles water on it without interruption with the karavīra shoots. Within seven days this will bring down streams of rain. If the rain has not fallen, on the seventh day one cuts the image into pieces, puts them into a vase, recites the mantra of the four hūṃs, and casts it into a great river; then rain will certainly fall.

oṃ ga ga a rig gā svāhā. In the morning, before washing your face, recite this mantra seven times over a pebble and put it into any hole whatever. If you then say, "Bind the mouth of so-and-so!", that person's mouth will be bound.

mukha tiṣṭha nati muka gacchati muḥkāḥkarya nati dantalaṃ galaṃ mūkānāṃ mutrapuriṣeṇa devadattasya mukhabandhāmi svāhā. Put cow dung that has not fallen to the ground into a rat hole whose opening faces the sky. If you recite this mantra and say, "Bind the mouth!", then at the royal palace and similar places, and in disputes, mouths will be bound merely by reciting it.

Place akāro mukhaṃ first;
then sarbbadharmmaṇī after it,
join it properly, and again
say ādyanutpannatvāt.
With the application taught before as well,
the Great Wise Lady standing before you,
if you recite this mantra seven times,
those who dwell in the sky will surely
pour down a great abundance of water.

Take the last letter of a class seated on the seat of ra, adorned with the fourth vowel and a bindu. Smear the base of the pillow you sleep on with red sandalwood, and there, with your own blood, write the name in the heart of that syllable. At bedtime scatter red flowers over it and fumigate it with incense, and your woman will become inseparable from you through passion.

Visualize hūṃ on a sun at your heart, and visualize yourself as Trailokyavijaya or another such deity. Using the blood of a vajra-toothed creature, and a young shoot of the jātī creeper as a pen, write on cloth or birch bark, in the center of the seed syllable of wind, the name of the target together with words of urging, placing it between the two seed syllables hūṃ ṭakkī. Perform the acts of summoning and the rest with the hook and other implements, then insert it in the middle of a brick. Wrap it with red thread, and in the wind quarter, the northwest, press it down with your foot, standing with the left leg extended, and recite. The target will then wander over the whole earth as far as the ocean. If it is taken out and washed with milk, he will be released, but not otherwise.

Generate yourself from a red maṃ in the form of a woman, and imagine a red oṃ at the crown of your head as well. If you imagine the garland of the mantra entering from the girl's mouth into your own mouth, according to the rite, she will come under your control.

Make the shape of a vulva from earth where a woman has urinated and earth where you yourself have urinated. Place your own organ on it, recite the mantra seven times, and urinate there. If for seven days, at the three times of day, you recite the mantra one hundred and eight times, even the wife of Śakra (Indra) will come, so what need is there to mention others?

On a man's skull cup, with gorocana and lac dye, draw an effigy of the target, and at its heart write the mantra interspersed with the name. Smear it with scented water, join the two skull cups mouth to mouth, and wrap them all around with thread from a corpse. Smear them with monkey fat, and over a fire of charcoal from a cremated corpse heat them while reciting this king of spells until the fat melts. Through such a rite of action, even the world of the gods will come.

Put the tongue of a mad yakṣa into the skull of a venomous creature, and put that into the cleft of a horse's hoof. Hide it at someone's house door, and within seven days he will be driven away. Or else, at the time of the rite, recite the mantra one hundred and eight times over beer from the first portion of food, and in wrath have the target drink it. Within seven days he will be driven away.

From cow dung that has not fallen to the ground make an effigy of the target, sixteen or twelve and a half finger-widths tall. Write the person's name on a piece of cotton cloth and put it in the belly, fill its mouth with dough, and wrap it with a corpse's cord. Recite the mantra seven times over a pestle, and if you pound its face in wrath, the person will be bound.

On cloth from a hero slain in battle draw a picture of the girl, and fumigate it with dust from an ox and with incense of the root of the pāṭala tree. That girl will then come.

On cloth, birch bark, a wooden board, or a wall, draw the form of the wrathful Yamāntaka, possessing all colors, standing with the right leg extended. In his right hand he holds a mallet and in his left a snake noose. He is creased with wrath, his brows knit, his fangs bared, his eyes red, his hair standing on end; he is adorned with ornaments of snakes and wreathed in blazing light. In the belly of the snake noose, with charcoal from a charnel ground, write the names of the host of enemies interspersed with the mantra, and set it up. As long as it remains, the enemies will be bound. They will not think of doing even the slightest harm, not even in their minds. If they do think of harm, they will be afflicted by bhūtas, by fevers, by grahas, by piśācas, and by ḍākinīs, and the gods too will scatter them. If you intersperse the words of an opposing army, that army will be bound.

Furthermore, on a leaf or the like write the mantra in two rows, and at the end of the second row write the name of the target interspersed with the letters. Tie it up and place it in a clay bowl, defecate there, and fill it with the ash of dried cow dung. Join it with another clay bowl mouth to mouth, draw your breath inward, and bury it underground. If you cover it only with densely packed earth, the enemy will be bound, and it cannot turn out otherwise.

Having said ratna vajra,
then say oṃ and vajra,
and join hrī and phaṭ at the end:
this mantra binds an army.

From black earth make the face of the goddess Cāmuṇḍī. With gorocana write the mantra interspersed with the names of however many targets there are, and put it into her mouth. Worship her mouth with the five kinds of offerings and place it in one corner of the house. If you worship it every day, mouths will be bound and brought under your control.

On a clay bowl draw the effigy of a woman with realgar, and insert your finger into its bhaga. Recite the mantra from the moment the moon is seized in eclipse until it is released. Or else the bhaga of an actual woman will also serve. With the finger accomplished in this way, merely by a movement of that finger, all girls will come.

On a nimba leaf, with poison, your own blood, and the juice of the datura plant, write the syllable phuḥ, and in its center write the name of the target. Put it inside a ball of dough or food, and in the evening feed it to a crow that is about to go to its nest. The target will surely be driven away.

On a wall or a wooden board write the letter tha. Inside it write a surrounding ring of the vowel letters, and inside that write pha. Inside that write ha, and inside ha, ka. Write the name of the target there, seal it shut, and set it in place. The target will come under your control and be bound by the binding.

In the center of a lotus at the navel, meditate on the letters of the name, or on the letter A. Close the lotus, and bind it with the noose of the heart mantra; then one will be protected.

A is, for all sentient beings,
their own seal, their sign and seed.
For protecting life and other aims,
one should bind it firmly with power.

Or else, imagine the letter A, like the light of snow, inside the hub of vajras joined face to face. If one binds its two ends with the noose of the heart mantra, it also becomes the supreme protection.

Imagine the letter BA facing downward, dwelling on the lotus at the heart, and imagine on the navel lotus the letter A, about the size of a barley grain. A stream of nectar flows down from BA like a stream of cow's milk, and one should constantly imagine that A is being bathed by it. By this practice of the meditation of the lotus union, the body becomes stable; life is protected, empowerment is conferred at the heart, and increase becomes perfect and abundant.

On a palm leaf, write the letters of the name of the one to be subdued, interspersed, in a figure with Oṃ joined at the beginning and the end. Fill that figure with charcoal from a charnel ground, put it inside a vase or the like, and seal its mouth with wax or the like. Then, drawing the breath inward, put it inside a heap of filth, and all that moves and does not move will be bound.

From the root of an arka tree, make an image of the Lord of Hosts (Gaṇapati) with four arms. Perform the seed syllable, the summoning, the giving of the pledge, and so on, and anoint it well with salt, black pepper, ginger, indigo, mustard seed, spunpa, and water. At midday, put it into the water of the house, immersed up to the neck and facing south, and worship it with the five kinds of offerings. The mantrin should say this: "If you accomplish this task, called such-and-such, for me and the others, then I will give you offerings in keeping with it, as much as I am able." One should say this and do so for seven days. Then, when the task has been accomplished, take it out, wash it with milk, give offerings as much as one is able, and ask its forbearance.

Fill a vase with scented water, provide it with jewels, medicines, and grains, and recite the mantra of Mahābala one hundred and eight times. Place the chief person on a square maṇḍala and bathe him with that vase. Then write that mantra on birch bark with gorocana and tie it to his crown topknot. If he enters battle, he will be victorious over the opposing army.

On cloth from a charnel ground, or on the cloth of an avadhūtī, write with poison, blood, and the juice of nimba leaves the letters of the mantra one by one, interspersed with the name of the one to be subdued, in reverse order. Tie it up and put it inside a fish bowl. If one hides it in the northwest of the root of a piśāca tree, he will be driven away. If one places it in the southeast, they will come to hate each other. If one hides it secretly in the quarter of Nairṛti, the southwest, he will be killed. If the yogin enters battle with this very rite, the army will be bound. To reverse it, take it out, wash it with milk, and recite the mantra interspersed with the person's name in the proper order.

With realgar, write the name of the one to be subdued on the palm of one's own left hand and warm it over the tip of a butter-lamp flame; he will come under one's control.

Smear a maṇḍala, recite the name mantra over a squeezed ball of dough, and in the evening give it to a crow that is about to go to its nest; this too will drive him away.

Take the rope from the corpse of someone who was strangled, and cut it into three pieces. Take a half-burnt piece of wood from a cremation and make it into a churning stick. In the charnel ground, sit on a wooden seat that wobbles and creaks, tie that rope to the churning stick, and put it into the corpse vase. If one churns with it while reciting the mantra, whoever's name one takes up in the recitation will come.

Again, on an ordinary stone or a whetstone, make the image of a nāga king with three hoods, and place it inside a black clay pot. Worship it with honey, cakes of puffed rice, scents, flowers, incense, lamps, milk porridge, and so on, and place these within that same vessel. Cover it with new cloth, tie it, and seal it. Place one's hand on it and recite one thousand and eight times, then hide it very secretly in the center of the city. As long as it is not taken out, that city will remain stable. If one wishes to summon townspeople who have fled, one should worship there and recite the mantra at the three times of day, and they will come. For townspeople who are beginning to flee, recite over molasses and give it to them to eat, and they will not flee.

Another practice for making a town stable: make a vessel from the clay that a potter has wiped from his hands, recite fully over it one thousand and eight times, and, without exhaling, place it in water; the town will become stable.

If one wishes to kill an enemy, make an effigy out of wax or dough. Wrap the effigy with a monk's robe, starting from the face and ending at the feet, and conceal it well. Place one's hand on it and recite the mantra interspersed with the name one less than a thousand times. Place it in a corner of a house where the gods are worshipped, drive a khadira peg into its heart, and at the three times of day pour hot water onto the peg while reciting fully; he will be destroyed.

If the yogin mixes the ten substances with saffron and makes a forehead mark, he will be favored among people. If one anoints all one's limbs with those substances together with Malaya sandalwood, whoever one touches will not leave one even at death. If one prepares those same substances mixed together with red palāśa, honey, the lord of disease, and the cooker, the people of the earth will always be loving and one will be victorious everywhere; there is no doubt that kings and royal ministers will protect the yogin everywhere.

Having recited the root mantra a hundred thousand times,
thereafter one should undertake the rites.
Otherwise there will be untimely death,
and one will surely burn in hell.
So spoke the one joined in union with all the ḍākas,
Vajraḍāka, supreme in bliss,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder.

From the great king of tantras, the Glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the fourth chapter, the detailed division of the rites called The Arising of the Collection of All Actions.

Then, in the center of the expanse of space,
one should imagine the disc of the sun,
with the sky in all directions and intermediate directions
completely filled with countless families.

At the center of that sun in the expanse of space,
one's own seed syllable, as its emblem,
blazes and churns with piercing rays
surpassing a thousand suns.
Shooting sparks burn up the hosts of māras.
Through the yoga of gathering it into a ball,
from its melting the body arises:
blue in color, greatly fierce,
its rays like the fire at the end of the eon.
With blazing body and a mighty roar
he terrifies even the terrifying.
Vividly, with great laughter, his teeth shine.
The unbroken maṇḍala of goddesses,
a wheel and canopy, abides at the rim,
and again, radiant, each with four arms,
his sides adorned by Mahāmāyā.
The Victor, the stainless stream of liberation,
churns with masses of rays like the finest snow,
and, arising twofold, it shines.
Entering the jewel, his two feet move;
moving to the furthest limit of the Triple City (Tripura) and the rest,
he is supremely beautiful.
He enters the stream of the Victorious,
and with masses of sound from the breath,
going out and coming in and filling all,
he crushes, and the three worlds quake.
All is filled with the many weapons he hurls;
as if overcast by masses of darkness,
he brings terror everywhere.
The gods who dwell in the heavens,
and the Lord of Spirits, Brahmā and the rest,
cannot bear even
the mere edge of his foot's shadow.
Other hosts of obstructors, the deceivers,
rākṣasas and other gods,
cannot come near
even a portion of the hundred thousand leagues
beyond his maṇḍala of light.
Whatever there is or is not in the realm of the gods,
even those whose bodies have some little light,
tremble like dust or smoke.
With the masses of rays of his left face,
which is like a ruby,
they think he will burn everything without remainder.
By his light, like a heaped pile
of bandhūka flowers,
all who have come from every side,
born of the three families, are amazed.
Thinking that this form of light will burn them,
with light surpassing a thousand suns
he spurs them on and brings them to awakening.
With the great sound phaṭ he awakens them,
and in his towering pride he burns all the gods.
The rays of his white right face,
like heaped essence of snow,
like crystal and beryl stone,
like geese, kunda jasmine and the moon,
illuminate with stainless light;
like his own stream of water flowing
between unsullied serpent hoods,
they illuminate all the families of the victors.
The rear face is yellow, like refined gold,
like kusumja flowers,
like a heap of blazing coals,
like the light of the rising sun;
with the corners of its eyes wide and glaring it brings terror.
From that great and exceedingly fierce form
all the lands without exception
hover and churn like the light of meteors.
Hard to look upon and hard to grasp,
he blazes like swirling tongues of flame.
With a great net of vajra arrows
spread across many thousands of leagues
the wheel is bound.
From every single pore
of Kākāsyā, the Crow-Faced, and each of the others,
wheels of ḍākinīs, as many as the atoms of many Mount Merus,
and those whose nature is the three vajras
stream forth and illuminate the sky.
With eight arms, three eyes and hair blazing upward,
encircled by twenty-four ḍākas in a half ring:
thus should the mantra practitioner visualize.

In his first right hand he holds a blue vajra, blazing, sharp and raised, entwined by Kulika. In the second is a supreme arrow bound by Padma. In the third is a sword whose hilt is entwined by Mahāpadma. In the fourth is a hook entwined by the noose of Śaṅkhapāla. In the first left hand he raises a khaṭvāṅga with Ananta tied to it as a banner. In the second is a supreme bow strung with the son of Vāsuki. In the third is the threatening forefinger with the noose of Takṣaka. In the fourth he holds the head of Brahmā, dripping blood, bound with strong sinews. All these nāga kings bare their fangs and curl their tongues; they have poisonous gazes, and with the light of the jewels on their hoods they illuminate the whole expanse of the sky.

Kākāsyā and all the others are adorned with serpent ornaments. They are three-eyed, their hair blazes upward, and in their hands they hold copper knives, khaṭvāṅgas and skull cups. They are adorned with garlands of skulls, naked, their mouths baring fangs, radiating rays of many colors, their brows furrowed in wrathful frowns. If one visualizes that their sidelong glances shake the earth and that the hosts of nāgas fall face down in terror, the nāgas will remain in the sky and send down streams of rain, filling the earth in an instant. Thus

united with all the ḍākas,
the ḍākinī, supreme among the blazing ones,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder, has spoken.

Or else, grind horse dung finely, strain it through cloth, mix it with red lead and knead it with honey. Make a seven-headed figure of a nāga, twelve finger-widths in size. Make a house for it sixteen finger-widths wide. Make that house square, with four faces: on the first side a horse's face, on the second a lion's face, on the third a monkey's face, and on the fourth a fish's face. Inside it place the nāga king, covered with cloth of many colors. Then empower it by reciting the mantra one hundred and eight times. Imagine offerings of flowers, incense, torma and the like, as much as the mind desires, and present them. Enchant white mustard seed seven times and scatter it over the nāga. Then, if one offers a torma of all desirable things, the nāga kings will become one's friends, and in a time of drought all the nāgas will send down rain for seven days and fill all of Jambudvīpa. If this is done at a time when rain does not fall, rain will fall without fail.

If the nāgas are malicious and send down cold winds, showers of stones, lightning and hail, and one wishes to avert this, the vidyādhara should recite over ash and about two zho of white mustard seed up to twenty times and seal the boundary of whatever area is desired. Then, reciting over the same seven more times, one should strike the nāga's head, mentally seal the directions as far as one wishes, and scatter it in the intermediate directions and above and below. The great boundary will then be sealed.

Now, if one wishes to terrify and subdue all enemies, rivals, hateful companions, and those who take the side of hatred, and the "wheel" of the opposing side; or to accuse, bind, kill, paralyze, or destroy them, one should take that same horse's afterbirth, smear it with ash from the charnel ground, and make the form of a man or a woman. Recite the mantra of the Great Wrathful One one hundred and eight times over it. Then name the person and place the figure in a hollow dug in the charnel ground. Recite over white mustard seed one hundred and eight times and scatter it on top. Recite one hundred and eight times over a peg of sengdeng wood, drive it in, and hide it. Then, until the peg is removed, the victim will undergo great suffering, and no one other than a vidyādhara will be able to release him. If one wishes to kill him, recite the mantra one hundred and eight times over a peg of human bone and strike with it. Recite over white mustard seed mixed with blood and scatter it, and he will not live seven days.

Thus spoke the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder, Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata, Vajraḍāka, supreme in bliss, who is united with all the ḍākas.

This is the fifth chapter, the expanded rite for summoning, entering, and binding all the nāgas, from the Great King of Tantras, the Glorious Vajraḍāka.

Then the goddesses again made offerings and prostrations to the Blessed One and spoke these words. If you do not wish to give the mantra of the nāgas that is magically emanated with great sound, then out of great kindness, please explain it. The Blessed One spoke: Goddess of great fortune, listen to the characteristics of the great king of mantras.

The fanged Yama herself,
seated upon a tortoise seat,
the very last of the central ones,
and again dwelling in the fourth,
dwelling at the end of Yama's direction,
adorned above with the Excellent One;
again the fanged Yama,
well adorned with a tortoise,
with the syllable oṃ and the word bha.
First the tortoise,
the seated protector, with the outline,
adorned with the crest, to be repeated twice.
The protector is adorned with the word phan phan.

kākimukhi mukakākesi nagṇi induabhagagi ketisajji pihivadihi aparioso kahakahijjai kasmadathaṃ jaiindabhamibiduthumahesarovacakkebi sarbbalapahaṇḍobihobiṇasanatahanasei kakihaaasantia

Thus spoke the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder, Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata, Vajraḍāka, supreme in bliss, who is united with all the ḍākas.

The one who pierces is the lord of the earth,
adorned with a fragment of the moon.
Again take the central of the center.
Of the central one in the northern direction,
the lotus feet are possessed of the sun.
Nairṛti is adorned with a spot,
bathed in blazing piercing.
The first of the northern direction,
and the one dwelling above the supreme bull,
dwelling at the end of Yama's direction,
and the wind, adorned with fire in the female.
The fanged Yama who dwells in Nairṛti
is adorned with the ornament of delusion.
Yama the Destroyer is bathed in delusion.
By this nāga mudrā
all the ḍākinīs will be pleased and delighted.
These sixteen great magical displays
all abide in each single letter.

Thus spoke the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder, Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata, the ḍākinī, supreme in bliss, who is united with all the ḍākas.

First take the tortoise,
dwelling above the Excellent One,
possessed of the first and second of the north.
Yama the messenger sits as Varuṇa.
The syllable ra is adorned with delusion.
Again it is spoken as ka śa.
The fanged Yama is adorned with the Excellent One.
Again the fanged one dwells in Nairṛti,
together with delusion.
There it is called "the end of the earth,"
adorned together with delusion.
By this teaching, goddess,
the supreme means of summoning girls,
taking eight from the eastern direction,
the end of the middle, possessed of the fourth,
the crown adorned with the female earth-mark.
Again take the last of the middle.
It is to be joined with piercing.
Having taken Yama the Destroyer,
make the earrings with the female earth-mark.
At the end it is fully adorned with svāhā.
Please it with the Veda.
Placed in the center of a skull cup, one becomes victorious.

mu oṃ ṇi ka kakā saṃbi ika mukaṇa rupate sakami kamubini aṇṭoha palaha larigiṇile ahi mupakate ssra iñji abi mutaṃ kigrasya hāmukahacchagā thakibayanhathṭamisusvākhahā phaṭ

By this nāga mudrā
one will bind and drive away.

oṃ indeṇahadabhaajamenau badiajihahādābāha dandenatajjiyasulaharenasuliyasvāhā oṃ jaṃbhani jaṃbhehi oṃ stambhani stambhahi oṃ mohani mohehi oṃ ākarṣani ākarṣehi oṃ thambhathambhamahathambha amukaṃ thambhaya svāhā

Thus it was taught according to the rite of the yogins.

oṃ namo bhagavani camundhesukhamukhiṣukakaṇthegaḍodari amukasyamukhaṃ bandhabandha hana hana daha daha paca paca hūṃ phaṭ svāhā

Thus spoke the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder, Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata, Vajraḍāka, supreme in bliss, who is united with all the ḍākas.

Take the skin from the sole of the foot of a hero who looked toward the direction of desire, along with manada, nyanpa, collyrium, and red stone. Grind these with human blood, and again grind them many times with red sandalwood. Form this into a pill like an āmra fruit. Insert a hair in its center and dry it in the shade. Place the pill in the midst of the sun, the moon, and a fire. Again have it rest especially under the constellation Puṣya. Place it in the mouth of the King of Knowledge Mantras, and recite until it becomes invisible. Obtain a king of snakes, separate the white and black parts of a striped one into pieces, and make the white and the black known separately. With this, and with conch, flower, and black aloeswood, recite the mantra twenty-four thousand times less one; afterward recite it one hundred and eight times more and make a fumigation. On the third day of Puṣya make a woolen cloth. In the waxing half of the month, fumigate it with the white pieces. In the waning half, fumigate it with the black dumabu. Then, if one recites ten thousand times with the body clothed in it, one will succeed. By wearing it separately for the white and the black halves, one becomes invisible.

One will go wherever one wishes to go, and will enjoy desire with whomever one desires. Insert antimony into the opening of its mouth and burn it without smoke. When it has burned, grind it finely at night. If one applies it to the eyes, one becomes clear like the finest flower of the house, and if one recites the mantra of one's own deity, one becomes invisible.

Put a king of mahoragas into a pot or a bowl and hide it away like a treasure. Then, when it has rotted and liquefied, take it out, grind the bone of its skull, and make a forehead mark with it; then even one's shadow disappears. If one smears the body with the earth on which it dripped, it pacifies poisons of every kind.

She whose menstrual blood always smells sweet, whose hair curls to the right, who is lovely, possessed of the seven shadows, whose body has no wound, who speaks the truth, and who does not blink for a long time, she is a goddess who grants accomplishments. Further, she whose breath is fragrant and who always dwells in happiness, like the bodhisattvas ever giving great gifts, abides in the form of a corpse in order to protect practitioners. To bless sentient beings, like the form of the buddha Śākyamuni passing into nirvāṇa, she remains close at hand to benefit, and is one laid out before one.

Because that goddess has become the source of all the qualities of accomplishment, she abides surrounded by all the flesh-eaters, such as jackals, crows and vultures. And also, in the manner of Indra, that form is surrounded on every side by yakṣas, rākṣasas, gods, pretas and others. The practitioner, deeply settled in meditative equipoise, having understood the rite in this way, should take the rocana that is at her heart and, reciting his own heart mantra, make a forehead mark with it. Then he will go upward as far as ten thousand million yojanas and come back again, will see various miracles and magical displays, will play joyfully with the ḍākinīs, and will attain all yoga. Merely by eating her flesh, one comes to possess the wisdom of the three realms, travels a hundred yojanas with the speed of the wind, gains a divine body, and grants whatever anyone desires. Make a wick of corpse cloth in the fat of a garuḍa, a cat and an owl, and light a lamp: one will then see all treasures, and will see all the piśācas and bhūtas that are hidden.

Thus spoke, united with all the ḍākinīs,
Vajraḍāka, supreme bliss,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
The Blessed One, the Vajra Holder.

From the Great King of Tantras, the Glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the sixth chapter, setting out the stages of application of all the nāga mantras.

The characteristics of the hand mudrās
I shall next explain correctly.
To one who touches the garland at the crown,
One should show the head.
To one who points to the forehead,
One should show the cheek.
To one who shows the teeth,
One should show the tongue.
To one who touches the lip,
One should show the throat.
To one who points to the belly,
One should show the navel.
To one who points to the buttocks,
One should show the ground.
To one who points to the vulva,
One should show the phallus.
To one who points to the knee,
One should show the calf.
To one who shows the hand,
One should show the upper arm.
To one who shows the foot,
One should show the sole.
To one who shows a finger,
One should show the nail.
To one who points to the sky,
One should show the sun.
To one who points to a river,
One should show the ocean.
To one who shows a single finger,
One should show two fingers.

Next, the signs for reviving the dead,
For increasing the living, free of illness,
For being seized by Hāritī and the others,
And for setting free,
I shall explain in brief;
O Goddess, listen to it.
To one who looks upward,
One should show the forehead.
To one who shows the eyes,
One should show the teeth.
To one who shows the navel,
One should show the lips.
To one who shows the lips,
One should show the fangs.
To one who shows the tongue,
One should show the belly.
To one who shows the calf,
One should show the thigh.
To one who shows the thigh,
One should show the foot.
To one who shows the foot,
One should show the nails.
To one who shows the ankle,
One should show the teeth.
To one who laughs "ha ha,"
One should show the place of blood.
To one who shows a spear,
One should show a śakti dart.
To one who shows play,
One should show a glance.
To one who points to a direction,
One should show an intermediate direction.
To one who shows urine,
One should show excrement.
To one who shows a change of face,
One should look on with a peaceful gaze.

Then, at the cheeks and the middle of the forehead,
A spear, and at the nose a śakti;
Brows knit, she looks back;
Breasts high and navel wide;
Her lower body is altered;
Three lines, perfectly rounded:
These are the marks, one should know,
Of a ḍākinī drawing near.
She does not turn back from ground she has covered;
She weeps without cause,
And likewise laughs for no reason;
She speaks gently, yet has no compassion;
She talks much to no purpose.
United with Mahāmāyā,
The ḍākinī is the supreme blazing one;
One endowed with these marks
Should be known as the supreme ḍākinī.
Thus spoke Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
The Blessed One, the Vajra Holder.

From the Great King of Tantras, the Glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the seventh chapter, setting out the application of the hand mudrās, the signs made with the teeth, and so forth.

Next, of the goddesses of mantra knowledge
I shall explain the characteristics.
Whoever shows the left hand
Is making a prostration.
Whoever shows the ring finger
Is giving the reply to the prostration.
Whoever pats the belly
Is indicating, "I am hungry."
Whoever points finger to finger
Is indicating, "Let us eat."
Whoever touches the calf
Is indicating, "I am weary."
Whoever touches a finger
Is indicating, "I have rested."

Whoever grinds tooth against tooth
is indicating "eating flesh."
Whoever touches the frowning brow
is indicating "I am bound."
Whoever touches the garuḍa
is indicating "I am freed."
Whoever shows hand to hand
is indicating "Today, eat the torma."
Whoever shows the right hand
is indicating "Do it thus."
Whoever taps upon the ear
is indicating "Stay."

Then the rite of verbal signs is explained.

potaṃ means a salutation. pratipotaṃ means returning a salutation. gamuṃ means going. lumpa means "come." nigaraṃ means "give!" cacukaṃ means "take!" hridaya means a hero. kuraba means dead. karṇika means a bell. alikara means the head. baraha means hair. śrabano means the ear. mantha means nectar. nara means going together. talika means a ḍākinī. narakaṃ means a maṇḍala. amukaṃ means a charnel ground. kakhila means a door. svasa means a brahmin. pariti means the warrior caste (kṣatriya). biradi means the merchant caste (vaiśya). krura means the servant caste (śūdra). antha means a butcher. alika means livestock. daki means a sister. mutaka means a nāginī.

Touching the tongue to the teeth indicates being hungry. trisna means anointed with scent. akaṃnaṃ means "from where?" sthanad means "from such-and-such a place." kirino means a flower. lambodaradanda means laughter. nirod means making rain fall. bignapati means satisfied. dhumapriyana means a cloud. sanu means a mountain. saroda means a river. aṅgula means a portion. "Self" means the face. rajika means the tongue. adana means a tooth. paṃkati means a banner. chando means a garland. cala means wind. mrigapati means livestock. mandala means equal. svasa means a crossroads. phalguśam means birth. mahākṣar means great livestock. cha means a horse. na means a man. ba means an ox. ma means a buffalo. ba means food. apyakā means a king's man. sthā means staying. Touching the mouth means "eaten." Touching the teeth means "satisfied." hrika means shame. Touching the empty place means "perform the act of union." A moist touch means "do it thus."

Thus spoke Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder,
Vajraḍāka, the supreme of bliss,
joined in union with all the ḍākinīs.

This is the eighth chapter, the analysis of the stages of the signs of all the ḍākinīs, from the great king of tantras, the glorious Vajraḍāka.

Then all the goddesses gathered together and made a request to the Blessed One, the great lord of vajra yoga, without beginning or end: "Blessed One, please bring forth the chapter called the yantra that subdues all who are hostile, the great secret of all the tathāgatas."

Then the Blessed One spoke the yantra called Jewel Union, the supreme and marvelous great secret of all the tathāgatas:

What is to be stated in summary,
that I shall explain; listen!
Kulika and the Horse's Gait,
Yama and the Pretas likewise,
and further the Vetāla Pair,
with the distinctions of the rākṣasas,
then the Sequence and the Outer Sequence,
the Vanquisher, the Door, the Empty,
and the one with vajras at the corners' ends:
these twelve great yantras
are distinguished by garlands of mantra.
They are stated in this order.

A square, perfectly proportioned,
having forty-nine cells,
the mantra practitioner should lay out evenly.
In the first two rows
place a and the rest, along with the remainder.
In the remaining cells,
all the letters, summarized,
beginning with ka,
should be placed as fitting, in their order.
In the empty central cell
one should write the pa
that abides among the letters called ka ṣa.
Encircle the wheel with two lines,
and between the lines the yogin
should place, in summary, Increase and the rest.
On the outer eight-petaled lotus,
in Indra's direction ka, in Kubera's ca,
in Varuṇa's and Yama's ṭa and ta;
in wind, fire, Mahendra and water,
place likewise ya ra la va.
These are called the great elements, the constituents.
Again, on the outer square,
wind and fire and the taste of nectar,
the Destroyer of Yama and the rest:
place what is taught as śa ṣa sa ha.
The four called ṛ ṝ ḷ ḹ,
taught as those called neuter,
should be placed at the four doors.
By such application of the rite,
then place the king of mantras.

Endowed with Piercing, Dream and Moon,
the first cell is to be adorned.
In the second, take the outer of Nairṛti
and place it joined with the Tortoise.
Abiding in the third cell,
take the last of the middle;
it abides as its fourth.
In the fourth cell, the southern end,
adorned on the crown by Excellent;
having taken the outer of Nairṛti,
join it with the Tortoise.
In the fifth cell place it properly:
having taken the last of the middle,
it abides as its fourth.
The sixth cell is to be adorned.
Having taken the outer of the wind quarter,
with Tortoise, half moon and dot,
it is to be placed in the seventh cell.
Wind abides in the eighth cell,
seated on the seat of the first neuter.
Place the third of the first in the ninth.
Seated on the seat of the twentieth,
having taken the outer of wind,
place it, adorned with Delusion, in the tenth.
The twenty-first itself
is to be placed in the eleventh cell.
The twelfth is to be adorned with wind.
Seated on the seat of the Tortoise,
the outer of wind, adorned with a crescent
and a dot, place in the thirteenth.
The fourteenth too is to be adorned with that.
With the first of the middle
the fifteenth cell is held to be adorned.
Adorned with Delusion,
the middle of the middle goes in the sixteenth.
Seated on the seat of the middle of the middle,
adorned on the head by Excellent,
place the southern end in the seventeenth.
In the southern middle, giving the seat of wind,
adorn it with Delusion
and place it in the eighteenth cell.
Abiding in the nineteenth cell
is fire, adorned with a;
it is joined with the Tortoise.
The outer of wind, adorned with a crescent
and a dot, place in the twentieth.
The second of the middle, supreme in benefit,
place in the twenty-first cell.
Seated on the seat of the southern end,
having taken the outer of wind,
joined with Delusion, is the twenty-second.
With the third of the first
the twenty-third cell is adorned.

The twenty-fourth cell
Should be joined with the west.
The twenty-sixth cell
Is held to have the northern middle.
The twenty-seventh cell
Is adorned with the second of the middle.
The twenty-eighth cell
Is made beautiful by the west.
Taking the first middle,
Place the one called ri below,
And set it in the twenty-ninth cell.
With the fourth of the middle
Adorn the thirtieth cell.
With the second of that same
Adorn the thirty-first cell.
Taking the outer of the wind,
Joined with the tortoise and the drop,
Set it in the thirty-second cell.
The thirty-third cell, too,
Is held to be adorned with that same.
The thirty-fourth cell
Should be made beautiful by the west.
o, joined with the drop,
Set in the thirty-fifth cell.
For the thirty-sixth cell, too,
That same lord of letters is held.
Taking the outer of the wind,
Joined with the awl, the thirty-seventh.
What dwells on the petal of the wind
Set in the thirty-eighth cell.
The thirty-ninth cell
Should be adorned with the southern end.
The fortieth cell
Should be adorned with increase.

Is adorned with awl, dream and drop.
Taking the middle of the eastern petal,

And set it in the forty-second cell.
The forty-third cell
Has the first of the west.
The forty-fourth cell, too,
Is adorned with the second of the middle.
Taking the outer of the wind,
Joined with the tortoise and the drop,
Set it in the forty-fifth cell.
Again taking the outer of the wind,
Joined with tortoise and drop,
Set it in the forty-sixth as well.
Taking the outer of the wind,
Join it with the southern end;
It stands in the forty-seventh cell.
The third of the eastern petal,
Seated upon the outer of the east,

This application of the rite
For binding all enemies,
And especially for bewildering,
And for liberating,
I have explained to you, O lovely-faced one.

Again, in the first cell
Those adorned with awl, dream and moon
Should be set.
Taking the outer of Nairṛti,
Seated on the seat of the tortoise,
Place it in the second cell.
Taking the last of the middle,
The fourth of that same stands there;
Set it in the third cell.
Taking the outer of the wind,
Adorned with makara, dream and moon,
Set it in the fourth cell.
Taking the outer of the wind
With tortoise, dream and offering,
Set it in the fifth cell.
What stands in the sixth cell
Is held to be the second of the middle
And the first of the west.
Joined with awl, dream and drop,
Set it in the seventh cell.
Taking the outer of the wind,
Adorned with tortoise and dream,
Is what stands in the eighth cell.
What stands in the ninth cell
Is the second of the middle,
And likewise the first of the west.
Taking the last of the south,
Adorned on the head with Excellent,
It stands in the tenth cell.
In the eleventh, that same letter.
Taking the outer of the wind,
Join it with tortoise and dream;
Write it in the twelfth cell.
What stands in the thirteenth cell
Is the second of the middle,
And likewise give the west.
Taking the third of the east,
Standing on the seat of the eastern neuter,
