It is held for the fourteenth cell.
Taking the outer of the wind,
Adorned with tortoise, dream and moon,
It stands in the fifteenth cell.
Standing on the seat of the fourth of the middle,
Taking the end of that same,
Write it in the sixteenth cell.
Standing on the seat of the tortoise,
Taking the outer of Nairṛti,
Place it in the seventeenth cell.
Riding a makara,
The outer of the wind adorned with the drop,
Write it in the eighteenth cell.
Seated on the seat of the southern end,
Taking the outer of the wind,
It should be written in the nineteenth.
What stands in the twentieth cell:
Take the third of the first,
Which stands on the seat of the mountain.
Seated on the seat of the southern end,
Taking the outer of the wind,
Write it in the twenty-first cell.
To stand in the twenty-second cell:
Riding a makara,
The outer of the wind adorned with the drop.
In the twenty-third, too, that same.
To stand in the twenty-fourth cell:
Adorned with makara, drop and moon,
Again take the outer of the wind.
The twenty-fifth according to the rite.
What stands in the twenty-sixth:
With makara and drop
Adorning it above, the outer of the wind.
The twenty-seventh, too, is said to be that.
To stand in the twenty-eighth,
Having makara, dream and moon,
The outer of the wind should be taken.
To stand in the twenty-ninth,
Taking the outer of the wind,
Write it joined together with the awl.
Taking the fourth of the middle,
There give also the third of the first;
The wise one adorns the thirtieth cell.
Taking the middle of the middle,
Made beautiful by delusion,
Place it in the thirty-first.
Riding a makara, with dream,
The outer of the wind adorned with the moon,
Set it in the thirty-second.
Taking the third of the east,
Give it the one called ṛ;
It stands in the thirty-third cell.
Taking the outer of the wind,
Seated on the seat of the southern end, adorned with delusion,
Set it in the thirty-fourth.
To stand in the thirty-fifth cell,
Taking the first of the middle,
Set the wind there too.
Increase and the southern end
And the wind should also be given there,
Adorning the thirty-sixth cell.
Standing in the thirty-seventh cell,
The second of the middle
Should be made beautiful by the west.
In the thirty-eighth cell,
Taking the southern end,
With the seat of the middle of the middle,
Adorn its crown with what was taken.
Again take the middle of the south,
Joined with delusion,
And place it below the wind.
In the thirty-ninth, too, that same.
Standing in the fortieth cell,
Taking the first of the middle,
Set the wind there too.
To stand in the forty-first,
Write the second of the middle
And the first of the west.
To stand in the forty-second,
Taking the outer of the wind,
With tortoise and dream, sun and moon.
To stand in the forty-third,
The awl adorned with dream and moon.
To stand in the forty-fourth,

Taking the outer of the wind,
the tortoise with dream, sun and moon.
In the forty-fifth,
write the one called ra,
joined with the middle of the north.
In the forty-sixth,
taking the outer of the wind,
the makara-rider with dream.
In the forty-seventh as well,
having taken the outer of the wind,
the southern end as seat, adorned with delusion.
In the forty-eighth square,
joined with the mountain,
it is to be adorned with the middle of the first.
In the forty-ninth as well,
the awl, adorned with dream and moon.
This is supreme for one endowed with yoga.
Stupefaction and likewise downfall:
when urged on by the king of mantras,
it accomplishes all rites.
Stupefying and harming
I explain to you, Goddess.
The stainless makara-rider,
perfectly joined with dream and moon,
resting upon a vajra,
adorned with a garland of flames,
radiating in all directions:
having meditated thus, in its center
place the target's name just as it occurs.
The mantra practitioner, setting that aside,
should put it in the place of phaṭ.
In the center of those squares
the target who is to become intoxicated
the practitioner should have written.
One should crush him with the wisdom vajra,
and imagine his shadow, too, destroyed.
In a hero's freshly made vase,
or in a brahmin's dry vase,
having placed the two wheels inside,
drawn according to the rite,
give torma to all the bhūtas.
When the practitioner has done this
and afterward undertakes the rite,
in accord with the command of the vajra mind
he will accomplish all rites.

Again, in the first square,
the awl, adorned with dream and moon.
In the second square,
one should take the southern end,
adorned on the crown with Excellent.
In the third square,
taking the outer of the wind,
adorn it with tortoise, dream and moon.
In the fifth square,
again the awl, adorned with the moon.
In the sixth square,
take the third of the east,
resting on the mountain's seat.
In the seventh square,
take the outer of the wind,
adorned with tortoise, dream and moon.
In the eighth square,
take the last of that same group,
resting on the seat of the fourth of the middle.
In the ninth square,
having taken the second of the middle,
place the first of the west.
In the tenth square,
one should take the outer of Nairṛti,
resting on the seat of the tortoise.
In the twelfth,
take the outer of the wind,
adorned with tortoise, dream and moon.
In the thirteenth as well,
having taken the second of the middle,
place the first of the western direction.
In the fourteenth,
take the third of the east,
resting on the mountain's seat.
In the fifteenth,
take the end of that same group,
resting on the seat of the fourth of the middle.
In the sixteenth as well,
take the outer of the wind,
joined with tortoise, dream and moon.
In the seventeenth,
take the outer of Nairṛti,
resting on the tortoise's seat.
In the nineteenth, one should place,
resting on the seat of the southern end,
the outer of the wind, taken as determined.
In the twentieth,
having taken the second of the middle,
place the first of the west as well.
Resting on the seat of the southern end,
having taken the outer of the wind,
write it in the twenty-first square.
The awl adorned with dream and moon,
write in the twenty-ninth square.
Having taken the fourth of the middle,
place the third of the east there,
and adorn the thirtieth square.
In the thirty-first,
take the outer of the wind,
adorned with tortoise, dream and moon.
In the thirty-third,
adorn with the awl, dream and moon.
In the thirty-fourth as well,
having taken the first of the middle,
place the wind there too.
The thirty-fifth likewise
is held to be adorned with that same.
In the thirty-sixth,
fire is to be joined with a;
having taken the middle of the north,
one should write it right there.
What is set in the thirty-seventh:
having taken the second of the middle,
place there the first of the south.
In the thirty-eighth as well,
one should write the one called a,
and take the southern end,
and write the wind there too.
In the fortieth,
having taken the outer of the wind,
with the southern end placed below,
adorn it with delusion.
In the forty-second square,
having taken the third of the first,
place there the first of the south.
In the forty-second square,
having taken the third of the east,
place the one called mountain below.
In the forty-third square,
having taken the outer of the wind,
adorn it with the awl.
In the forty-fourth,
having taken the outer of the wind,
join it with tortoise and dream.
In the forty-fifth,
adorned with delusion,
take the middle of the middle.
Having taken the southern end,
make the middle of the middle its seat,
and adorn its head with Excellent.
Take the middle of the south as well,
and, joined with delusion,
make the wind its seat.
The learned should set it right there.
In the forty-seventh square,
adorned with delusion,
seated upon the southern end,
take the outer of the wind.
In the forty-eighth square,
having taken the outer of the wind
joined with tortoise, dream and moon,
write it twice.
In the forty-ninth square,
by taking the third of the first,
joined with the one called mountain,
it is held to be most beautiful and beneficial.
By this, O Goddess, they grow hostile,
and above all they wage battle.
A horse and a buffalo, in a vase,
should be placed according to the rite.
Adorned with the awl, dream and moon,
again take the outer of Nairṛti,
together with the tortoise,
seated on the seat of the fourth of the middle;
again taking its end,
it is held to adorn the first square.
Take the southern end,
adorned on the crown with the awl;
then take the outer of Nairṛti,
joined with the tortoise,
seated on the seat of the fourth of the middle;
again taking its end,
adorn the third square.
Having taken the second of the middle,
again beautified by the western direction,
it is held to adorn the fifth square:
the awl, adorned with dream and moon.

Taking the third of the first,
Add the neuter of the east beneath it.
Taking what is outside Wind,
Make the last of the south its seat.
With these the seventh is to be adorned.
Take the second of the middle,
And add the western direction to it;
Richly adorn the fifteenth.
Taking what is outside Wind,
Adorned with Tortoise, Dream,
And Moon, twice over,
Beautify the seventeenth.
Taking what is outside Wind,
Adorned with Tortoise, Dream and Moon,
And repeated twice,
Place it in the nineteenth square.
Taking the middle of the east,
Seated on the seat called Mountain,
And taking what is outside Wind,
Seated on the seat of the last of the south,
Beautify the twenty-first.
Taking the fourth of the middle,
The middle of the eastern direction
And the middle of the middle
Are to be beautified with the one called a.
Taking the last of the south,
Take the middle of the middle,
And adorn its head with Excellent.
Again take the middle of the south,
And add Wind beneath it,
Beautified with Delusion;
And extract Fire joined with a.
Taking the middle of the north,
Write these in the twenty-ninth.
Taking what is outside Wind,
With Tortoise, Dream and Moon,
Write it doubled in the thirty-first.
Again write what is outside Wind,
With Tortoise, Dream and Moon,
Doubled in the thirty-third.
Taking the second of the middle,
Again with the first of the west,
The thirty-fifth is to be adorned.
With Piercer, Dream and Moon,
And Increase too should be added there.
Taking the last of the south,
And again likewise Wind
And what is outside Wind,
Adorned with Piercer,
These are to be written in the fourteenth.
Taking the second of the middle,
Adorned with the first of the west,
Beautify the thirty-fifth.
The third of the first,
Resting on the seat called Mountain,
And what is outside Wind, taken
Resting on the seat of the last of the south,
Are to be beautified with Delusion.
Taking the first of the middle,
And adding Wind there as well,
Adorn the forty-seventh.
With Piercer, Dream and Moon,
Taking the third of the east,
Add the one called Mountain.
Then take what is outside Wind,
Resting on the seat of the last of the south,
And beautify it with Delusion.
Taking the first of the middle,
Likewise together with Wind,
To adorn the forty-ninth square
Is supremely beneficial.

This wheel, great goddess,
Is supremely sharp, and it destroys.
On the tenth day of the waning fortnight,
Or likewise that same day of the waxing,
Or else on the night of the bhūtas,
Seated on a seat of skulls,
Facing toward the south,
Make ink from poison, blood,
And the seed of Caṇḍa.
Pour it into a skull cup held in midair.
With a pen of human bone,
Write out the terrifying mantras of the Terrifier.
First give a torma of great flesh,
And then begin the rite.
Wear clothing that is especially
Red or else blue.
The worship, the vajra rite,
And likewise meditation and recitation,
The mantrin should always perform
In such a way that no one at all perceives it.
For killing, the King of Death (Yama);
For pretas, the form of a preta;
For vetālas, a vetāla;
And rākṣasas are to be subdued by a rākṣasa.
For the empty, the empty Padma;
In the corners of certain destruction,
A garland of mantras, likewise
Recited in the order of a garland.
This is called Victor over the Three Realms.
Adorned with these words of mantra,
This mantra of the universal monarch among kings of knowledge
Has never arisen before, nor will it arise again.
The twelve great yantras
I have explained to you, fair-faced one.
This is the unbearable terror
Of all enemies.

Next is explained the rite of the sādhana
Of food and the gathered feast,
The true ritual by which
Accomplishments swiftly come.
The summoning of the beasts
The practitioner should perform by the method taught before.
Without a cause, the beasts
Are to be destroyed without a reason.
The beasts, and likewise the five:
The flesh of donkey, human and tortoise,
Camel, jackal, horse and so on,
Of dog and of pig,
And likewise of the rooster:
If food, fire offering and torma
Are made of these without discrimination,
Everything without exception is accomplished,
And all kingship is fully accomplished.

Thus spoke Vajraḍāka, the supreme bliss,
In union with all the ḍākas,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
The Blessed One (the Buddha), the Vajra Holder.

This is the ninth chapter, called The Arising of All Yantras, from the great king of tantras, the glorious Vajraḍāka.

Increasing life and freedom from illness,
The supreme application of what to do and what not to do,
The characteristics of the workings of the breath and so forth:
Listen, goddess of great fortune!
The breaths of sixty water-hours,
Counted as twenty thousand, one thousand,
And six hundred,
Are known as the year of a day and night.
Of the breaths going out and coming in,
One should count by the sign of the outgoing.
The intelligent one, of the two breaths,
Should dissolve the incoming into the outgoing.
In the twelve places as well
The outer signs should be set.
Just as the distinctions of the lunar days were taught,
So the distinctions of the vowels should be known.
From the distinctions of the planets
And the movements of the stars,
The poison of poison and so forth, and the others,
Should be stated in this order.
Beginning from the first month of spring,
The time when the sun travels north,
The twelve months
Are to be set out in their proper order.
Starting from the eastern direction,
The wise should reckon in the morning.
The third and the thirteenth lunar days,
The fourth and likewise the fourteenth,
The fifth and the fifteenth
Should be joined as equivalent.
Now listen, and I shall explain it properly.

It is as follows. The first is Increase. The second is Delusion. The third is Excellent. The fourth is Bull. The fifth is Tortoise. The sixth is Sea Monster. The seventh is Widow. The eighth is Mole. The ninth is Piercer. The tenth is Wandering. The eleventh is Dream. The twelfth is Lord of the Earth. The first is Lord of the Earth. The second is Increase. The third is Delusion. The fourth is Excellent. The fifth is Bull. The sixth is Tortoise.

The seventh is Makara. The eighth is Widow. The ninth is Birthmark. The tenth is Piercing. The eleventh is Wandering. The twelfth is Dream.

The first is Dream. The second is Lord of the Earth. The third is Increase. The fourth is Delusion. The fifth is Excellent. The sixth is Bull. The seventh is Tortoise. The eighth is Makara. The ninth is Widow. The tenth is Birthmark. The eleventh is Piercing. The twelfth is Wandering.

The first is Wandering. The second is Dream. The third is Lord of the Earth. The fourth is Increase. The fifth is Delusion. The sixth is Excellent. The seventh is Bull. The eighth is Tortoise. The ninth is Makara. The tenth is Widow. The eleventh is Birthmark. The twelfth is Piercing.

The first is Piercing. The second is Wandering. The third is Dream. The fourth is Lord of the Earth. The fifth is Increase. The sixth is Delusion. The seventh is Excellent. The eighth is Bull. The ninth is Tortoise. The tenth is Makara. The eleventh is Widow. The twelfth is Birthmark.

The first is Birthmark. The second is Piercing. The third is Wandering. The fourth is Dream. The fifth is Lord of the Earth. The sixth is Increase. The seventh is Delusion. The eighth is Excellent. The ninth is Bull. The tenth is Tortoise. The eleventh is Makara. The twelfth is Widow.

The first is Widow. The second is Birthmark. The third is Piercing. The fourth is Wandering. The fifth is Dream. The sixth is Lord of the Earth. The seventh is Increase. The eighth is Delusion. The ninth is Excellent. The tenth is Bull. The eleventh is Tortoise. The twelfth is Makara.

The first is Makara. The second is Widow. The third is Birthmark. The fourth is Piercing. The fifth is Wandering. The sixth is Dream. The seventh is Lord of the Earth. The eighth is Increase. The ninth is Delusion. The tenth is Excellent. The eleventh is Bull. The twelfth is Tortoise.

The first is Tortoise. The second is Makara. The third is Widow. The fourth is Birthmark. The fifth is Piercing. The sixth is Wandering. The seventh is Dream. The eighth is Lord of the Earth. The ninth is Increase. The tenth is Delusion. The eleventh is Excellent. The twelfth is Bull.

The first is Bull. The second is Tortoise. The third is Makara. The fourth is Widow. The fifth is Birthmark. The sixth is Piercing. The seventh is Wandering. The eighth is Dream. The ninth is Lord of the Earth. The tenth is Increase. The eleventh is Delusion. The twelfth is Excellent.

The first is Excellent. The second is Bull. The third is Tortoise. The fourth is Makara. The fifth is Widow. The sixth is Birthmark. The seventh is Piercing. The eighth is Wandering. The ninth is Dream. The tenth is Lord of the Earth. The eleventh is Increase. The twelfth is Delusion.

The first is Delusion. The second is Excellent. The third is Bull. The fourth is Tortoise. The fifth is Makara. The sixth is Widow. The seventh is Birthmark. The eighth is Piercing. The ninth is Wandering. The tenth is Dream. The eleventh is Lord of the Earth. The twelfth is Increase.

A child born on the day of Increase, if it does not die on the ninth day, in the ninth month, or in the ninth year, will be wealthy and fortunate. Its mind will be malicious and harsh, its digestive heat weak, and it will become a chief of a borderland. It will delight in lust, speak gently in all things, possess wealth, be free of illness, and always be happy. It will live eighty-one years and will die on the day of Lord of the Earth.

A child born on the day of Lord of the Earth, if it does not die on the eighth day, in the eighth month, or in the eighth year, will always have wealth and grain. It will speak the truth, have many friends, know the Dharma, be learned, live in celibacy, and benefit all sentient beings. It will know crafts, speak gently, and be fortunate, and there is no doubt that it will become versed in all the treatises. It will live eighty-eight years and will die on the day of Dream.

A child born on the day of Dream, if it does not die on the fourth, fifth, or tenth day, or else in the eighth month or the eighth year, will torment its teachers. Brave and bold, it will overpower others and conquer all its enemies. It will be friendly with holy people and be an ally to those without protection. It will speak harshly in all things and be diligent in evil deeds. It will be poor and arrogant, free of illness, and will always find food. It will live by relying on people of high rank and will always think like a lion. It will live sixty years, and there is no doubt that on the day of Wandering it will die by an enemy's weapon.

A child born on the day of Wandering, if it does not die in the fourth, sixth, ninth, or tenth month, will have little wisdom and weak intelligence and digestive heat. It will have much hatred, its children will not survive, and it will have a large retinue. Its possessions will increase, yet it will have much suffering. All its relatives will be fond of it. It will hold the base in esteem, have much wealth and livestock, and take three wives. It will grow angry at the slightest thing and be very proud; its enemies will fear it, and it will live by trade. It will live eighty-one years and will die on the day of Piercing.

A child born on the day of Piercing, if it does not die on the second day, in the second or fourth month, or in the eighth or tenth year, will cause its father and mother to go hungry. It will have little wealth, be dull, harbor evil intentions, be of bad character, and be sickly. It will live in celibacy, be brave and handsome, and be miserly. It will live sixty-four years and will die in its own house on the day of Birthmark.

A child born on the Mole day, if it does not die on the second or the fifth day, or in the third or the ninth month, or in the ninth year, will praise its own lineage and know the Dharma. It will be accomplished in the treatises and skilled in many crafts, heroic and very proud. It will be deceitful and envious, with little wealth, and it will be good-looking and quick in all its tasks. It will live happily for sixty-three years, and on the Widow day it will without doubt die in water.

A child born on the Widow day, if it does not die on the second or the tenth day, or in the second or the eighth month, or in the eighth year, will have a sinful mind and speak idle talk to all beings. It will have little wealth and be boastful, and will be struck by illness everywhere. It will be cut down by the king's punishment, will be fortunate with all women, and will be skilled in crafts and in every art of gaining wealth. It will live for fifty-five years, or else thirty-six, and on the Makara day it will without doubt die.

A child born on the Makara day, if it does not die on the tenth day, or in the third or the fifth month, or in the ninth year, will be endowed with merit and perfect in moral discipline, gentle and disciplined, and possessed of training. It will be accomplished in wealth, form and fortune, and its glory and fame, its sons and grandsons, will increase. It will become the chief of a city, perfect in retinue and enjoyments, skilled in crafts and in the reckoning of time, and it will destroy all its enemies. It will live for fifty-five years, or else sixty-four, and will die on the Tortoise day.

A child born on the Tortoise day, if it does not die on the second or the ninth day, or in the eighth or the ninth month, or in the ninth year, will always be full of worry, prone to anger, and content with little. It will talk a great deal and love its relatives, be always lustful, and be very generous with livestock. It will kill its son and wife, be small-minded and ill-intentioned, live by farming, and be struck by leprosy. It will live for sixty-four years, or a full eighty, and will die in its own land on the Bull day.

A child born on the Bull day, if it does not die on the ninth day, or in the second or the eighth month, or in the eighth or the ninth year, will be brave and bold and will overpower others. It will be charming and rich and take many wives, and all its relatives will be glad. It will destroy its father's life. It will delight in profit and in lust, be lazy, talk nonsense, have a bad character, and delight in cruel deeds. It will live for sixty years, or twenty, and will die by a weapon on the Excellent day.

A child born on the Excellent day, if it does not die on the fifth day, or in the ninth month, or in the tenth year, will be poor but will practice the Dharma. It will speak the truth and speak pleasantly, delight in a single wife, and become a master of knowledge. It will know the crafts and all the times, love its own relatives, and live by expounding its family's learning. It will live for a hundred years, and on the Delusion day it will die in water, and not otherwise.

A child born on the Delusion day, if it does not die on the second day, or in the ninth month, or in the ninth year, will eat a great deal, be small-minded, and always delight in lust. It will be constantly angry and very greedy, and will become sickly. It will be upright of mind and love its relatives, yet always be angry and proud. It will have a bad character and sinful conduct, delight in harsh words and idle talk, and delight in striking with weapons. It will live for sixty-four years, and on the Increase day it will without doubt die of stabbing pains in the limbs and of swellings.

If one is struck by illness on the Increase day, one must be very careful. If one does not die within fifteen days, then within five days more one will recover. For the Lord of the Earth day, if one does not die within three days, then within seven days one will recover. For the Dream day too, if one does not die within three days, likewise within seven days one will recover. For the Wavering day, within three or five days life and death are evenly balanced. For the Piercing day, if one does not die on the third or the fourth day, within ten days one will recover. For the Mole day, one dies on the third day or recovers on the hundredth, and will be tormented by illness for a long time. For the Widow day, one dies on the fifth day or rises on the ninth, and within ten days is freed from the illness. For the Makara day, one dies on the eighth day, or else for twenty-one days life and death are evenly balanced. For the Tortoise day, if one does not die on the eighth day, one is freed from the illness on the tenth. For the Bull day, if one does not die on the third or the fifth day, a slight illness remains and one rises on the eighth. For the Excellent day, if one does not die on the eighth or the tenth day, one is then freed. For the Delusion day, if one does not die on the third or the seventh day, one is then freed.

"One who has been seized by the fangs of a serpent
keeps close company with the Lord of Death.
Through the practice of drawing out poison,
how are these things accomplished?
And to increase life and freedom from illness,
how is the rite to be applied for others?"

He declared:
"The illnesses of all sentient beings
are to be destroyed, and beings are to be protected.
If even the dead are brought back to life,
what need is there to speak of others?
The rite of the water fire offering,
through which their merit becomes great,
is to be stated in brief.
I shall explain it; listen!

On a pleasing patch of ground,
smeared with white-colored scent,
adorned with garlands of white flowers,
and made resplendent with banners and canopies,
set a vase with a black base and without streaks,
filled with water, and the rest.
In its center, visualize the goddess
seated perfectly on a lotus seat,
with one face and two arms,
clad in exquisitely beautiful garments,
adorned with every ornament,
and beautified with a garland of lotuses.
Emanate the circle of ḍākinīs.
With a stream of nectar water
white as snow, the kunda flower and the moon,
she steals away the power of the poison.
If one meditates through such a yoga of the goddess,
the poison will be destroyed.

Until the milk has turned black,
long overwhelmed by darkness,
fumigate it once more with incense.

Through the spring months and the rest, in their order,
when the sun travels northward,
the signs should be applied step by step,
in accordance with the time.
The wise reckon the nāḍikās of the day
as four plus two tens,
and those of the night as six plus three tens.
The wise should thus lay out the nāḍikās.
In the second chart,
one skilled in time should make the signs:
for the day, six plus two tens;
make these the signs of the nāḍikās.
Four plus three tens
should be examined as the nāḍikās of the night.
Eight plus two tens
should be examined for the day.
Two plus three tens
should be examined as the nāḍikās of the night.
Exactly thirty nāḍikās
are applied to both at the middle equinox.
Two plus three tens
are to be known as the nāḍikās of the day;
eight plus two tens
are to be made its signs for the night.
The sign of four plus three tens
is the nāḍikās of the day;
six plus two tens
are made the sign of the nāḍikās of the night.
Six plus three tens
are to be known for the day;
four plus two tens
are known as the nāḍikās of the night.

Likewise, I shall explain how to know
the times of the southward course.
Four plus three tens of nāḍikās
are explained as those of the day;
six plus two tens
are to be made the sign of the night.
Two plus three tens,
likewise, are the nāḍikās of the day;
eight plus two tens
are well known as the nāḍikās of the night.
Three tens
are the nāḍikās of the day at the equinox,
and likewise the nāḍikās of the night
are to be reckoned as three tens.
Eight plus two tens
are to be considered for the day;
two plus three tens
should be examined as the nāḍikās of the night.
Four plus two tens
are the sequence of the nāḍikās of the day;
six plus three tens,
likewise, are the nāḍikās of the night.

With three hundred and sixty breaths
the time of one nāḍikā passes.
By the count of sixty nāḍikās
one full day and night is reckoned.
According to the sequence of the months,
make the signs in that same order.
The breaths of the nāḍikās, first complete,
gradually increase and gradually decrease.
According to the distinction between act and non-act,
the breaths are to be taken in order.
They are to be marked by the two sky-goers,
by the seasons, and by the two courses of the sun.
The two multiplied by ten:
likewise are the nāḍikās of the year.
Endowed with their signs,
reckon them as the breaths of day and night.

Taking all the lords of speech,
set them out as the signs of the vowels.
Leaving aside the two neuters,
set them throughout upon the lords of the earth.

If it falls on the stage of a,
one keeps close company with fortune
and is seen to gain wealth;
there is no doubt one is very fortunate.
If it falls on the chart of a,
one is tormented by plague and illness
and appears frightened and terrified;
this is what a definitely shows.
If it falls on the letter i,
it bestows royal splendor;
one gains wealth on every side,
and whatever one wishes is accomplished.
If it falls on the letter i,
know that within six months there is death,
likewise torment by enemies
and danger from fire.
If it falls on the letter u,
one gains a glorious female companion,
wealth and grain increase,
and splendors too are gained.
If it falls on u,
one appears harmed by enemies,
and servants, wives and kinsfolk
appear frightened and terrified.
If it falls on the letter e,
one at once becomes a fortunate person
and attains the status of one honored
by men and women alike.
If it falls on the sign of a,
there is danger from enemies and kings,
one is tormented by thieves
and struck by illness and plague.
If it falls on the sign of o,
one becomes the master of the place,
wealth and grain are gained,
and likewise a pleasing succession follows.
If it falls on the sign of au,
there are quarrels and harm from enemies,
loss of one's position, beatings and disgrace,
and whatever one has done goes to waste.
If it falls on the letter aṃ,
one gains servants and wives,
one gains sons and learning,
and likewise one gains friends.

Just as with the earlier a,
so too the last is to be taken.
Setting it down sixfold,
make the former likewise threefold.
Having mixed this into three hundred and sixty
and divided it into four places,
take the remaining letter
and so indicate the virtuous and the nonvirtuous.
Or else draw a crooked line
and count from the first of the vowels and the rest.
Or else scatter flowers
and likewise indicate the place.
Or else in the morning, at the time of Rohita,
observe by means of the spokes.
This is the greatly extended treatise of yoga.

In the twelve places
the procedure for the thought that arises is explained.
At the time of Rohitā, think of oneself;
at Lord of the Earth, likewise the household;
at Dream, think of brothers;
at Vyākulī, of relatives;
at Bhidrikā, think of a son;
at Mikirā, an enemy is indicated;
at Raṇḍā, think of union;
at Makara, matters of medical treatment;
at Tortoise, matters of Dharma;
at Bull, fierce acts;
at Excellent, know it to be wealth;
at Delusion, utter turmoil.

If one touches at Increase,
one's aims will be accomplished,
one will come upon wealth and grain,
and one will meet a dear friend.
It will not be long in coming;
one will meet soon, without a doubt.
By merely touching at Lord of the Earth,
going on a journey,
food, the mind, a son and wealth,
and intentions concerning work are shown.
At Dream, one finds wealth,
receives honors and meets friends;
kin and friends are glad at heart,
and peace and the fulfilment of needs are shown.
At Vyākulī there is sorrow,
special aims decline,
and one becomes entangled with relatives.
Bhidrikā brings giving,
and one finds those who speak with love;
one will see a son born.
At Mikirā, disputes
and clamor afflict on every side;
there are thieves and ill omens,
and relatives are struck by illness.
At Raṇḍā, drink and food,
clothes, ornaments and the like are found;
what was lost is found again,
and one meets a beloved woman.
At Makara there is worry,
and endeavors come to nothing;
one is parted from cherished wealth
and does not meet with what one desires.
At Tortoise there is good fortune,
peace, protection and auspiciousness;
seat, wealth and drink are found.
At Bull, in all one's undertakings,
one will surely fear the king's punishment;
there is sorrow and going to another place,
and separations will be seen.
At Excellent, enjoyments
and all one's aims are surely accomplished;
one becomes an object of honor, and friends
and auspicious things will arise.
At Delusion, all one's aims
are like grass without a core;
confinement in one place and thoughts of the road
will be seen.

Listen, O goddess of great fortune!

The signs of the limbs are these. On the day of Increase, if the left eye twitches, father and mother will be frightened. On Lord of the Earth, one appears beautiful. On Dream, one obtains clothing. On Vyākulī, one obtains wealth. On Bhidrikā, harm comes to mother and father. On Mikirā, nothing at all happens. On Raṇḍā, relatives come. On Makara, one obtains clothing. On Tortoise, there is quarreling. On Bull, a guest comes from the north. On Excellent and on Delusion, likewise one obtains clothing.

On the day of Increase, the result of the right eye twitching is also as before. On Lord of the Earth, it is no sign of anything at all. On Dream, there is quarreling. On Vyākulī, one will hear news of a long journey. On Bhidrikā, wealth will decline. On Mikirā, one will obtain wealth. On Raṇḍā, it indicates weeping. On Makara, nothing at all happens. On Tortoise, one obtains clothing. On Bull, one will find what was lost. On Excellent, a guest will come. On Delusion, one meets a messenger.

Again, on the day of Rohitā, if the right ear rings, one meets a woman. With Lord of the Earth, one becomes beautiful. With Dream, one becomes pleased. With Vyākulī, one obtains drink. With Bhidrikā, one becomes somewhat frightened. With Mikirā, it is the same. With Raṇḍā, one is gripped by sorrow. With Makara, one becomes beautiful. With Tortoise, a guest comes. With Bull, one becomes beloved. With Excellent, one becomes pleased. With Delusion, nothing at all happens.

On the day of Rohitā, if the left ear rings, food and drink will come. With Lord of the Earth, it will be good. With Dream, one will see something not seen before. On Vyākulī, one will be bound. On Bhidrikā, quarreling arises. On Mikirā, others come. On Raṇḍā, one will hear of a loss. On Makara, one will hear of a death. On Tortoise, one is gripped by sorrow. On Bull, it indicates good. With Excellent, one will hear bad news. With Delusion, nothing at all happens.

On the day of Rohitā, if the palate itches, a great man comes. With Lord of the Earth, the household will prosper. With Dream, it indicates that someone will come. With Vyākulī, one obtains clothing. With Bhidrikā, worry arises. With Mikirā, it indicates well-being. With Raṇḍā, there is fear. With Makara, the household flourishes. Tortoise indicates that someone will die. Bull indicates that a guest will come. Excellent indicates that thieves will come. Delusion indicates that the household will be beautiful.

On the day of Rohitā, if the whole body twitches, quarreling and strife arise. With Lord of the Earth, one obtains wealth. With Dream, one becomes afraid. With Vyākulī, harm comes to mother and father. Bhidrikā indicates that work is close at hand. With Mikirā, fear arises in father and mother. Raṇḍā indicates that a guest will come from the south. Makara indicates that relief will come. With Tortoise, a guest comes unexpectedly. With Bull, sorrow arises. With Excellent, great fear arises. With Delusion, one recollects the Buddha.

On the day of Rohitā, if the flesh of the foot twitches, it is good. Lord of the Earth is the same. With Dream, people come asking for news. With Vyākulī, there is quarreling. With Bhidrikā, one's master becomes pleased. With Mikirā, too, there is quarreling. With Raṇḍā, there will be quarreling and strife. Makara is the same. Tortoise indicates that someone will die right there. With Bull, one obtains clothing. With Excellent, one will meet those of one's household. With Delusion, one will meet with sorrow.

Growth is the son of Vāsuki;
Delusion likewise is Takṣaka;
Excellent is the Mover by Strength;
Bull is to be known as Padma;
Tortoise likewise is Mahāpadma;
Crocodile is said to be Śaṅkhapāla;
Yoke-Trained is held to be Ananta;
Endowed with Knowledge is to be known as Mole;
Piercer is called the Hundred-Headed;
Roamer is the Goer by Mind;
Dream is called the Clenched;
Lord of the Earth is Rain-Pourer.
These twelve goddesses
are in reality the twelve links of dependent arising that dwell in the year.
Having come to know the stations in this way,
one should examine what is virtuous and nonvirtuous.
Next, the passage through the times:
I shall explain it; practitioners, listen!
Joy and the instant,
and the particular limbs of the fruit,
are to be multiplied threefold,
and all are to be filled out with four quarters.
By the cutting of the knot and by the rising,
time is thus declared.
Punishment, obstructors and planets,
spears and enemies, and falls,
the examination of house sites,
digging into the belly of the mahoraga,
the distinctions of going, coming and joining,
the increase of life and health,
likewise the sowing of seeds,
how livestock are to be tamed,
separation, union and kingship,
and gains to be avoided,
what arises from the mind, and joining battle,
likewise a heap of tree trunks,
the practice of nursing children,
likewise the spreading of eye salve,
times, measures and so on:
these are to be distinguished day by day.
Rasāyana and the two kinds of elixir;
action is to be brought to full ripening.
Through the equipoise in which the mind increases,
it is the very nature of existence and of peace.
Thus elixir, rasāyana and the rest
the wise should come to know with effort.
So spoke Vajraḍāka, supreme of bliss,
joined in union with all the pledges,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder.

From the great king of tantras, the Glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the tenth chapter, called the Section on the Counting of Breaths.

Next is to be rightly explained
the vidyā of supreme accomplishment
called Mahāmāyā,
the sovereign of the great secret,
the friend who tames all sentient beings,
the maker of every sovereignty.
This is the holy, supreme vidyā;
even its mere name is supremely hard to find.
First imagine the source of phenomena;
then the letters are to be set out.
The first vowel, with its bindu,
rests rightly upon a lotus.
It is ever the very nature of breath,
and it acts as the elements.
Its color is beautified by garlands of flame,
radiating in every direction.
Great is the rising of its manifold knots;
meditate on it dwelling in the center of the moon.
One will attain the Goer,
and this becomes the buddha so hard to find.
When even that is abandoned, there is no letter:
unchanging, unsullied,
stainless like a conch or the moon,
that is called the mind of awakening.
In the very center of one's heart,
beautifully adorned with an eight-petaled lotus,
in its center the seed
drips like snow:
the supremely peaceful ha,
joined with the sixth vowel
and adorned above with a bindu;
set out this letter by setting it out.
It brings about the yogin's inhalation,
free of both entity and nonentity.
And dwelling in the center of one's throat,
a red lotus, beautiful with all light;
there the thirteenth vowel,
adorned with bindu and moon,
is renowned as pu lu ta.
Dwelling in the center of the brain within the head,
the indestructible having the form of a bindu,
like an atasa flower,
faces downward and hangs;
by day and night, in the manner of nectar,
it flows down, and so it rightly abides.
First meditate on the cakra,
then join it in particular with the vowels.
Endowed with one's own mudrā,
it fully bestows the awakening of a buddha.
Its observance is not taught:
fire offerings, rites and mantras,
holding, and rosaries
are turned back, one after another.
Mantra, maṇḍala, shapes and teachings
are called the three kinds of application.
Having rendered them free of poison, earnestly
rely on what is to be relied upon,
and the enjoyments of existence will come about,
and through existence one becomes lord of supreme existence.
By means of the three kinds of wisdom
one is not stained by the faults of existence.
If the yogin constantly
and continually practices this application,
within just six months, certainly,
he will become equal to Vajrasattva.
So spoke Vajraḍāka, supreme of bliss,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder.

A full and complete disk of the moon
is to be meditated on in the center of the bhaga.
In its center is the seed,
with a light that is blue and slightly red.
From a tumult of garlands of blue rays
comes one like a blue lotus,
with one face, four arms and three eyes,
hair tawny and standing upright,
fully adorned with all ornaments,
the waist wrapped in a tiger skin,
holding in the hands a hook and a noose,
and drawing the arrow and bow to the full.
Like a sapphire,
meditate on him as arising from the first letter.
Through the full application of his vidyā
everything is accomplished at once.
Having meditated on him as red,
then one should act according to the rite.
Together with the second vowel
one should meditate on the three existences.
With effort make the target appear,
and then dissolve it back into that.
Thereby the three realms, without exception,
come under the practitioner's power.
Brahmā, Viṣṇu, Rudra,
Indra, Kāmadeva and the rest,
urged on by the letters one upon another,
are summoned together with their wives.
That Blessed One is to be known
as chief through the dance of conduct.
Then, endowed with the end of the middle,
fully marked with bindu and sound,
well placed in body, speech and mind:
if one meditates by meditating on the entity,
it is supreme for summoning the piṇḍa.
In the center of the bhaga
meditate on an ocean of wisdom.
From the complete unchanging letter
comes one white in color, with four faces,
holding a vajra and a khaṭvāṅga,
the hero's crown adorned with a half moon,
holding also a basin and a skull cup.
He is to be meditated on with unmoving breath.
Wind and fire are rightly joined,
water and Mahendra are pressed down,
and the one whose nature is emanation emanates.
If with effort one focuses on the target,
it is supreme for summoning seminal fluid.
Arising from the wisdom of ci,
yellow in color, blazing all around,
with four faces and four arms,
the hair adorned with a garland of skulls,
holding the threatening gesture, a vajra noose
and a skull cup in the left hands,
and in the right a blazing vajra
and likewise the supreme hook:
having assumed the form of a horse-face,
it is supreme for summoning liquor.

Arisen from the wisdom of the syllable ra,
with red rays of light, greatly fierce,
the hero wears a garland of skulls
and is fully adorned with every ornament.
He has four faces and four arms,
and the face of a jackal.
He holds a khaṭvāṅga and a skull cup,
and likewise a vajra and a sword.
Meditate on him with a body filled with blood.
With effort, fix your mind on the one to be subdued,
and send forth the wheel of ḍākinīs.
Pierce him with a fine vajra,
and if you meditate intently on sucking,
this is called the supreme rite for drawing out blood.
So spoke the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder,
the Tathāgata Vajrasattva,
renowned as Vajraḍāka,
who is joined in union with all illusions.

From the Great King of Tantras, the Glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the eleventh chapter, the section on the stages of the complete magical display of all illusions.

I shall now explain the means of accomplishment of Vajraḍāka.
Listen, goddess!
First, having meditated on the corpse,
the wise one should contemplate the nature of the dharmadhātu.
Abiding in that, the yogin
should meditate on the supreme state.
In the center of a moon at his own heart
he sets out all the realities.
He worships the ḍākinīs and the others
who arise from their rays of light,
then bows and says these words:

"Buddhas and bodhisattvas,
all of you, please consider me.
I, who am named so-and-so,
from this time onward
until I reach the heart of awakening,
just as the protectors of the three times
made certain of perfect awakening,
so shall I generate
the supreme, unsurpassed mind of awakening.
As the vow that brings forth the yoga of the buddhas,
the Buddha, the Dharma and the Saṅgha,
the unsurpassed Three Jewels,
I shall hold as supreme from this day on.
The moral discipline of training,
the gathering of virtuous qualities,
and the work for the benefit of beings, these three disciplines,
I shall each hold firmly.
For the great and exalted vajra family,
I shall truly take up
the vajra, the bell and the mudrā,
and I shall also take up the master.
For the delightful pledge
of the yoga of the great jewel family,
six times every day
I shall give the four kinds of giving.
For the pure and great lotus family,
which arises from great awakening,
I shall truly hold the sublime Dharma,
the three vehicles, outer, inner and secret.
For the great and supreme action family,
I shall make what offerings I can,
and I shall keep all the vows.
I shall generate that sublime,
unsurpassed mind of awakening,
and for the sake of all sentient beings
I shall take up the vows without exception.
I shall carry across those who have not crossed,
I shall free those who are not free,
I shall give relief to those without relief,
and I shall establish all beings in nirvāṇa."

Then: oṃ svabhāvaśuddhaḥ sarvadharmāḥ svabhāvaśudho'haṃ. Having then recited these words, one views all phenomena as selfless.

That very one is the Blessed Vajra Holder,
the Tathāgata Vajrasattva,
blue in color and greatly terrifying,
adorned with a garland of skulls.
He has four faces and four arms,
and is beautiful with three eyes.
He takes a great preta as his seat,
abides upon a sun, and dances.
In his hands are a skull cup and a khaṭvāṅga,
and also a bow and arrow.
Through that very ritual procedure
one should meditate on the ocean of wisdom.
In the east she is white with blue light;
in her left hands a khaṭvāṅga and a bell,
in the others a vajra and a skull cup.
In the north she is emerald with yellowish light;
in her left hands a khaṭvāṅga and a noose,
in her right she holds a small drum and a skull cup.
In the west she is red with yellowish light;
a supreme bow and arrow,
and a crossed lotus and a skull cup
are placed in her left and right hands.
In the south she is white with yellow light;
in her left hands a conch and a jewel,
in her right she holds a banner and a skull cup.
Their skull cups should be, in order,
those of an elephant, a jackal, a human and an ox.
The first is Pātanī,
the second likewise Māraṇī,
the third is called Ākarṣaṇī,
and the fourth is Narteśvarī.
These knowledge women have blazing hair standing upright,
are fully adorned with every ornament,
and abide in the posture of dance.
In the four intermediate directions
set four kinds of skull cups,
adorned with forms of the Buddha
and clearly crowned with the uṣṇīṣa.
If one meditates on them joined with the first letter,
in that instant they blaze
and grant the excellent fruit, the accomplishment of substances.
Goddess, if one meditates in this way,
there is great knowledge of the accomplishment of union,
and true confidence will arise.
What is invisible, and all things,
the practitioner will see in an instant.
Having carried off those born of the supreme gods,
he enjoys the daughters of all the buddhas.
Buddhas as many as the sands of the Ganges,
each abiding in their own vajra abode,
he will see with the vajra eye
like a fruit placed in his hand.
In fields as many as the sands of the Ganges,
all the marks of body, speech and mind
he will see arising
from the drawing of his own mind.
With the letters of the mantra reversed,
one should meditate on the tantra and the mantra.
These great and sublime secrets
I have explained to you, beautiful one.

At dawn, at one's own heart,
imagine a moon maṇḍala arising from a.
Upon it meditate on the syllable hūṃ,
and having generated a mind of loving-kindness,
one should then draw the maṇḍala.
It is square, with four doors,
and its parapet likewise marked with vajras.
It has four lines,
and is beautified with garlands of silk.
At all the corners of the maṇḍala,
and at the doors and the door alcoves,
richly adorned with vajras and jewels,
one should lay the lines of the outer maṇḍala.
Within it, the one possessed of wisdom
adorns it with a garland of wheels,
and in its center makes a lotus
most beautiful in white.
Again, in the center of that,
draw a radiant red lotus.
Then, within that, the mantra practitioner
draws a garland of vajras, all interlaced.
Again, draw a great eight-petaled lotus
blazing with the light of sapphire.
Within the vajra pillars,
adorned with five maṇḍalas,
on top of Mount Meru,
imagine a lovely crossed lotus.
So spoke Vajraḍāka, the supreme bliss,
joined in union with all ḍākas,
the Tathāgata Vajrasattva,
the Blessed Vajra Holder.

From the Great King of Tantras, the Glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the twelfth chapter, the section on the stages of the arising of Vajraḍāka.

Next I shall properly explain
the thorough accomplishment of all attainments,
by merely knowing which
the practitioner will gain attainment.

Having imagined A at the tip of the nose,
imagine PA at the center of the liṅga.
Then, joining them in union,
join all the wheels of the yoginīs
at the centers of the lotuses.
Likewise the place of the avadhūtī
should be set up to the point between the eyebrows.
Fully endowed with the first vowel,
when it is meditated upon, it blazes in that instant.
Then, in the ocean of blazing garlands,
imagine the wheel of doors all around.

The wheels of the navel, heart and mouth,
of the forehead and of the crown of the head:
first bless them with the assemblies of mantras.
At the navel and the heart is the yoginī;
the two tongues are blessed by the hero.
The hero and the yoginī,
according to the nondual rite,
abide in the wheel of the whirling firebrand,
and the ḍākinīs likewise circle.
Their light is clear like lightning;
by the great force of the mind, their knowledge is great.
At the time of deity yoga
one should emanate them into the ten directions.
The assemblies of extremely subtle particles
should be meditated upon as extremely subtle entities.
Unchanging and without appearance,
this grants the fruit of omniscience.
Having imagined the abode in a flower,
whoever it is given to becomes sealed.

Imagine the application of Pātanī,
who makes a light like conch and moon.
Having placed the target in her mouth,
if one rises and recites, he falls.
Then imagine in the mind Māraṇī,
abiding on the sun,
spear-like, with the light of the sun,
utterly terrifying like the fire at the end of the eon.
Becoming one together with the target,
if one looks upward, he dies.
Then imagine Ākarṣaṇī,
abiding in the maṇḍala of wind and fire,
radiating masses of sparks.
Entering the target's heart with the mind,
pull his heart out from the root.
Reciting the mantra of Ākarṣaṇī, he is summoned.
With a mind of the wind maṇḍala,
abiding in the wind maṇḍala,
imagine the target, the color of wind, within the wind.
Again, oneself and the target
become one; if one recites the mantra
of Narteśvarī, the Dancer, and dances,
the one who dances is made to dance.
With offerings, by the vajra application,
one should then accomplish all the actions.

The heart itself, and likewise the mouth,
the head, the crown, and the weapon:
at the navel and the heart
one should meditate with unmoving breath.
The first is Vajrasattva,
the second Padmanarteśvara, the king,
the third Vairocana,
likewise the fourth Paramāśva, the king,
the fifth Vajrasūrya,
and the sixth is said to be Heruka.
Again, the navel, the heart,
likewise the mouth and the head,
the crown, and the weapon itself:
Vajravārāhī, Yāminī,
likewise Mohinī, the Protectress,
Santrāsanī and Caṇḍikā.
These six are of great knowledge,
the six kinds of perfection.

Then, having meditated on the empowerment,
afterwards one should begin the offering.
Having made oneself Heruka,
one should call to mind the glorious Heruka.
From the oneness of the samaya and the wisdom beings,
he is therefore called the Vajra Holder.
This yoga is the great offering,
and likewise the setting out of the mantras.
Then, knowing the offering of the heroes,
one should perform the actions with diligence.
The mantra practitioner should differentiate them at will
and apply them definitely in reverse.
One should meditate in the colors of the actions
of subjugation and of benefiting.
Having divided it by its divisions,
one should apply it to the path of the channels.
For the aims of the application,
one should accomplish with this great joy.
If one accomplishes by this rite,
gods, asuras and humans are brought under one's power.

Vajraḍāka, supreme bliss,
united with all the ḍākas:
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One (the Buddha), the Vajra Holder, has spoken.

From the Great King of Tantras, the Glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the thirteenth chapter, the section on the nondual offering of the heroes and the arising of the assemblies of actions.

Now I shall explain
the distinctions of abiding in the wheels
of body, speech and mind,
the classification of the places,
and the divisions of the lands.
The head is the land of Malaya;
the crown is Jālandhara;
Oḍḍiyāna is the right ear;
Arbuda is the back.
These four bear the name of pīṭha, place.
Godāvarī is the left ear;
Rāmeśvarī is said to be between the brows;
the eyes are Devīkoṭi;
the shoulders are called Mālava.
These are the secondary places.
By way of the distinctions of the places,
they abide in the mind wheel of the sky-goers.
Kāmarū is the armpits; likewise
the breasts are renowned as Oḍra.
These are fields; the navel
is called Triśakuni;
the tip of the nose is Kosala.
These are called the secondary fields.
The mouth is Kaliṅga;
the throat is said to be Lampāka;
these are renowned as chandoha.
The heart is called Kāñci;
the urinary passage is called Himālaya;
these are said to be the secondary chandoha.
By way of the distinctions of the places,
they abide in the speech wheel of the earth-goers.
The genitals are Pretapurī;
the anus is Gṛhadevatā;
these are said to be meeting places.
The thighs are called Saurāṣṭra;
the two shanks are Suvarṇadvīpa;
these are the secondary meeting places.
The toes are Nagara;
the soles of the feet are Sindhu;
these are said to be charnel grounds.
The big toes are called Maru;
the knees are said to be Kulutā;
these secondary charnel grounds, the underworld,
abide fully in the body wheel.
These lands arisen in one's own body
are said to be the places of the sky-going women.
Outer and inner, they abide perfectly;
divided into twenty-four,
they are the wheels of body, speech and mind.
In all of the places
all the channels are fully explained.
"These lands are filled with maidens,
who abide in the form of the channels."
Thus the Saṃvara of the net of ḍākinīs,
united with all the heroes,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder, has spoken.

Again I shall explain it in brief;
listen as I explain it.
At Pullīra and Malaya are Khaṇḍakapāla and Pracaṇḍā; teeth and nails flow there.
At Jālandhara are Mahākaṅkāla and Caṇḍākṣī; head hair and body hair flow there.
At Oḍiyāna are Kaṅkāla and Prabhāvatī; skin and impurities flow there.
At Arbuda are Vikaṭadaṃṣṭra and Mahānāsā; flesh flows there; they abide there.
At Godāvarī are Suravairin and Vīramatī; sinews flow there everywhere.
At Rāmeśvarī are Amitābha and Kharvarī; a garland of bones flows there.

At Devīkoṭa are Vajraprabha and Laṅkeśvarī,
and liver always flows there.
At Mālava are Vajradeha and Drumacchāyā,
and heart flows there; this is a secondary seat.
These, as taught here,
are the wheel of mind, the sky-goers;
being absorbed in union, they truly abide.

At Kāmarūpa are Aṅkurika and Airāvatī,
and eyes always flow there.
At Oḍra are Vajrajaṭila and Mahābhairavā;
bile is said to flow there, and this is a field.
At Triśakuni are Mahāvīra and Vāyuvegā,
and lungs flow there.
At Kosala are Vajrahūṃkara and Surābhakṣī,
and a garland of intestines flows there;
this is a secondary field.
At Kaliṅga are Subhadra and the goddess Śyāmā,
and the membrane of the ribs flows there.
At Lampāka are Vajrabhadra and Subhadrā,
and stomach, as of donkeys, flows there;
this is a pleasure ground.
At Kāñcī are Mahābhairava and Hayakarṇā,
and feces are said to flow there.
At Himālaya are Virūpākṣa and Kākāsyā,
and the parting of the crown flows there;
this is a secondary pleasure ground.
This is the wheel of speech, the earth-goers;
being absorbed in union, they truly abide.

At Pretapuri are Mahābala and Cakravegā,
and phlegm flows everywhere.
At Gṛhadevatā are Ratnavajra and Khaṇḍarohā,
and pus always flows there; this is a gathering place.
At Saurāṣṭra are Hayagrīva and Śauṇḍinī,
and blood is said to flow there always.
At Suvarṇadvīpa are Ākāśagarbha and Cakravarmiṇī,
and likewise menstrual fluid flows there;
this is a secondary gathering place.
At Nagara are Suravairin and Suvīrā,
and fat flows there.
In the land of Sindhu are Padmanarteśvara and Mahābalā,
and tears always flow there; this is a charnel ground.
At Maru are Vairocana and Cakravartinī,
and saliva is said to flow there.
At Kulatā are Vajrasattva and Mahāvīryā,
and fresh mucus flows there; this is a secondary charnel ground.
The wheel of body, those who move beneath the earth,
being absorbed in union, have entered.
So spoke the one in union with all the ḍākinīs,
Vajraḍāka, supreme in bliss,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder.

From the great king of tantras, the glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the fourteenth chapter, called the Teaching on the Places of the Nondual Heroes.

Then, the most supreme of rites,
the most excellent of all applications,
the mantra that accomplishes anything whatsoever:
I shall explain its divisions in due order.
The mantras of letters, messengers and the rest
are encircled by the force of the arrow.
In the center of the wheel of emanation,
it is fully encircled by the eight groups.
From it all mantras arise,
and moreover whatever else there may be is born.
What is called the wheel of Dharma
gladdens the hearts of embodied beings.
It is endowed with the five sounds
and adorned with ya ra la va.
What is called the wheel of enjoyment
has the nature of the support of the tastes;
by the four parts
it is encircled all around.
The great wheel of great bliss
possesses the thirty-two of awakening.
Those called moon and sun,
on the two sides, left and right,
are declared to be, with certainty,
the going and coming of the heroes and ḍākinīs.
Mounted equally upon the two places,
they rest truly below and above.
On the two sides, left and right,
the twelve vowels are written facing upward.
kā and the rest, facing downward,
are interspersed on the two sides,
and in the center one should join them with that.
These, as taught here,
are the nature of channels and of sound.
The nondual wisdom is the vajra;
the ḍākinī is the nature of sound.
Renunciation is all the buddhas;
it is renowned as the place where they are born.
The utterly pure great wisdom,
which is the nature of all the goddesses,
is called Vajrasattva,
and is declared to be supreme bliss.
This very self-arisen form
emanates and gathers back.
The mantras of letters, messengers and the rest:
whatever there is free of parts,
formless, in the center of the bhaga,
that abides always.
Between the brows, in the moon of the lotus,
it is encircled by sixteen threads.
Through the union of wisdom and means
it is declared to be what is called "union."
The two arms embrace completely;
this is declared to be the vajra chain.
Again, the path of liberation:
the mass that flows is supremely joyful.
Union itself and
the vajra chain are so held.
The separating of vajra and lotus
is explained as producing the honey of the feather tuft.
Circling, because it moves,
by the means of that bliss all is known.
By the nature of joy and bliss
the noose is inserted into the lotus.
Passion alone is the hook;
summon all the tathāgatas.
With the unshakable vajra one should churn;
that is declared to be the kindling stick.
Then the habitual tendencies of the afflictions, passion and the rest,
should be broken into pieces.
With a very vast visualization,
likewise strike the conventional,
and swallow all things with the vajra.
The Tathāgata is what is called vajra.
However it is taught, in its center
is the one who constantly emits and gathers in.
That is called suchness;
it is pervaded by the one self-arisen.
Whose name is renowned
as the illusion of all the buddhas,
in the delight of the supreme secret,
where the self of all abides:
this is called the omniscient one,
the all-pervading, the indestructible.
At the tip of the nose of the lotus
one should meditate on all that moves and does not move.
Having abandoned external letters,
one should engage in mental recitation.
The definitive state abides always;
it is taught as the nature of the dharmadhātu.
Ever subtle, free of dust,
the wisdom body, stainless;
know the luminous nature of phenomena,
whose sole domain is nirvāṇa.
The secret mantras knotted to one another,
having been separated one by one,
one should then meditate on the garland of seeds
and string them on the thread of hūṃ.
If the one to be subdued is to be halted,
he will not go even two steps.
Its wisdom is coemergent;
it abides, having become its form.
Stirred by the wind of karma,
it blazes up from this maṇḍala of the navel.
It is explained as what is called Mahāmāyā;
it fully accomplishes all rites.

A portion one hundredth of a hair's tip,
with light rays like a flash of lightning in an instant,
at the time of deity yoga
streams out from the pores of the body hairs
into the ten directions,
threatens all the gods and asuras,
burns the Dharma wheel at the heart,
and goes to the wheel of enjoyment.
Emerging from the right nostril,
it enters again and, coming out
from the opening of the ūrṇā hair between the brows,
it enters the left nostrils
of all the buddhas and bodhisattvas
who dwell in the ten directions.
Having burned the crown wheel,
it comes back out by the former path
and enters again at the crown of the head.
The buddhas who were burned,
filled with joy, go
to the maṇḍala of the navel
and abide there as before.
This itself is victory over untimely death.
Join it with the khaṭvāṅga.
In a skull cup as the vessel for the offering water,
offer the water to the mouth without fail.
So spoke Vajraḍāka, supreme in bliss,
in union with all the ḍākas,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder.

This is the fifteenth chapter, called "The Arising of the Three Bodies, of Mantra and of Tantra, and the Ransoming of Death," from the great king of tantras, the glorious Vajraḍāka.

Then, at the abode of all accomplishments,
the delight of the supreme secret,
the lovely essence, the support of all,
which bestows bliss on all sentient beings,
adorned with the five sense pleasures,
blazing with a fine canopy spread above,
to him who sat on the seat of union,
a great lotus of a thousand petals,
the goddess there made this request:
"I do not know the certainty of all the wheels,
the distinctions of the vowels,
or the divisions of the letters.
What is the number of the wheels?
What are their rites?
The mantras of the various songs,
the yantras in brief,
the times of the Lord of the Wheel,
and likewise the seats of the heroes:
about these I have doubts.
May the Great Bliss please explain them."

The Blessed One spoke:
"You who wish to ask about all
that is hidden throughout the tantras:
excellent, excellent, great goddess!
Great intelligent one, it is excellent.
If even all the ḍākinīs
have doubts, what need to speak of others?
You whose magical display is all illusion,
listen, goddess Mahāmāyā!
That by the mere drawing of which
the practitioner will gain accomplishment
is to be stated in brief:
I shall explain it, so listen.
Keeping always to black and red,
give torma above all;
eat the caru food together;
the practitioner circles from the left.
Through this, for all practitioners,
the mātṛkās will be pleased, without doubt.
Then, the ḍākinīs
who sport upon the grounds:
Mahāvīryā and Cakravartinī,
Mahābalā and Suvīrā,
Cakravarmiṇī, Śauṇḍinī,
Khaṇḍarohā, Cakravegā,
Khagānanā and Hayakarṇā,
Subhadrā, Śyāmādevī,
Surābhakṣī and Vāyuvegā,
likewise Mahābhairavā,
Airāvatī and Drumacchāyā,
Laṅkeśvarī, Kharvarī,
Vīramatī, Mahānāsā,
Prabhāvatī and Caṇḍākṣī,
and Pracaṇḍā: these are the accomplishers.
These are the twenty-four ḍākinīs;
they were accomplished long ago.
To the mantra that states each one's own name
join hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ at the end;
oṃ makes them all shine forth.
For all who wish to request accomplishment,
once they have seen this supreme sacred yoga,
all else is like grass without essence.
The accomplishment of becoming a hero oneself
comes from seeing the sacred maṇḍala
of glorious Heruka.
Then, for one who knows it and meditates constantly,
the Tathāgata has declared accomplishment.

Listen, goddess endowed with wisdom,
to the means of accomplishing the king of vidyās,
a great and marvelous wheel
called the Lord of the Hooded Ones.
If even by merely being heard it burns them,
then when it is drawn together with a name,
what need to mention all the hooded ones?
Beginning from Īśāna, the northeast,
imagine nāgas arising from seed syllables,
endowed with jewels, with three raised hoods,
and imagine them in all the other directions as well.
Then, taking up this song,
write kṣa in the east and maṃ in the middle,
hi in the west and haṃ in the north;
in the direction of Yama write hūṃ;
kili kili in that of the pretas;
in that of the rākṣasas, cili as many as there are;
in that of the wind write sili sili;
in the direction of fire, dhili.
Beginning first from Pracaṇḍā
and ending with Mahāvīryā,
these mantras are to be consecrated.
This wheel possesses great fortune;
it severs the vital points of enemies.
In its center write the target,
interspersed with hūṃ phaṭ.
Apply as fumigation
guggulu recited over once,
and all the nāgas will be brought down."

Draw this wheel on the left hand, either physically or mentally, and do this while reciting the mantra once; by this one raises up a person whom death has seized at the time of dying.

If it is written with the juice of karavīra leaves using a karavīra pen, recited over once, and touched, one will transfer into whatever womb one wishes.

If it is written with poison, salt and mustard, then when one is angry it will kill.

Quench a firebrand from a cremation fire with datura juice, and on the day of the bhūtas draw the wheel with that same firebrand and recite over it once; whoever is struck with it will fall down.

Write the mantra with a pen of saṅkoṭa according to the previous rite, and consecrate ash with mantra; whoever is struck with it will be seized by a piśāca.

With charcoal from the charnel ground, on a wooden board or a wall, on the night of the bhūtas, whoever has his name written at the heart will be seized by a great graha.

Make an effigy of the target, insert the yantra into its heart, and keep it in scented water for seven days. Consecrate an atimukta twig seven times, and if one strikes the effigy with it, at the end of seven days the person will come with the speed of the wind.

That same wheel, in particular, should be drawn on charnel-ground cloth, distinguished by the colors of the messengers, and practiced together with the action messengers.

On a karavīra twig one and a half cubits long, write the mantra while facing toward the target, throw it into a blazing cremation fire, and quickly take it out before it is burned. Recite the king of vidyās seven times, call someone to mind, and whirl the twig around the head; that person will instantly come with the speed of the wind.

On the skull bowl of a man's corpse, write the name of anyone at all with orpiment and turmeric juice, and that person is bound that very instant. On cloth from a charnel ground, or on menstrual cloth, draw with a pen of aśoka wood this wheel that drives away the nāgas. If the mantrin, following the rite, writes in its center the name interspersed with the letter e, all the various kinds of ḍākinīs and all pūtanas are bound.

On a skull, write the name of anyone at all with a man's blood. If the mantrin recites the mantra with wrath, it stops that person's blood. If, with a peaceful mind, he recites the mantra peacefully, it releases it again.

Draw that same wheel, adorned with a moon, clearly in gorocana on birch bark on the day of the constellation Puṣya. If any woman wears it constantly, the King of Knowledge Mantras himself enters her womb. That supreme man who always engages in peaceful union with a woman has a glory in the palm of his hand that no one can steal. A rite of this kind bestows the fruit of all accomplishments.

With this Blessed One, even without vows, conduct, fasting or ritual, one carries out every action just as one wishes, by this very method of subtle ritual application.

Imagine in the center of the expanse of space
a sublime moon disk,
a mass of light rays, a clear radiance,
encircled by a garland of wheels.
In its center is an eight-spoked wheel,
beautifully adorned with a garland of lotuses.
Then a further wheel should be drawn,
radiating a garland of vajras.
It is like the maṇḍala of the maṇḍala
of the vajra of body, speech and mind.
Covering the whole of space,
one should emanate it from one's own body.
White, red and blue:
distinguish the supreme colors.
By this ritual procedure,
draw likewise the Lord of Heroes.
In the center the name of the target
should be inserted as one wishes.
Outside, a garland of vajras
should be drawn, a tumult of blazing rays.
This is the great protection,
the unsurpassed protection of all.
Epidemics, illness and poison,
harm from ḍākinīs,
grahas, māras, vināyakas,
pūtanas and any others such as these
will not even step upon its shadow;
what need is there to mention its body?
The letters, like images of the moon,
scatter them in the ten directions.
With white there is pacification;
with black one kills in an instant;
with red one brings under control,
and with that same color one surely summons;
with yellow there is sure binding,
and whole armies are bound.

So spoke the Blessed One. This is the protection wheel called Source of All Actions.

Next I shall rightly explain
the particulars of the wheel of command.
By seeing the rite of action,
one acts for the benefit of all beings.
Abiding in the center of the expanse of space,
like a moon disk,
imagine a maṇḍala like a wheel.
In its center a lotus should be drawn,
adorned with sixteen petals.
In its center, a great eight-petaled lotus
with a supreme five-pronged vajra,
beautiful with white light rays.
The practitioner draws the outside of the wheel.
In the center of the lotus write svāhā;
it bestows all accomplishments.

In a delightful house of the mātṛkās,
smeared with red sandalwood,
on a soft and very smooth
seat, bind the yoni mudrā.
The mantrin, facing the target,
should recite one hundred and eight times.
Imagining his limbs adorned with bone ornaments,
naked, he should draw it himself
on the cloth of the dead, or on
the dried-out pots of a corpse,
with the nail-dye of the dead and blood
and likewise red sandalwood.
Staying upon that wheel,
in three sessions, as he wishes,
the wise one moves his little finger,
and even the killing of a brahmin is accomplished in seven days.

From red sandalwood powder
make an effigy of the target;
smear its body with the three hot spices
and smear it also with beeswax.
Pierce its genitals with a copper needle.
Over smokeless embers of khadira wood
the wise one warms it for seven days.
If the practitioner recites a hundred times,
the king with his ministers is brought before him;
together with his retinue of queens
he is swiftly summoned.

On a leaf of birch bark,
with the nail-dye of a corpse and rocana,
the lord of men with his retinue
should be written, interspersed with the name.
While reciting, facing the target,
in a fire of khadira and of the charnel ground,
so long as the beeswax has not melted,
one should heat it over the fire.
If merely by tormenting the target
even gods and asuras come,
what need to mention people on earth?

With the nail-dye of the dead, a man's
blood and gorocana,
write on birch bark, interspersed with the target's name.
Earth from the target's footprint,
red sandalwood itself,
and leftover food and ashes from the charnel ground:
the practitioner gathers these together.
In a smokeless khadira fire,
kneading that powder with both hands,
he performs the fire offering;
then, taking those ashes,
he makes an effigy of the target
and places the yantra in its heart.
If it is heated in the fire while he recites,
the target faints and loses his mind;
deluded and utterly intoxicated,
he comes with the speed of the wind.
For this ritual procedure
twenty-two syllables are prescribed.
These great mantras of the goddess
are the crushers of the ḍākinīs' hearts.

Then she who brings forth the delightful peace
of all the tathāgatas:
I shall explain the wheel of command.
Imagined in the center of space
is a sublime moon disk;
a three-pronged vajra with four faces
should then be drawn with it.
In its center, an eight-petaled lotus
of great beauty having been drawn,
there is the great knowledge mantra of Vairocana,
she who brings forth and gathers all beings.
So spoke Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder.

This is the application of the protection wheel called Eliminator of All Diseases.

Then the Blessed One, the Omniscient,
declared the wheel called the Higher Knowledge of Wisdom:
Draw a lotus with six petals,
with anthers at its hub,
adorned close about with a fine moon,
encircled by a garland of vajras.
Outside it is a wheel,
stainless like the autumn moon.
The supreme letters are of seven kinds;
the great goddess, the one who benefits.

This is called the King of Wheels, which destroys the works of all māras.

With white sandalwood and gorocana,
draw it on birch bark,
and hold the wheel in the left hand.
This wheel, O great goddess,
is adorned with the light of action.
Then imagine the target in the center of the sun,
and recite a thousand times.
Imagine the letters as if made of crystal,
and show it to one tormented by illness.
At the very moment of seeing it,
that one will be wholly freed from all illnesses;
of this there is no doubt.
If this wheel is set out in the left hand
in the center of the moon,
it becomes like the autumn moon.
When this wheel, O great goddess,
is richly adorned with the light of action,
through the sight of the rite of action
it accomplishes every task.

On charnel-ground cloth, or on a skull,
or on a leaf of the arka plant,
with the charcoal taught before
and an ink of a man's blood,
the practitioner draws with a pen of human bone.
With a wrathful mind, placing it in the center of the sun,
make an effigy, and in the center
of the target, insert it;
smear it with mustard seed
and heat it again and again.
Through urging on the king of mantras,
it accomplishes every task.
All of that person's illnesses
it makes increase, and likewise inflicts them.
For fever, draw a preta at the heart;
for some, a preta at the brain;
for apasmāra, draw upon wind and cloud;
for inflicting stabbing pain, upon a spear;
for poison and leprosy, upon a mahoraga;
for the planets, draw upon the planet's sign.
In brief, set them out in the wheel.

The letters, like crystal and the light of the moon,
pour forth a stream of nectar,
like the rays of the autumn moon.
Merely by contemplating them in meditative concentration
even the dying are surely raised up.
Even one seized by a hundred great serpents
and dying, it swiftly raises up.
Those who have fainted from poison
are raised up merely by its being shown.
Fevers, grahas and apasmāras
are destroyed by the sight of the hand.
If they will not even tread upon its shadow,
what need is there to speak of looking at its body?

On a leaf of copper,
or in the left hand, this wheel
should be drawn with yellow pigment and turmeric.
Then place it in water
and worship it diligently with whatever is at hand.
At the junctures of the day, in meditative equipoise,
recite the mantra in the same way.
Take it out of the water,
wash it with milk, and cast it into the ocean.
In battle, holding the fist clenched,
adorned with every ornament,
if one constantly recites in the mind,
then as long as the fist is not opened,
all swiftly become paralyzed.
Even if the enemy has raised a weapon
and holds it in his hand,
as long as the fist is not opened
he cannot let the weapon fly.

These seven letters and the letters
of the six yoginīs,
abiding in the six wheels,
are set out in the center of the hand
and held according to the stages of the rite.
Reciting the king of secret mantras aloud,
and seizing the image of that person,
he is summoned; there is no doubt.

Powder of red sandalwood,
salt, and likewise mustard seed,
and dust from the target's footprint
should be combined into one and ground.
If one recites a hundred times and meditates intently,
the ruler of the land, together with his people and wealth,
comes under one's power,
or he is summoned; there is no doubt.
This application is proven;
I have not spoken of it elsewhere.

Taking that same dust,
mix it with iron filings,
wrap it in charnel-ground cloth,
and recite over it seven times.
At a crossroads dig about eight finger-widths,
and uttering the enemy's name,
cover it over with earth.
The one whose name was uttered becomes bound.
Taking that same powder,
wash it diligently with milk,
and he is freed again; there is no doubt.

Taking that same powder,
combine it with the five maddening substances.
On a corpse's cloth write
the target's name and wrap it.
Recite a hundred times, and if it is hidden
in a charnel ground, the man at once
goes mad; of this there is no doubt.
If it is taken out and diligently
washed again with cow's milk,
he is released; here there is no doubt.
So spoke Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder.

Then the Blessed One, the Tathāgata,
spoke the wheel of the Vajra Hero:
In the center of the moon a vajra,
three-pronged and likewise six-faced.
Again, upon a moon, draw a lotus
well set with six petals.
The seed syllable in its center
accomplishes every desired aim.
The mantra practitioner should draw it at the hub,
interspersed with the target's name.
The deities, Vajrasattva and the rest,
all manifold and unsurpassed,
the great armor of all the buddhas,
I explain to you, fair-faced one.
In the center draw the secret mantra given before;
on the lotus petals, likewise the others.
Leaving out the six long vowels entirely,
draw them at the hub of the vajra.
With saffron, lac, one's own blood,
and gorocana, on birch bark,
having drawn it according to the rite,
recite at the three times of day,
make a great offering, and then wear it.
Reciting over it seven times,
