tie it to the hem of one's garment.
When traveling on the road, there will be no fear
of thieves, tigers and the like.

It will protect in every way;
it will succeed, of this there is no doubt.
There at the center, with drops,
one should also make a ring of drops around it.
With the juice of aricataka
and, above all, with turmeric,
using a pen of aśoka wood,
one should write on the cloth of a hero.
Interspersing it with the name of the target,
one should then recite this mantra a thousand times.
From earth from the target's footprint,
dust from an elephant's footprint,
together with charnel-ground ash,
the practitioner makes an effigy,
binds it with bonds, and then
places this at its heart.
Having worshipped it with yellow flowers,
if it is hidden in a secret place,
all enemies will be bound
and terribly destroyed.
So spoke Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder.
Then the Blessed One (the Buddha) spoke
the heart mantra of the ḍākinī.

The seed that has become the expanse of space
radiates light rays everywhere.
Stainless like the autumn moon,
one should make a wheel of twelve spokes.
On those spokes one should set
the supremely beneficial letters of the seed.
In its center one should set
the great letter of the Great Vidyā.
Knowing the vajra rite of protection,
one should arm all the ḍākinīs.
In the center, with the urging of the king of mantras,
one should write the name of the target.
Endowed with all the supreme aspects,
one whose nature is to emanate should emanate.
With saffron and gorocana,
on birch bark or on white cloth,
one of great courage should write.
The practitioner who has seen the ritual of the act
protects in every way and,
as taught before,
subdues the wicked.

Next I shall explain the supreme act
that bestows all accomplishments,
by merely accomplishing which
one brings the three worlds under one's power.
A twice-born girl of the vajra family,
or else another girl of beautiful form:
with red karavīra flowers
one should accomplish it by the royal application.
Having consecrated that flower with gorocana,
one places it in water,
and the practitioner, deeply composed,
should recite a hundred times.
Entering a great river,
immersed in the water below the navel,
holding the joined palms at the center,
one should recite the vidyā a thousand times.
Together with a handful filled with water,
the practitioner should drink.
Then it will be accomplished:
through the practitioner's accomplished speech,
kings together with their wives
will be summoned by the mind, without doubt.

Taking properly
a red karavīra flower,
one recites the vidyā seven times
and places it in the center of the womb.
Closing that flower,
one should place it in the secret place.
The yogin of nondual wisdom
should recite it one hundred times.
The practitioner, with one-pointed mind,
takes up that flower,
and whoever is struck by the flower
comes under control that very instant.
Fainting and bereft of mind,
all limbs and joints under another's power,
the manifest mind turned back,
in an instant he is brought under control.
This is the unfailing subjugation
accomplished in direct perception.
Thus spoke the Blessed One. This is the rite for summoning yoginīs, called the King of Wheels.

Next I shall properly explain that
by merely drawing which
Vajrasattva is overwhelmed
and swiftly grants accomplishments.
A vajra placed in the center of a moon,
and likewise also a lotus:
one should set the seed of the ḍākinī,
radiating in all directions.
In the center Vajravārāhī,
a form most fierce and clear,
should be set by the setting of letters.
In the order thus stated:
for pacifying, with a peaceful mind,
one writes with gorocana on birch bark.
At a shrine of the mātṛkās and in a charnel ground,
a great cloth likewise for the wrathful;
for passion, a red cloth;
for increase, likewise a yellow cloth.
Having meditated in each case,
one should emanate as is fitting.

Having carefully taken
the cloth from the corpse of a young girl,
on the eighth and the fourteenth days,
with charcoal produced from that very thing,
on the four prongs of a crossed vajra
one writes, interspersed with the mantra.
Having recited seven times,
in whosever house it is hidden,
that person will go
to the city of Yama in seven days.
In the hollow of its leg bone,
or in a vase and the like,
or in the hollow of a bone peg,
this yantra should be inserted.
By this application of the rite
he will surely die here.
In all acts the practitioner
should be endowed with the rite.
With a mantra taken up on one's own,
there is no accomplishment in a billion eons.
Know this act to be supreme;
what is the use of elaborating other mantras?
Abandoning all of them,
one should diligently take up this alone.
With whatever meditative concentration
the practitioner undertakes recitation,
through each such application
he will obtain the desired fruit.
Just as a pure jewel
takes on the form of whatever substance it rests upon,
so too Vajraḍāka
holds the supreme true meditative concentration.
With whatever intention
people properly apply their minds,
they become that very thing,
like a jewel of many forms.
The Compendium of Reality and the Assembly,
and others, earlier and later,
possessing rites for applying reality,
in all the action tantras,
until destruction by fire,
beings there will be afflicted.
It is hidden in all of them;
it is the diligence that vanquishes adversaries.
The great vajra master,
equal to Vajrasattva,
causes accomplishment to arise swiftly
and at once inspires true confidence.
Through the application of the mudrā the practitioner
will succeed without affliction.
If it is read to people without samaya,
or if it is given to them,
that wicked one is ruined
and falls into the lower realms.
Focusing on the left-hand tantra,
people should practice left-hand conduct.
If everything is done with the left,
accomplishment is swiftly attained.
So spoke Vajraḍāka, supreme bliss,
united equally with all illusions,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder.

From the great king of tantras, the glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the sixteenth chapter, called the Arising of the Wheel United Equally with All Heroes.

Next I shall properly explain,
for the benefit of all sentient beings,
what gathers together all the essences:
the supreme preparation of the practitioner's nectar.
Camphor and saffron,
gandha and gandhapatra,
cinnamon together with cardamom,
kusum and likewise water,
pisita and kunduru resin,
likewise mountain fat,
mountain-born and the fat of the hollow reed,
fragrant oil and tooth,
likewise the powder of nail perfume,
snow water and the essence,
and what is born of the ocean: these
benefit all embodied beings,
each and every one, like snow water.
Thus these eighteen substances
are supreme for rasāyana, the taking of essence, and are beneficial.

Next are taught the rites of examination,
the qualities, the components and the procedures.
Campaka and bakula,
nāgakesara, kumuda,
utpala and śephālikā,
honey-drip and priyaṅgu:
these are the flowers.
Śrībala, first, and the chewing stick,
kapittha and lakuca,
kuba and vārtākī,
arura and barura,
bhallātaka, skyurura,
cardamom sap and punaka,
eraṇḍa and marica,
and likewise long pepper,
cawa and sweet flag,
turmeric and costus,
dry ginger and garlic,
the dowa of moist places,
white nāgaphala,
likewise "that which makes the old young,"
"the elephant's foe" and "mountain beauty,"
"the dripping one" and "dense child,"
"red mane" and "the maddener,"
"night tree" and "swift-goer,"
kadamba and the golden tree,
and likewise śirīṣa,
karañja and śigru,
nimba and paṃlasi,
likewise gadumajari,
palāśa and the two sandalwoods,
likewise black aloeswood,
saffron, gurshing, bekara,
honey, mokṣa, puṣkara:
flowers, fruits and roots,
bark, wood and their juices.

The cuckoo, the copper-crested one,
the crow and the garra,
the peacock, the magpie, the pigeon,
and the sage's son increase strength.
Goat and hare and deer,
sāliga and pig,
and especially rats,
destroy all diseases.
Liquor of flowers, fruits and bark,
and likewise liquor of grain,
and other kinds of liquor besides:
in brief, these are the supreme drinks.
These are medicines that build up
the strength of men and women,
and they destroy all diseases.
Eating, drinking and sipping,
licking and chewing,
nasal medicine, anointing the head,
purging and rubbing:
these, eating, drinking and the rest,
are the yogin's supreme applications.
Thus combine the sixty-four
substances with the eighteen.
The gross and subtle preparations,
for men and also for women,
are divided into small and so on.
Wind, bile, phlegm and the rest
are each held to be threefold again.
Black, white and pale blue
apply to flowing and likewise to emission.
Enemy, friend and neutral:
this is the condensed classification of the elements.
One hero and the twice-born roots two,
the great fruits, the astringents three,
sulphur and mercury four,
the essences of nectar five,
the seasons six, and likewise sixty,
the seven with branches, leaves and so on,
dry land and moist regions.
Butter, black butter and purgatives
are given as food to the caustic and to mercury.
Sour gruel and turmeric
in equal parts are the fruit of mercury.
Having heated it in the rays of the sun,
pour in drops of honey.
With alkali and salt, prepare it in the opening.
And with the astringent tastes,
give a paste of white sulphur.
Making mercury one sixth part is best.
Its generative power destroys old age.
Measured portions are what bind it,
and through the fruit the work is done.
First, pouring in gold leaf
is the rite that destroys its own faults.
Then water and salt,
and ash or powder, should be poured in.
If it is poured in warm, white hair appears.
Taking the essence of the elephant's head,
cook it to the measure of the lion.
Then, taking it out once more,
pour in the juice of the arka tree.
By the luster of what is prepared, ailments of heart and eye are overcome.
Candra and rasanā,
garlic and kersa,
the bones of atrapati,
and likewise take flowers:
vyāghrī and kunduru,
sārivā and nails,
moonstone, nāgakesara,
stream of drops and kadamba,
campaka, riso,
śiśu, bilva, utpala,
together with sour gruel:
color the flowing one and mercury.
When taken out it becomes like the form of a snake.
Pour in blood, the maddener and the night tree:
this destroys the fault of white hair
and removes old age and leprosy.
When one washes and anoints with caustic alkali,
it destroys hemorrhoids.
The work with fresh substances born of the belly,
essence and rasāyana, butter, fat and mantra:
filter it also with śirīṣa grass,
and make hair grow on the head.
Red-colored mercury revives what has swooned.
Once revived and washed, it is the path to the fruit.
If it goes beyond measure, it turns to poison.
Then with skin and bark,
flowers and fruits and the like,
with bitter, astringent and sweet,
salty, sour and so on,
cook each in its particular way.
It should be cooked in a lidless pot.
Burning it with the lid on
brings ruin and harm.
Lidless, and shaped like a skull cup,
the bamboo and the crow's beak:
these are the zhiga pots for cooking.
The four measures of cooking are:
sinking, seething, scattering,
and fourth, fleeing into water.
When it becomes overcooked,
there is trembling, dullness and smoke,
and it gives out a bubbling sound.
Squeezing, kneading and stirring,
and likewise pounding;
cooking in honey and cooking in molasses,
the elephant cooking and the lion cooking:
make it caustic and then cook it.
By kneading, the dust of impurity is scattered.
With an unconfused mind,
knead it step by step.
Restoring it in measured portions
comes only after purification, not otherwise.
Then, with the other portions,
the powder of girikarṇī,
setting up the path that reverses the usual course,
cook it according to the measured rite.
Turuṣka and bālika,
the seed of cakramarda,
bhallātaka and the fruit of śigru,
the flowers of pārijāta,
utpala and mallikā seed,
bardar bones and kasi,
sirisaku seed,
honey, poson, palāśa,
and cooked zhiga-jeu:
pour these in to form a paste.
Having understood these,
by the device of mental reflection,
draw out the essence of the maiden and pour it in.
By this one should accomplish it fully,
and never in any other way.

One should examine one maiden, two,
and so on up to many.
Lhangtser, mercury and the great tree:
then the fruit will ripen.
By the path of every action
one becomes both the doer and the eater.
As the gods churned the ocean,
up to twenty-five portions,
the Powerful One, once seized, becomes stable,
endowed with the measure of miraculous power.
Mercury like a peacock's neck,
in the form of a snake, in the form of a frog,
and in the shape of a cloud: these are the four kinds.
The scent of earth and a sweet scent,
likewise the scent of liquor and of what is not liquor;
two portions and three portions.
The woman appears with faded complexion,
of moderate or tall stature,
rough-limbed, passionate, long-necked,
her eyes red like copper:
the Powerful One is dark blue and without breasts,
her feet with the features described before,
and she has a scent like honey.
She produces the qualities of the practitioner's faculties.
These are purified by method:
rough, supple and firm of limb,
she is called the Powerful One.
What arises from embracing them
is the substance to be fully applied.
Relying jointly
on the sharp substance and on lhangtser,
by pouring in lead it is destroyed.
Its taste becomes bitter and astringent.
Then, within sour gruel,
if it is cooked with urine, its faults are cleansed.
Then, if it is kneaded together with urine salt,
pig's grease, fat and blood, its faults are also cleansed.
Again, with cūta and jāti,
salt and the red substance itself,
piñjara and campaka,
and datura in equal portions, one portion of mercury,
and honey-water equal to that:
if it is kneaded, its faults are exhausted.
Then, in a buffalo's hoof
together with rainwater,
pour in copper powder, bell-metal powder
and collyrium powder, and cook it.
When it is cooked, place it in honey;
by this it becomes bound.
Then, if it is purified
with seven portions of blood, it becomes oily and potent;
again, by kneading it with an equal portion of blood
it becomes oily and coarse.
Brass, the wood of the cūta,
copper, turmeric and the rest, six in all:
by adding these, its faults are eliminated.
Then candra wood,
dripping honey and nakha,
alkali and the powder of worm-eaten wood,
mahāhata and mokṣa,
puṣkara and bartaki,
kuba and sindubari powder:
put these together into a human long bone.
Leave it a little while, take it out,
and knead it in a stone mortar.
Having poured it into a bamboo tube,
cooking pots shaped like a mare's mouth,
shaped like the bill of the karanada bird,
like a donkey's face and a camel's face:
these wide-mouthed vessels
are praised for binding and for eliminating.
Any apparatus with a wide mouth,
shaped like a clay drum,
shaped like a bamboo tube and the like,
like the sheath of a sword,
or like the neck of a heron,
is praised for eliminating faults.
Further, for binding mercury,
follow those explanations.
When a quarter remains, it is the great cooking;
when half remains, it is the cooking of great strength.
The impurities that cling to mercury
should likewise be treated:
purifying and ascertaining,
stupefying, and likewise killing.
Then, together with the dust and powder of lhangtser,
pour it into a juice.
The powder called bandu
is to be drunk in buttermilk.
Lhangtser, mercury and the sharp substance
are each to be bound according to the rite.
Then let it rest a little while.
Place it at the hearth,
or set it against a tree at the water's edge,
or in dung, or in a heap of rice,
or inside stones,
or in a shed of cattle, buffalo and the like;
hide it in a heap of sand.
Then, after the rest, take it out,
and examine its form, sound, smell, taste and the rest,
its measures and its signs, just as they are.
Of some, the fruit; of some, the root;
of some, the flowers and leaves,
the bark, or even the bone:
the yogin should look to
enjoyments of many different kinds.
If one obtains requisites, prepared or unprepared,
one becomes like the Vajra Holder.
But if the Powerful One is devoid of essence,
various faults arise as before.
Mercury and lhangtser
of mixed color are faulty:
they produce blood disorders and bile.
The red also produces blood disorders.
The white produces stabbing pains
and destroys life.
The greenish yellow is unripe,
and the pale yellow blocks the channels;
a double portion binds severely.
Since the faults number a hundred,
the wise should eat it only after examining it.
The blue and the black are of great potency.
If the elixir is examined in this way,
it has the qualities of the great lotus.
One olse of mercury,
with twice that amount of what is born from the ocean,
and with four times that amount of flowers,
is the supreme nasal medicine and electuary.
Blood and mercury are to be increased
gradually up to tenfold and eaten;
beyond that there is no result.
Mercury stupefied by tempering,
or better still, even killed,
together with the flower taught before
and with the mind of awakening, is to be eaten.
In every case it is eaten in the morning.
This is the sequence of the rite.
Lhangtser water and mercury
are to be licked in the evening.
For the moon, the path of the moon;
for the sun, the sun:
one should give it from each accordingly.
Thus the places, the times
and the substances, taught extensively,
increase mental acuity and the like.

Vajraḍāka, the supreme of bliss,
in union with all the ḍākas,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One (the Buddha), the Vajra Holder, has spoken this.

From the great king of tantras, the glorious Vajraḍāka, the seventeenth chapter, setting out the sequence of the rite of taking the essence (rasāyana).

Next, what is to be correctly explained,
hidden in the tantra, must be ascertained.
The rite of the correct summary
I shall explain; listen, practitioners.
The abodes of the supreme Cakrasaṃvara and the rest
are all pervaded by ḍākinīs.
Self-born, endowed with wisdom, they dwell,
manifesting in land after land.
She is called a ḍākinī,
the chief lady of the vajra maṇḍala.
The six yoginīs are explained to the practitioner
in the symbolic language.
In the lands of Kulutā and Maru
dwell the mātṛkās;
in Sindhu and Nagara
are the chief ladies of the families.

Those who dwell in Lampāka and Saurāṣṭra
are the goddesses of the family.
Those in the Snowy Mountains and in Kāñci
are called the women with children.
In the lands of Vāñcala
and in Gṛhadevatā
dwell the coemergent maidens.
In Kaliṅga and Kosala
are the vow-holding women who eat the great flesh.
In the place called Pretapuri
and in Triśakuni dwells
the Stout Sovereign called Khaṇḍarohā.
In Pullīra and Jālandhara
dwell the caṇḍālī women.
In the lands of Oḍra and Kāmarūpa
are the great maidens,
and the maidens of the place called Devīkoṭa
and of Rāmeśvarī are held to be so as well.
In Godāvarī and Arbuda
are the supreme sovereigns of the ḍākinīs.
As was taught for Suvarṇadvīpa,
so it is for Oḍḍiyāna too.
The maidens of these lands
pervade the heroes, nondual;
all of them take whatever form they wish,
and they enter by the force of the mind.

Then the goddess again made offerings to the Blessed One, bowed, and said this:

The distinctions among the ḍākinīs,
the names of the field protectors, the greatly terrifying ones,
the names of the fields,
and the distinctions among the abodes,
hearing which the practitioner
will without doubt attain success:
Lord, please explain to me
all of these in brief.

Then the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata, said:
The great secret, so very hard to find,
which I have told to no one,
and which you wish to ask about:
excellent, excellent, great goddess,
you who move of yourself as Mahāmāyā!
I shall explain; listen with one-pointed mind.

Dwelling in the land of Aṭṭahāsa
is the one renowned as Peaceful Face,
born from the source of the Royal Goddess.
She holds the vajra as her weapon.
The goddess who dwells in that land
abides at a kadamba tree.
The kṣetrapāla, the field protector, called Great Bell
is mighty.

In Kolagiri is the Great Glorious One,
born from a fierce source,
a goddess of fierce form,
changing and terrifying.
Dwelling in that city, the violent one
abides on a mountain peak.
The kṣetrapāla called Fire Face
has a beautiful face.

In Darana is Śaṅkarī,
born from the source of the great Ucchuṣma.
The goddess holds the mace as her weapon,
she who makes requests of the Lord of Dharma.
She who is renowned as dwelling in that field
abides at a tāla tree.
The kṣetrapāla called Upward-Blazing Hair
is greatly fierce.

In Devīkoṭa is Great Ears,
born from the source of Mahābala.
The goddess bears a spear in her hand,
supreme among all lords of yoga.
In that place the goddess Great Fierce One
abides at a bhadra tree.
The kṣetrapāla of huge body
is called Moving Supreme Lord.

In Viraja is the Grandmother;
she holds the banner as her emblem,
a great goddess of peaceful form
who bestows mastery over desires.
She who dwells in that field, Blazing Fire,
abides at a mango tree.
With great laughter, "ha ha," a mighty sound,
she terrifies all sentient beings.

In Eruda is Jvālāmukhī,
born from the source of the Auspicious Skull.
The goddess holds the vajra spear;
she dwells in that field greatly intoxicated.
The goddess who dwells in that city
abides at a golden tree.
The kṣetrapāla called Bell Sound
is a great hero.

In Pretapuri is the Tawny Red One,
with eyes of great terror.
The goddess holds the pestle as her weapon;
she is born from the source of the Solitary One.
The goddess who dwells in that field
abides at a jāti tree.
The field protector called Great Shank
is exceedingly fierce and terrifying.

In Elapuri is the Donkey Rider,
born from an exceedingly wrathful source.
With a noose in her hand, the greatly terrifying one
dwells truly intoxicated in that field.
The goddess who dwells in that region,
of great strength, she who overcomes others,
is born from the great and supreme field-born one
called Elephant Ear.

In the land of Kashmir is Cow Ear,
born from a wrathful source.
Bearing a vajra chain,
she strives supremely to crush enemies.
The goddess who dwells in that place
abides on a mountain peak.
The kṣetrapāla called Hollow Shank
is greatly terrifying.

In the land of Maru is the Ordered One,
born from the source of the hero Terrifying Face.
The goddess who bears hook and noose
dwells in that field greatly intoxicated.
The goddess who dwells in that land
abides at the Great Cleft.
The field protector called Fierce
is vast, as one who bewilders.

In Nagara is She of the Plough,
born from the womb of Ucchuṣma's limb.
Great Radiance, attended by vetālas,
grants accomplishment to all the heroes.
That excellent lady who dwells in that place
dwells in a grove of cane.
The kṣetrapāla called Hair-Given
is fierce and mighty.

In the land of Puṇḍravardhana
is the one called Cāmuṇḍī,
born from the womb of Mahābala.
Her hand is adorned with a khaṭvāṅga.
The goddess grants enjoyment and liberation
and exhausts every sin without remainder.
The kṣetrapāla called Vase
truly dwells in that field.

In Jālandhara is Bright-Faced,
born from the womb of the family of passion.
Holding a vajra chain,
she bestows supreme bliss on all.
The goddess dwelling in that supreme city
dwells in a many-storied house.
The kṣetrapāla called Trijaṭa
is exceedingly fierce and mighty.

In Pretapuri is Lightning-Face,
truly born from the womb of the fierce one.
The goddess holds a staff and a spear,
she who fulfills every wish.
The goddess dwelling in that field,
of wrathful form, strikes terror.
The kṣetrapāla called Bell-Sound
is not to be scorned:
owl-faced and terrifying.

In Soba is Fire-Tongue-Face.
Her hand is adorned with a copper knife.
She sits upon a seat of great flesh
and fully bestows the sovereignty of bliss.
The great goddess dwelling in that field
dwells on a śālmali tree.
The kṣetrapāla called All-Acts-Beautiful
is greatly terrifying.

In the land of Cāriṭra,
at the place called Karañja,
is one born from the womb of the fierce Śakti.
She holds the mudrā of the spear.
The kṣetrapāla called Great Bell is mighty,
his hair standing upright, entwined with snakes;
he terrifies all the wicked.

In Oḍiyāna the great goddess
is born from the womb called Secret.
The goddess holds a vajra chain,
her form exceedingly fierce and clear.
The one called Great Sound, of wrathful form,
is a mighty kṣetrapāla.
The goddess dwelling in that supreme place
dwells on an aśoka tree.

In Jālandhara Caṇḍālī
is born from the womb of the peaceful one.
She brandishes a copper knife in her hand
and bestows every kind of sovereignty.
The great fierce one dwelling in that place
dwells on a golden tree.
The great hero called Generator
terrifies all the māras.

In Kṣereker is the Mother of Beings,
fierce and blue, Mahābalā,
the great goddess with a sword in her hand,
she who bestows every accomplishment.
The great goddess, her upright hair ablaze,
dwells on a sāla tree.
The kṣetrapāla called Great Meru
dwells in that field.

In Māyāpurī is the Desirous One,
born from the womb of Terrifying-Face.
She holds the auspicious vajra spear
and grants strength to practitioners.
Blue in form and greatly terrifying,
she dwells on the bhūta tree.
The kṣetrapāla dwelling there, called Terror,
is mighty.

In Putanara is Amrake,
terrifier of all enemies,
holding a plough and a club,
she who brings all beings under her power.
She dwells on a deodar tree
and terrifies all beings.
The kṣetrapāla called Great Observance
dwells in that field.

In Rājagṛha is Janasa,
born from the womb of Great Blood.
Well endowed with a vajra hook,
she brings victory over foes in battle.
The goddess dwelling in that field
utters the sound "jila" and strikes terror.
The kṣetrapāla called Great Ear
always dwells close beside her.

In the land of Tibet is the Coemergent One,
born from the womb of the self-arisen.
She holds a makara banner in her hand,
her form peaceful and clear.
The goddess dwelling in that land
dwells in a house of rock.
The great hero called Enjoyment
is a kṣetrapāla hard to overcome.

In Mālava is the Empowering One,
holding the mudrā of the hammer.
Ever loving toward the practitioner,
she grants fame, glory and good fortune.
The goddess dwelling in that field
dwells on a sweet tree.
There is the kṣetrapāla called Lord of Men,
who has powers of many kinds.

The daughters and the heroes
of these fields grant accomplishments.
Always at night they roam about,
and always at night they gather.
They will grant the vast accomplishment
of sky-going, so hard to find,
and all the practitioner's actions
will be accomplished, without doubt.

Next I shall explain the rite of worship
that grants every accomplishment.
With flowers and incense likewise,
with lamps and choice fragrances,
and with vessels of torma, make offering:
fresh meat, fish and the like,
every kind of food,
and likewise the supreme five nectars.
On the left side are the vessels;
on the right the wrathful one should be displayed.

Oṃ! You who dwell at a lone tree and in charnel grounds,
on mountains, in mountain ravines and caves,
in villages, at crossroads and in fields,
and especially in empty houses,
in the dwellings of beings and on open plains,
and especially in the waters;
Kṛṣṇa, Raudra and Mahāraudra,
those attended together with Devadatta,
the Black Fierce One and Bhairava,
the One Beyond Old Age, and the vināyakas;
the Old Hag and Raudrī,
Bhairavī and Umā,
Jayā and Vijayā,
Ajitā and Aparājitā,
Kālī, Bhadrā and Mahākālī,
Thick Black One and Yoginī,
Indrāṇī and Candrī,
Fierce Hatred and Lambā,
Hārītī and Dīpā,
the Sucker, the village-dwelling yoginī,
Raudrarūpā and Mahārūpā,
Fierce Fangs and Mahākapālā,
Skull-Garland, Garland and Khaṭvāṅgī,
Great Serpent, she with sword and axe,
and likewise she of the vajra and the bow;
the five ḍākinīs of the great reality,
who carry out every action;
the great queen of the maṇḍala of union,
and likewise the lord, Vajreśvara,
the great body of the Tathāgatas,
she who brings forth stainless yoga:
by this command of Vajreśvarī,
all of you, from every side, come here!

oṃ kakkadhyanabapabandhana khakha khādana sarbba duṣṭān hana hana hana ghātaya ghātaya amukasya kāryaṃ kuru hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ jaḥ svāhā

Filling the mouth with nectar,
one should satisfy all the deities.
Together with the yogins and yoginīs,
one should meditate that all actions are accomplished.

oṃ bakṣa bakṣa sarba yogeśvari hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ jaḥ svāhā

With these and the like, according to the application,
the rite of torma should be employed:
for pacifying, increasing, subjugating and summoning,
for killing, for sowing enmity and for binding,
the practitioner, by the very essence of the torma,

should accomplish all the rites,
and by means of the particular wisdom hook
should satisfy the deity of his choice.

In fields, in sacred places, and likewise in villages,
in towns, and on the summits of mountains,
where all the bhūtas have gathered as a host,
when they assemble they grant the supreme to the practitioner.

Dwelling always in the sacred places and the rest,
to the practitioner endowed with devotion
the goddesses grant the supreme;
of this the practitioner has no doubt.

I shall explain the corpse practice
by which the practitioner attains accomplishment.
The corpse should not be old, should have no wounds,
and be free of leprosy, scabies and the like.
With mind deeply absorbed,
one should practice on the day of the bhūtas.
It should be washed with the special five nectars
and with divine water,
anointed with the great oil,
and fumigated with the great incense.
Having made a square maṇḍala,
in its center one should carefully make a triangle.
"All buddhas, listen to me!"
This and the rest should be recited as before.
ha hā hi hī hūṃ hūṃ he he:
this is called the eightfold laughter,
and the practitioner should perform it according to the rite.
The seed that abides in fire,
opened by the seed of wind,
should be placed at the crown of the head,
and worship should be made upon the ground.
Dwelling in the midst of the yoginīs,
one should gaze swiftly and with effort.
The practitioner should contemplate himself,
gazing in sequence at the center of the place of birth,
an ocean of blazing garlands,
and gazing at the messengers together with their light.

Thus spoke Vajraḍāka, supreme bliss,
in union with all the ḍākas,
Vajrasattva, the Thus-Gone One,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder.

From the great king of tantras, the glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the eighteenth chapter, the section on the determination of the places of gathering and on the sequence of the rites of practice.

Next I shall rightly explain
the virtuous maṇḍala of the mātṛkās,
the practice of all the gods,
and likewise the particulars of the messengers,
the great knowledge of bhūtas and grahas,
and others such as the pūtanas,
and likewise the garlands of mantras.
Now I shall explain them to you; listen!

One should draw a delightful maṇḍala,
square, with four gates,
furnished with gate porticoes,
the abode of the qualities of every siddhi,
richly adorned with the five sense pleasures,
and fully endowed with the five offerings.
On cloth that has covered a corpse,
or on cloth that has been struck by weapons,
on cloth from a childbirth,
or on cowhide and the like,
the supreme practitioner should have it drawn
by a painter who keeps the samaya.
On the eighth and the fourteenth days
one should practice the maṇḍala of the bhūtas.
Then, by reciting one hundred thousand times,
they will come to stand directly before one.

Brāhmī and Śivā,
Kaumārī and Vārāhī,
Indrāṇī and Cāmuṇḍī,
Gaṇeśvarī and Āgneyī:
these belong to the second tier.
Indra, Yama, Varuṇa,
Kubera and the lord of the pretas,
Agni, Nairṛti, and the god of the wind:
these belong to the third tier.
The Sun and the Moon,
Mars and Mercury,
Jupiter and Venus,
Saturn and Rāhu:
these belong to the fourth tier.
Brahmā, Viṣṇu, and likewise
Rudra and Skanda are at the four gates.
Earth, water, fire and wind,
the elements of the great elements,
the Ḍākinī and the Lamp Goddess,
and the chief together with the Sucking One and Hāriṇī:
the forms that arise even from the bhūtas
the practitioner should accomplish according to the rite.

By the method of the great maṇḍala
one should draw the outer maṇḍala.
In the center, one should encircle it
all around with a beautiful round line.
Having drawn an eight-petalled lotus,
in the center of the anthers, the domain of secret activity,
one should draw the great wrathful one,
with one face and four arms,
the fangs of his mouth slightly bared,
his face wrathful and laughing,
his body blue, greatly fierce,
adorned with a garland of skulls,
riding a preta, seated on a sun,
terrifying the Thirty-Three gods,
dancing, wearing a tiger skin,
his form with three exceedingly clear eyes.
A blazing vajra, a triple banner,
together with a plowshare brimming with blood,
and likewise arrow and bow
he brandishes in his right and left hands.

In the east one should draw the Ḍākinī,
in the southern direction Hāriṇī,
in the west one should draw the goddess, the Sucking One,
and in the north the goddess, the Blazing One.

The goddess called Vārāhī
has four arms and arises from the syllable hrīḥ.
Her body is blue, she is greatly fierce,
sow-faced and terrifying.
She holds an arrow and likewise a bow,
a victory banner and a skull cup.

Śivā arises from pheṃ,
red in color and of great splendor,
with a skull cup and a hook,
and likewise a khaṭvāṅga staff and a ḍamaru drum.

Kaumārī arises from haṃ,
yellow in color, with four arms,
holding a trident and a triple banner,
and likewise an arrow and a bow.

Cāmuṇḍī arises from ho,
blue in color, with four arms,
holding a khaṭvāṅga and a skull cup,
a plowshare and likewise a ḍamaru.

Brāhmī, who arises from baṃ,
is white in color, with four arms,
holding a lotus and a skull cup,
and likewise in the others an arrow and a bow.

Gaṇeśvarī arises from gaṃ,
pale blue in color, with four arms,
holding a trident and a skull cup,
and a rosary in her left hand.

Vaiṣṇavī arises from bam,
blue in color, with four arms,
holding a skull cup of liquor and one of water,
a noose, and likewise a khaṭvāṅga in the other.

Indrāṇī arises from iṃ,
yellow in color, with four arms,
holding a vajra spear and a skull cup,
the threatening gesture, and a noose.

All of them have blazing hair standing on end,
and are adorned with various ornaments;
they are seated on corpses, have three eyes,
and their waists are wrapped in tiger skins.

At the eastern gate one should draw Brahmā,
white in color, with four arms,
holding a skull cup and a rosary,
a water flask and a lotus.
In the north, likewise, Viṣṇu,
blue in color, with four arms,
holding a spear and likewise a discus,
a skull cup and a supreme staff.
In the south one should draw Rudra,
smoke-colored, with four arms,
holding a trident and a skull cup,
a noose and a rosary.
In the west one should draw Skanda,
yellow in color, with four arms,
holding a spear and a staff,
a hero's skull cup and a rosary.

Īśāna rides on a bull,
white in color, with four arms;
he holds a skull cup and a trident,
and his other two hands are joined palm to palm.
Indra rides an elephant,
yellow in color, with four arms;
he holds a vajra and a skull cup,
and his other two hands are joined palm to palm.
Agni rides on a ram,
red in color, with four arms;
he holds a fire hearth and a skull cup,
and his other two hands are joined palm to palm.

Yama rides upon a buffalo seat,
Blue in color, with four arms;
He holds a staff and a skull cup,
And the other two, in fear, are joined palm to palm.
Nairṛti sits upon a corpse seat,
Green in color, with four arms;
He holds a khaṭvāṅga and a skull cup,
And the other two, in fear, are joined palm to palm.
Varuṇa sits upon a makara seat,
White in color, with four arms;
He holds a noose and a skull cup,
And the other two, in fear, are joined palm to palm.
Vāyu, the wind, sits upon a deer seat,
Smoke-colored, with four arms;
He holds a banner and a skull cup,
And the other two likewise are joined palm to palm.
Kubera dwells upon a seat of treasure,
Yellow in color, with four arms;
He holds a jewel and a hammer,
And the other two likewise are joined palm to palm.
All the planets and the stars as well
Arise each from their own sacred commitment;
Endowed with miraculous power and great strength,
Imagine them abiding on their own seats.

The mātṛkās too, the mothers of all,
And likewise the other spells of the bhūtas:
Next is explained the sādhana of the messengers
That accomplishes all the rites,
Each subdued by force.
Listen, O greatly fortunate goddess!
The practitioner who delights in enjoyments
Should undertake the rite of the messengers.
By the rite of the moon-vajra,
Having meditated on Vajrasattva,
Through urging on the king of mantras,
All will act like servants.

In a charnel ground or at a solitary tree,
There one should begin the maṇḍala.
In the east, the north, the west,
And likewise in the southern quarter,
Having made six in each,
One should then begin the offering.
The twenty-four sacred places and the rest
Should be encircled in due order
With perfume and flowers, and likewise
With lamps and scented water;
One should adorn it with the cloths of vases
Filled with that water.
Having remained one full day and night,
One should then remain one night more.
Having duly paid homage to the sacred places and the rest,
One should then ask for forbearance.
With a feast of heroes
One should first please the master.
The mantrin who has done this
Should afterward undertake the rite.
Otherwise there will be untimely death,
And one will surely burn in hell;
Harassed by obstructors,
One will surely fall into hell.

A painter endowed with the sacred commitments,
On cloth obtained on the day of the bhūtas,
Such as a birth cloth,
Having mixed the paint with blood
And placed it inside a horn,
Should draw the maṇḍala of accomplishment.
One should fumigate it with preta nail-resin,
With great fat and with great flesh.
Having anointed it with perfume,
One should likewise offer preta flowers.
By this offering to the great goddess
The Blazing Lady is supremely pleased.
At the foot of a solitary tree
One should recite a hundred thousand times according to the rite.
At times such as Jayā,
The master should have a painting made,
Twelve finger-widths in size:
Of lovely form, youthful and perfect,
Adorned with every ornament,
She is drawn wearing yellow garments,
Her two hands raised, joined palm to palm,
Blazing with fire, radiant all around.
Having spread it out before the practitioner,
One should diligently worship it according to the rite.
With red flowers, perfumes and the like,
One should fumigate with dried meat.
Meat, fish, liquor and the like
Should be given as a torma of enjoyment offerings.
Having worshipped with the five substances,
If one recites a hundred thousand times,
At the first a sound arises,
At the second the smell of fish is perceived,
At the third the tree will shake,
And at the fourth she stands before one.
Having displayed the samaya mudrā,
One should recite the mantra to her.
Joining the two hands palm to palm
And placing them on the crown, one should say:
oṃ debeśvari hūṃ phuḥ phuḥ phuḥ
This is the samaya mantra and mudrā.

Samaya water filled
With the five nectars should be given.
Having diligently given the samaya,
One should hold her with the left hand:
"Nondual with the yoginī,
Bless me and accomplish me!"
Then the most beautiful lady
Will give a feast of food, drink and the like,
And two zho of gold;
One should spend it all on enjoyments.
If the Blazing Lady who delights in desire
Has not been accomplished,
Oppressing her with the seed of the Fierce Lady,
One should recite a hundred thousand times.
Merely by oppressing her with the seed,
The crown of the Lamp Lady is crushed.
Always striving for the welfare of beings,
One should accomplish the Blazing Lady as one's consort.

Listen, O goddess of great wisdom!
Further, the means of accomplishing the ḍāka:
Having made a painted scroll two cubits long,
Having performed the preliminary consecration and offered torma,
One should then have the image painted:
White and red, exceedingly beautiful,
With one face and two arms,
Wearing exquisitely beautiful garments,
Her hands holding the threatening gesture and a hook,
Splendid with her hair hanging loose,
Her blazing fiery rays extremely bright,
Laughing "ha ha," with sidelong eyes.
Having smeared the ground with blood,
One should spread out the painted image in that place.
With red flowers and the like,
And with choice perfumes and incense,
One should set out the torma of enjoyment offerings.
Meat, fish, liquor and the like
Should be filled with the five nectars.
Meditating on the yoginī, one recites;
With a hundred thousand she will be accomplished.
Joining the two hands palm to palm
And putting them to the mouth, one should laugh.
oṃ ḍākinī hūṃ phuḥ phuḥ
This is the mantra and mudrā of accomplishment.
At the first the earth will shake,
At the second there is laughter before one,
At the third she appears before one,
And at the fourth one receives her command.
Having diligently given the samaya
Of the choice five nectars,
Through accomplishing her as a servant
One should give whatever command one wishes.
Killing and humiliating enemies,
Tormenting their limbs, whatever is wished:
All such rites and the rest
Will be accomplished through the servant's work.
If, after a hundred thousand, a graha devours it,
With the seed of Vajrā,
According to the rite taught before,
The wise one should oppress her and recite.

On the skin of a goat
One should diligently stretch out the painted scroll,
And have the Sucking Lady painted,
Red in color, exceedingly beautiful,
Her body adorned with ornaments;
She is drawn wearing beautiful garments,
The left hand with the threatening gesture and a noose,
And in the right an axe should be placed.
At a pleasing crossroads
The wise one should practice.
Having smeared it with cow dung,
One should scatter various flowers.
With flowers, incense, lamps and the like,
One should give torma to all the bhūtas.
Blood, fish, meat and liquor,
And various foods,
Filled with the five nectars, should be given.
Diligently abiding in meditative concentration, one recites:
oṃ ākarṣaya phuḥ phaṭ
Holding the two hands level,
One should move in dance in the manner of a noose.
This is the heart mantra and mudrā.

When the yogin recites a hundred thousand times,
the Sucking One is accomplished.
First comes the sound of a dog howling.
At the second, she laughs out loud.
At the third, bearing weapons,
she comes in order to threaten.
At the fourth, standing before one,
she says, "What shall I do? Give your command."
Having given her the samaya substances
of the special five nectars, say:
"For the benefit of sentient beings,
I, a poor man, have accomplished you."
Then, day after day,
she will give half a measure of gold.
If she is not accomplished by a hundred thousand,
torment her with the vetāla heart mantra as you recite.
Merely by tormenting her with this seed syllable,
the Sucking One's head is crushed.

On ground at the edge of the water,
one should accomplish Kambojī.
Stretching out the hide of an ox or the like,
have the Ravisher drawn upon it.
Her body is adorned with snakes,
blue in color, with blazing light,
beautified with loosened hair,
clad in red, lovely with her two arms,
holding a sword and a noose,
laughing "ha ha," with bared fangs.
Having smeared a maṇḍala with blood,
offer scents and flowers and fumigate with incense.
Give a torma as a close offering
of meat, fish, onions and the like.
Endowed with the five nectars,
give the deity's torma as before.
The yogin meditates and recites;
by one hundred thousand she is accomplished.
First, the water stirs.
At the second, a great rain falls.
At the third, lightning strikes.
At the fourth, she stands before one.
Then, with effort, give her the samaya
of the special five nectars.
oṃ kriṃ kruṃ phuṃ phuḥ phaṭ
Binding both hands into fists,
one should utter her mantra.
This is the heart mantra and the mudrā.
Standing before one with palms joined,
she will say, "What shall I do?"
"Out of love, take me
to the summit of the snows of Mount Kailāsa,
and come back here again.
A king's daughter or anyone else,
whomever I desire, bring her here.
Gold, silver, copper and the like,
food, clothing and drink,
bring them just as I wish."
Whether a goddess or a human woman,
she leads her, just as one wishes,
to the summit of the snows of Mount Kailāsa.
If she is not accomplished by the rite taught,
one should accomplish her by another rite.
The wise one, having meditated
on himself as the gnosis ḍāka,
presses her down with his left foot
and, if he recites five hundred times,
she will be accomplished without doubt.
If she is not accomplished even by that,
summon her into the midst of the heroes
and chop her into pieces.
By meditating on the syllable kṛ, on killing,
and on the seed hūṃ,
imagine that all of them devour her.
Through that, the yoginī to be accomplished,
even if she is the mistress of all,
will die if she is not accomplished.
If she is not accomplished by the rite taught,
in the middle of a kindled fire
of black balataka wood,
offer white mustard seed mixed with blood
at midday, uttering hūṃ.
Having performed the fire offering,
the ḍākinīs will die,
so she will be accomplished without doubt.

Next, the means of accomplishing Śaṅkara:
listen, greatly fortunate goddess!
Accomplish him in a charnel ground, at a solitary tree,
at a riverbank, or at a crossroads.
A painter who keeps the samaya
should draw a colored maṇḍala.
Fourteen finger-widths in measure,
it should be drawn on a hero's cloth.
A triangular maṇḍala is auspicious;
make the maṇḍala round in form.
His body is blue, he rides a bull,
his body is smeared with ash,
his phallus erect, his form peaceful,
with a crown of matted locks and a spear.
He has four faces and four arms,
adorned with a garland of skulls,
with a khaṭvāṅga and a skull cup,
and likewise, in the others, a shor.
He should be worshipped with feces and urine;
give a torma for all the bhūtas.
Having summoned him with this mantra,
accomplish him by reciting a hundred thousand times.
oṃ kapālamāla alaṃkṛtaśirse hūṃ hūṃ
āgacchaya āgacchaya rudrāya sāhā
This is the invitation mantra.
oṃ āḥ rudra hūṃ hūṃ svāhā
By reciting it a hundred thousand times, he will be accomplished.
If he is not accomplished,
then he should be burned.
Once accomplished, he will perform
all tasks, and not otherwise.

Next I shall rightly explain
the means of accomplishing Vāsudeva.
On a mountain, on the shore of the ocean,
or where flowing rivers meet,
by the rite taught before,
one should accomplish the supreme maṇḍala.
Twelve finger-widths in measure,
it should be accomplished with special care.
He is black in color, exceedingly beautiful,
with one face and four arms,
holding a wheel and a club,
and likewise a skull cup and a lotus.
He is adorned with a jeweled crown
and with every ornament.
Feces, urine, semen and blood
should be given as offerings,
and if one fumigates with human flesh,
the siddhis will draw near.
oṃ āḥ vajra nārāyaṇa hūṃ hūṃ āḥ svāhā
This is the invitation mantra.
oṃ mama cakra sādhaya sarbba asur daman hūṃ hūṃ svāhā
By reciting it a hundred thousand times,
one gains the power to enter
the gates of all the asuras,
displays every illusion,
is able to subdue all enemies,
and also obtains kingship.

By this very sequence of the rite,
Brahmā too should then be accomplished.
At a secluded spot by the water,
and especially in a lotus grove, accomplish him.
Yellow in color, with four arms,
beautified by four faces,
peaceful, wearing a crown of matted locks,
adorned with the sacred thread,
he holds a lotus and a rosary,
a staff and a skull cup.
The foremost of practitioners,
having focused on the painted image,
should anoint it again and again with urine
and with various delightful fragrances,
saffron, sandalwood and the like.
One who is constantly engaged in meditation
should offer those sequences of worship rites
in the midst of the bhaga.
oṃ brahma āgaccha hūṃ āḥ svāhā
This is the invitation mantra.

oṃ bhurbhubastatvabidurbbareṇu bhargodebasadhimahi dhiyoyonpracodayata
By reciting it a hundred thousand times it is accomplished.
Pacifying and enriching,
summoning and bringing under one's control,
increasing wealth, fierce rites and the rest:
one becomes able to perform all these rites,
and all are accomplished through recitation and fire offering.
This I have explained to you correctly.

Listen, goddess of great fortune,
to the means of accomplishing the King of Obstructors.
In a pleasing house of the mātṛkās,
having performed virtuous meditation,
the wise one, beginning with offerings,
should diligently visualize the painted image.
Red in color, blazing intensely,
he is seated in a dance posture with legs half crossed.
He has one face and three eyes,
and bears topknots of matted hair.
Red rays of light blaze and churn about him;
he should be drawn terrifying, with fangs bared.
An axe and a pestle,
an arrow and likewise a bow,
a khaṭvāṅga and a hook,
a skull cup filled with blood,
likewise a vajra and a sword;
further, he holds a skull of great flesh,
a muṣuṇḍī and a banner, these three,
and a śakti spear: twelve in all.

oṃ a mo lha siddha sarbba artha me prasādhaya hūṃ hūṃ jaḥ jaḥ sāhā
This is the mantra of invocation.
oṃ āḥ g hūṃ svāhā
This is the mantra for recitation.

By a hundred thousand recitations it is accomplished;
after that one should undertake the rites.
If, having recited it once,
the mantra practitioner makes tilaka marks, he becomes fortunate.
Even at the king's gate he will obtain honor,
and he will destroy all his enemies.
That he will drive away every obstructor without exception
and grant the fruit of every desire,
of this there is no doubt.
By that very application of the rite
comes the supreme means of accomplishing the mātṛkās;
by that very sequence of application
the planets too are held to be accomplished.

oṃ sarbba tithi nākṣatra graha maṅgalebhyaḥ svāhā
This is the mantra of all the planets.

Beginning with oṃ
and ending with svāhā, it is called the blessing.

Vajraḍāka, supreme in bliss,
in union with all the ḍākas,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed Vajra Holder, has spoken this.

From the great king of tantras, the glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the nineteenth chapter, the section on the arising of the means of accomplishing all bhūtas and grahas.

Then the goddesses made offerings and bowed to the Blessed One, and addressed him with these words:

"What is the reality of primordial wisdom, its distinctions,
and what are the signs of the body?
On what do the channels depend?
We do not know their divisions,
and we wish to hear this from the Blessed One.
Great Bliss, please explain it."

"Listen, goddess Mahāmāyā,
who conjures all illusions everywhere.
The great channels are thirty-two,
and these are foremost above all others.
Likewise, ten and two
are the characteristic of the shifting of the vowels;
distinguished by the waxing and waning fortnights,
they are declared to be twenty-four.
Time is swift or long.
As for the characteristics of the signs in the limbs,
merely by knowing them
one knows that one has arrived at the time of death.
If the breath becomes agitated,
embodied beings die within six and two.
In the morning, at the time of Rohita,
one should observe its signs.
The places of the nose and eyes,
the cheeks and the roots of the ears,
the junction of tongue and teeth,
likewise the outside of the throat,
the network at the heart, the navel,
the thighs and calves, and likewise the rest,
the urinary passage and the genitals,
the head and the region of the crown:
signs in the body where the breath is cut off again and again
swiftly reveal the time of death.
By the measure of the count of days
one reckons with the wheel the nature of time.

When the breath flows from one door
for six days,
the years numbering the three qualities,
divided into three hundred and sixty days,
their measure shall be shown in brief:
two years and months
shown in the number of the times,
and eighteen days;
this is correctly declared as its measure.
If the wind flows for seven days at the end,
one lives two years
and likewise six months.
If the wind flows for eight days,
likewise two years, months
numbering the Vedas,
and twenty-four days:
this is the number of the time one lives.
If it flows for nine days,
two years, four months,
and days numbering the suns:
this is the span of embodied beings.
If it flows for ten days,
one does not live beyond two years.
If for eleven days
it flows from one door,
one does not live beyond one year, nine months,
and eighteen days; this I tell you, beautiful one.
If it flows for twelve days,
one does not live beyond one year, months
numbering the vowels, and days
numbering the times.
If for thirteen days
it flows through the right-hand path,
one does not live beyond one year, months
numbering the Vedas,
and nine days; this I tell you, lovely-faced one.
If for two weeks
the wind flows through the right-hand path,
one will not live beyond one year, two months,
and half of ten days;
this is declared as his definite lifespan.
If for five times three days
the wind flows from the right-hand door,
within one year, without doubt.
If for twice eight days
the wind flows from the right,
his definite lifespan
I declare: one does not live beyond
ten months and twenty-four days.
If for seventeen days
the wind flows through the right-hand path,
then his lifespan:
one does not live beyond nine months and eighteen days;
this is declared without doubt.
If it flows for eighteen days,
eight months and days
numbering the suns
are declared as his time.
If for nineteen days
the scent-bearer, the wind, flows,
I shall explain how long one lives:
one will not live beyond seven months
and days numbering the times, without doubt.
If it flows for twenty days,
in six months
his life will depart.
If for twenty-one days
the wind flows through the path of the sun,
one does not live beyond five months
and days numbering the suns.

If it flows for twenty-two days,
it is rightly declared that one will live
no longer than months to the number of the Vedas, that is, four,
and twenty-four days.
If for twenty-three days
the wind flows through a single door,
it is declared that one will live no longer than four months
and days to the number of the seasons, that is, six.
If it flows for twenty-four,
one will live no longer than three months
and eleven days.
If it flows for twenty-five days,
one will live no longer than three months.
Beyond that, the higher ones should be known
in brief from the numbers taught before.
From the reckoning of twelve,
the intermediate ones are shown.
Portion by portion, in sequence,
the intermediate days have been made clear.
So that those of lesser understanding may grasp them,
these times have been explained.
For yogins, the times
that are very subtle I now explain.
From a body whose elements are balanced,
examine these and gain conviction.

When it descends through the door facing upward,
if the flesh between the nostrils gives way,
in seven days, without doubt.
The ninth portion truly abides.
Likewise, from the door facing upward,
if the water of the eyes runs dry,
one will die in five days.
The tenth portion is to be known.
From its going facing upward,
from signs on the left ear
and on the side of the cheek,
one lives no longer than a single day.
Of the eleventh portion abiding,
from its clearly going upward,
by resting on the right ear,
one is cut off in eighteen days.
The twelfth portion likewise abides.
If a black thread is seen on the tongue,
the embodied one dies in days to the number of the seasons.
Where the thirteenth portion rests,
likewise blazing upward,
if the upper and lower teeth lock together,
the lord of beings leads him away entirely;
life departs in days to the number of the seasons.
Where the fourteenth vowel rests,
the channel situated at the throat,
through what is known as facing upward,
one lives no longer than five days.
Where the fifteenth portion abides,
in that supreme facing upward,
if the net of the chest collapses,
in half a month, without doubt.
The self complete with sixteen portions,
at the center of the navel, facing upward:
if the blood of the nails is gone,
in ten days one truly lies down.
Again, from the sign at the thigh
where the seventeenth portion abides,
in twenty-four days
one will die; here there is no doubt.
The channel situated at the calf,
where the eighteenth portion abides,
likewise by facing upward,
one dies in days to the number of the sun, that is, twelve.
The nineteenth portion abides;
when the urinary passage likewise faces upward,
in a like number of days
one will die; here there is no doubt.
The channel situated at the male organ,
where the twentieth portion abides,
the supreme lord facing upward:
by this sign one will die, without doubt,
in days to the number of the seasons.

In reckoning the twelve squares of the chart,
the intermediate days that have been shown
are to be worked out by the sequence of the years;
by seventy-two in order,
by thirty-six and by eighteen,
for months and so on in their sequence,
subtract step by step.
Whatever is left as remainder,
when gathered, is the time of living:
know this, great goddess.
Thus spoke the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder,
Vajraḍāka, supreme in bliss,
in union with all the ḍākas,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata.

This is the twentieth chapter, called "The Arising of Breath in Union with the Channels," from the Great King of Tantras, the Glorious Vajraḍāka.

The characteristics of the nine doors,
and where the channels abide,
how their virtues, faults and so on are to be seen:
I do not know these in detail.
I wish to hear, O Lord of Wisdom;
may the Great Bliss please explain.

I shall explain the characteristics of the yoga
of transference upward; goddess, listen.
First one should purify oneself,
and afterward begin the action.
Dwelling in the formless, blue in color,
a great lotus drips like the moon.
Again it dwells in form,
adorned with every aspect.
Fully endowed with four portions,
it hangs with its mouth turned downward.
From the place where the state abides,
it is ever pure, like the kunda flower and the moon.
The nectar of consciousness and wisdom,
great goddess, is the supreme descent.
Whatever lotus has become its support
is adorned with the light of the moon.
That is the form of portion upon portion;
it is said to be "abiding as a sphere."

Then something more is to be explained:
the extremities of the places are to be purified.
Once they are purified, existence is to be transferred;
otherwise it becomes meaningless.
If the body is filled by the filling breath,
it removes poison and the heat of fever.
If the doors are bound by the vase breath,
the tubes of the doors become pure.
By dispersal, the many are made into dust;
by complete pacification, it becomes pacified.
The rite of stealing consciousness,
and going elsewhere too, I shall explain.

Then the wise should purify
the external application.
Having contemplated the two places,
one should meditate on the maṇḍala of wind.
In the middle of the wind, with fire,
one should divide it again and again.
The seed endowed with the vowels and consonants
should be placed there.
The yogin especially
should recite with the outgoing and the incoming breath.
The outgoing breath should be imagined
as striking with the seed of wisdom.
The incoming breath should strike
the consciousness of the heart like a flower.
The yogin, reciting with the outgoing breath,
makes it external from himself.
The incoming breath is to be marked as the outgoing;
meditate on suchness in evenness.
If one meditates on the thing in meditative equipoise,
it will be accomplished; here there is no doubt.

Then, upon an external body,
the practitioner makes an emanation.
Drawing out the swan by the path of the sun,
one makes the goddess endowed with portions.
Having entered the heart of the one to be subdued,
blazing with wind and fire,
one seizes his consciousness
and enters the maṇḍala of the body.
Having gathered the consciousness of the one to be subdued,
one should apply the lower wheel.
For as long as it has not been exhausted,
for so long he remains mute.
When the time of death has arrived,
when the signs of death appear,
before one is struck by fevers and the like,
transference upward is supreme.
Next, entering another's body
I shall explain in brief.
Having bound the mudrā of the Lion's Play,
the yogin should constantly practice the breath and its restraint.

Having joined it to your place,
one wholly abandons one's own body;
having separated by gradual practice,
and by performing the rite in this way,
the mantrin enters another's body.
By that very sequence of practice,
one is held to rejoin one's own body.
Whoever practices this in meditative equipoise,
through the distinctions of going and coming,
honors through this union
all the Lords of Heroes and the ḍākinīs.

That by which one comes to hear from afar
I shall explain correctly; listen!
Likewise the wise should come to know
the intentions of others' minds.
The supreme practice, distinguished
by meditation and recitation, will arise.
If one contemplates by way of this path,
it will be accomplished; of this there is no doubt.
Entering the wheel taught before,
at the two ears,
mingling with the three worlds,
it whirls about like sparks.
If the one endowed with wisdom imagines it entering
with fire stirred by the wind,
in about six months he will certainly
hear what lies far away.
By this practice of meditation
one comes to see with the eye of wisdom;
even what lies very far away
the practitioner sees clearly in an instant.
Whatever has been taught or not taught,
all of it I explain correctly.

Listen, Goddess Mahāmāyā!
Going by the door of virtue,
and the path by whose going
the accomplishment of liberation is granted,
the distinctions of highest and lowest,
I shall explain in brief.
From the navel, the gods of the desire realm;
from the bindu, the heaven of form.
Going from above,
one swiftly goes to what is above.
From the nose, to the abode of the yakṣas;
the ears, the gods who are siddhas.
If consciousness goes from the eyes,
one becomes a lord of men.
From the door of becoming, a hungry ghost;
likewise from the water door, an animal;
by the non-water door one falls into hell.
These go elsewhere than the path of liberation.

First, by undertaking the vase breath,
one should bind all the doors.
One should meditate within the aperture
of the door of the stūpa of the five Victors.
Then at the lower door
imagine the white seed of water.
At the doors of water and non-water
is the blazing seed of fire.
Fully absorbed in that,
cultivate the breath as the vase.
The body has the color of wind;
one should meditate on the wind maṇḍala.
The edge of the wind lies at the root;
the seed of wind is at the root.
The root seed, endowed with bindu and nāda,
should be drawn upward.
Below, by the wind maṇḍala,
one propels it with sound upon sound.
When the time has come, one should transfer;
out of time, one would kill the deities.
Merely by killing the deities
one will surely burn in hell.
Therefore the wise should strive
to know the signs of death.

The great herb taught before by the yogin
is perfect in bliss.
When it is ripe, practitioners
should eat its upper root.
At daybreak, with water,
mixed with the nectar of the left,
if eaten, it pacifies and increases,
and removes poison and the heat of fever.
By this ritual application
chronic and ripened illnesses are destroyed.

Goddess, next, by reversal,
I shall describe going in the sky.
By the distinctions of wind and fire
one applies the wheel of the feet;
having set the mind in perfect calm,
one goes into the sky,
and, surrounded by all the ḍākas,
plays everywhere in a divine body.
For as long as sun and moon remain,
so long one dwells in the sky.

Again, reversing the order,
imagine a lotus white in color.
Placed in the center of the target,
it always brings accomplishment of speech.
One should meditate in the center of one's own body;
like the wind maṇḍala in turmoil,
it comes forth from one's own mouth
and should be dissolved into the target's mouth.
In seven days it becomes accomplishment,
and not otherwise.
Having dissolved by way of the wind
into the body of the target,
one seizes his mind
and draws it into oneself; then, contemplating fire,
by kindling wind and fire
his body is thrown into turmoil.
Know that from the nine joints upward,
by the other way, it transfers at once.

Even one who kills a brahmin every day,
who commits the five deeds of immediate retribution,
who steals, robs, and indulges in desires,
is purified by this path;
he is not stained by evil
and goes far from the faults of existence.
Just as a lotus born from the mud
is stainless and lovely,
so from the mud of the body
the wisdom body goes as it wishes.

The supreme bliss of Vajraḍāka,
in union with all the ḍākas,
was spoken by Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed Vajra Holder.

From the Great King of Tantras, the Glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the twenty-first chapter, setting out the sequence in which arise the meditation of upward transference and the practice of the mudrā of Siṃhavikrīḍita, the Lion's Play.

Next I shall correctly explain
the characteristics of the speech mudrā.
On the right side is the yogin,
on the left the yoginī.
Putting a finger into the mouth
is the sign of a ḍākinī.
gho ghu
Placing the palm on the crown of the head
is the sign of the Blazing One.
gu ghru
Pressing the thumb
is the sign of the Sucking One.
mu ghu
Striking the hand against the ear
is the sign of the Robber of the Wise.
lu ghu
Tapping the tip of the nose with the cupped hand
asks, "Are you well or not?"
rdṃṣṭu
Showing the hand like a deer's head
is the sign of practicing yoga.
gan dho de
Touching the middle between the eyebrows
means, "my beloved friend."
a li ni da ha
Pressing the tongue with the teeth
means, "Let us eat the torma within the gathering."
bha kṣe yo
Grasping the wrist
means, "I went to your house."
dvi ra ti
If one rubs the hand at the heart,
it means, "Now you delight my mind."
ru ca pa ta ba ta
Holding a fist at the tip of the nose
shows, "There is none."
śu nya tā
If one stretches out the hand and strikes the belly,
it means, "There is plenty."
kri kas ha
If one rubs at the navel,
it is wanting to eat food.
canḍā li ka
If one points the forefinger forward,
it means malicious, fierce and violent.
nī la dan ta ba ta
If one touches the eyebrow,
it shows desire here and now.
a naṃ ga u ta
Rolling up the tongue
means, "Dying now."
dur gti
Looking all around
is wishing to know the date.
ta ra ga na
Beginning from the first day of the month,
one should draw lines with the thumb;
the hours, letters and so forth,
one touches in front, as taught before.
gi ri
Looking upward
means, "At midday, playing with joy."

samachaya: looking with a smile,
taking hand in hand,
and making a gesture like the ocean,
this means "Wealth and food are given."
jirnapata: if one holds both hands in fists,
it means "Let us go together now."
salabata: touching the ring finger
with the thumb means "One will not die."
soma: the thumb, the wrathful one, eating, and so on.
sūrya.

Vajraḍāka, supreme in bliss,
in union with all the ḍākas,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder, has spoken this.

From the great king of tantras, the glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the twenty-second chapter, on the rite of play through the sign language of the mudrās of yogins and yoginīs.

The goddess danced once more
and gave delight with this song:
e baṃ sita aruni nabha janite madhyama gama āgama dhama pāsini sā sā sā nigama sā sā sā rigamo bhupati sariri sarirī sā sā sā madhyama adhama sā sā sā śrutanisa sā sā sā sanisaritaghamaghama adhamadhama sā sā sā babamaya babadhanamo. So it goes.

In the east is me. In Yama's quarter, the south, is bre. In the west is mi. In the north is ku. In the fire quarter, the southeast, is dhasi. In Nairṛti's quarter, the southwest, is maka. In the wind quarter, the northwest, is tuku. In Īśāna's quarter, the northeast, is bimo.

ā is the Crooked One (Mars). ī is the one born of the Moon (Mercury). ū belongs to the guru of the gods (Jupiter). e belongs to Venus. o belongs to the son of the Sun (Saturn). aṃ is the lord of the planets, the hare-marked one (the Moon). a is the Sun.

From the rite of the planets,
one should speak of the Moon and the Sun.
The Moon holds sway over Karkaṭa,
the Sun over the five-faced one, the Lion;
the remaining planets, however many,
each occupy two signs.

"You have explained the wheel of high and low.
The distinctions of lunar mansions, times, and days
I do not know.
Great Bliss, please explain them."

He declared:
"Listen as I explain the death
of those of the Ram, Rohita, and the rest;
I shall state it in summary.
The month and the lunar date,
together with the time of the lunar mansion,
likewise the grahas and diseases,
and the measure of high, low, and sign,
I declare for each according to that under which one was born.

On a Bhadrā day
in the first month of winter,
when the planet is Mars
and the lunar mansion is Kṛttikā,
that is his time of death;
no one at all can save him.

On the day of the Bull,
I shall explain how he will die
of signs in the body and the like,
and of a disease of madness and trembling.
Likewise, in the middle month of winter,
when the planet is Mercury and the mansion Rohiṇī,
on the fourth day,
in the evening he will die, without doubt.

In the last month of winter, O great goddess,
when the mansion is Mṛgaśiras
and the planet is Mercury,
whoever is a person of the Twins
will die within six days.

On the eighth day of the first month of spring,
when the mansion is Mṛgaśiras
and the planet Venus is conjoined,
at the time of morning,
one of Karkaṭa will die.

On the tenth of the middle month of spring,
when the mansion is Maghā
and the planet is Saturn,
a person of the Lion will die.

On the twelfth of the last month of spring,
when the mansion is Citrā
and the planet is the Sun,
of tumors and stabbing pains
a person of the Maiden will die.

On the fourteenth of the first month of summer,
when the mansion is Anurādhā
and the planet is Mars,
a person of the Balance will die.

On the eleventh of the middle month of summer,
with Citrā and Mars,
a person of the Scorpion will die.

On the ninth day of the last month of summer,
when the mansion is Śravaṇa,
a person of the Bow will die.
If these are definitely conjoined,
I have declared truly that death will come.

On the seventh of the first month of autumn,
when the mansion is Puṣya
and the planet is the Moon,
a person of the Makara will die.

On the thirteenth of the middle month of autumn,
when the mansion is Aśvinī
and the planet is the Moon,
a person of the Water Jar will perish.

In the last month of autumn, O Mahāmāyā,
on the eighth day,
when the mansion is Revatī
and the planet is Saturn,
a person of the Fish will die.

First one should know the time of birth;
afterward one should calculate the time.
Otherwise, all who pronounce on times
will become utterly meaningless.
With the birth time, in deep meditative equipoise,
one should examine the peaceful and the fierce,
the span of life, the time of death, and so on;
then one foretells accordingly." So it is said.

Vajraḍāka, supreme in bliss,
in union with all the ḍākas,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder, has spoken this.

From the great king of tantras, the glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the twenty-third chapter, concerning the cutting off of certain death through the planets and days.

The auspicious characteristics of omens
I shall fully explain; goddess, listen.
Having set the vowels and consonants side by side,
one should arrange them into groups.
Looking at the first vowel and consonant and so on,
one should take up the name.
One endowed with the portions, possessed of courage:
his family should be known.
The nāgas, beginning with Ananta,
the planets likewise, beginning with the Sun,
and the mātṛkās, beginning with the ḍākinīs,
are renowned as belonging to its family.
All the gods themselves
and all the mantras are born from it.
She who gives birth to all beings
is therefore declared to be called "Nam."

The letter a and likewise ā,
together with ka, ca, ṭa,
ta, pa, ya, and śa:
these are the letters of a group of nine.
i, and again, O goddess, ī,
and likewise the others, kha, cha, ṭha,
tha, pha, ra, and ṣa
are declared to be the second group.
u and, O goddess, likewise ū,
together with ga, ja, ḍa,
da, ba, la, and sa:
these are the third group.
The letter e and likewise ai,
and likewise again gha, jha, ḍha,
and again likewise dha, bha, va, ha:
these are the fourth group.
The letter o and the letter au,
the one called aṃ, together with the visarga,
and again ṇa, ña, ṇa, na, ma:
these nine are the fifth group.
In all there are forty-four,
to be taken together with the five remaining.
These, of all beings…

abide truly in arising and perishing.
The twelve letters, O Mahāmāyā,
express the nature of life and of time.
They are called rāśis, the signs of the zodiac,
and they are joined with all the stars.
By the distinction of moon and sun,
look likewise below and above.
Stability and movement:
these two are said to be twofold,
single and combined,
long and short, defeat and victory.
Of these, the upper is victory;
the lower should be known as death.

The planets, rāśis and stars
that move along the course of the sun:
that station rises in the sun,
and with the rāśi and the planet they are held to be three.
Joined with the movement of the breath,
all should be known as rāśis.
Cancer and the elephant's enemy, the lion,
likewise the maiden and the scales,
endowed with six portions,
rise when impelled by the planets.
The scorpion and the water-born makara,
the bull and the water jar,
having the measure of five portions,
rise when impelled by the planets.
The fish and the ram, and likewise
the bow and the pair joined in union,
endowed with four portions,
rise as before.

Again I shall explain the rising
of the planets together with their omens.
Have the eye that sees results:
listen to how the great omens arise.

Kālī, Karālī, Bhīṣaṇā, She Beyond Old Age, Vināyakī, Caṇḍī, She of Fierce Form, the goddess Durgā, Jayā, Vijayā, Ajitā, Aparājitā, Bhadrakālī, Mahākālī, Sthūlakālī, Indrāṇī, Cāndrī, Raudrī, the Hostile One, the Pendent One, Tridaśeśvarī, the Snatcher, Jvālā, and the Sucker.

The names of these goddesses
and of the twenty-four great omens,
being alike in name, bestow results.
Having doubled it,
joined with the portion of the faculties,
take the remainder as the omen;
thus the omen is to be known.
Kālī and Bhīṣaṇā,
She of Fierce Form and Durgā,
Sthūlakālī and Jayā,
Raudrī and Tridaśeśvarī,
the Snatcher and the Sucker:
these ten are known as harsh.
Taking a bride, auspicious rites,
founding towns and markets,
measuring out a road, and battle,
going out to great places, and the like:
since from those omens there is no result,
virtuous acts should not be done.
In those classes, O Mahāmāyā,
good works should be given up.
If even by virtue one is killed,
what need is there to speak of fierce acts?

The breath that dwells in the body,
and the planets and rāśis outside:
if one knows their movement
both outer and inner, one will succeed.
Whatever is a planet is called a vowel;
whatever is a vowel,
that too should be known as a rāśi.
Like the vowels and the rāśis,
the stars too are to be seen as forms of vowels.
Without their mutual combination
the result will not be accomplished.
When a moving one rises, acts of movement;
under a stable one, things become unmoving.
The guide is guided by the guide;
it is made clear through outer and inner together.

Vajraḍāka, the supreme of bliss,
in union with all the ḍākas,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder, has spoken this.

From the great king of tantras, the glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the twenty-fourth chapter, the analysis of the rite of the movement of the planets and the omens of the groups.

Now listen as I explain
the movement together with the planets and stars.
On the day ruled by the Sun,
in the fifth and the seventh half-watches,
no action whatever will succeed.
For the Moon, the fourth and the sixth;
for Mars, the third and the fifth;
for Mercury, the second and the fourth:
without doubt there will be destruction.
For Jupiter, in the first and the third,
death itself will come about.
For Venus, the second
and the seventh should be known.
For Saturn, the first
and the sixth bring death.
At the rising of these classes,
their times are taught to be cut off.
These are their common characteristics;
yogin, listen to the rest of the explanation.

From five up to twenty-five, these are to be stated;
from that, up to thirteen and three, one should insert one at a time.
In the last month of winter: earth, equal to the sun; three, eye, moon, six;
three, pair, moon; the intercalary month, the lunar day, direction, arrow, quality.
These are explained correctly as the lives of the drops.
From five up to ten, and likewise from ten
up to fifteen, and from there up to twenty,
and from there up to twenty-five, one should reckon.

Then I shall explain the changing states
to be known by the practitioner.
First count the lunar mansion
in which the moon is standing.
However many hours of the sun
have passed, that many,
because the moon's states move on,
should be converted into sixty.
Again, adding the solar time and the moon,
convert it into sixty,
and with arrow, burner and taste,
together with the faculties, divide it likewise.
From among the twelve,
take the remaining portion.
By that remaining portion
one experiences states of happiness and suffering.
By the joining of day and night
it is stated in the remaining hours.
For one placed in the first moon,
his undertaking will fail.
The second is auspicious and fortunate.
The third is death: one will be killed.
The fourth is laughter: one will be satisfied.
The fifth is wealth: one will gain.
The sixth is fever: the body will be destroyed.
In the seventh there is joy:
one will attain satisfaction.
In the eighth the world will be ruined.
For one standing in the ninth moon,
the body will be beset by enemies.
In the tenth one plays in delight
and is victorious through variety.
In the eleventh one is tormented by various fears.
The twelfth is enjoyment:
one is victorious through great bliss.
As shown by the ram and the rest,
the moon moves through them in order.

Vajraḍāka, the supreme of bliss,
in union with all the ḍākas,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder, has spoken this.

From the great king of tantras, the glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the twenty-fifth chapter, the analysis of all movements.

Then the goddess, having worshiped the Blessed One and bowed to him, said this:

"The wheel of highs and lows that you have explained:
what are its divisions like?
And tell me the reckoning of time just as it is.
About the distinctions of their movements
a doubt has arisen in me.
Great Bliss, please explain."

"You who sport through every illusion,
listen, goddess Mahāmāyā!
The reckoning of time has no body;
it abides as all things in all things.
It pervades every entity;
though it dwells in the body, it produces no body.
Time itself is without time,
and the world itself is not the world.
It is neither true nor false."

"O god, the inconceivable defining mark of time
that you explained before,
that which you have explained,
the ultimate, so very hard to find,
beyond the body and stainless:
how does a yogin realize it?
For the benefit of all people,
please explain the regulation of the breath."

The Blessed One (the Buddha) declared:
"Take a person free of illness and free of wind disorders,
