whose bodily elements are in balance,
who eats in moderation and moves about properly,
and count the blinks of his eyes.
A blink is the closing of the eye.
Six blinks make a portion,
which is said to be one brief moment.
Six brief moments make an instant;
six instants make a short spell;
six short spells make a little while.
When this sequence of time
is multiplied by a full thirty,
it is called the time of a heavy syllable.
Ten heavy syllables
are explained as the time called one breath.
That multiplied by thirteen,
and that doubled again,
is called one pala.
A full sixty palas
are held to be one nāḍikā.
Eight of these are called one watch;
four of those make one day.
When the times of day and night are equal,
the night is said to equal it.
From Capricorn up to Cancer
the day is said to lengthen:
goddess, add three palas
each and every day.
Once past Capricorn,
subtract three palas each from the night.

To demonstrate these measures,
take copper of twelve palas in weight
and hammer it carefully
into a vessel eight finger-widths high
and twelve finger-widths wide.
From thirty olsé of gold
hammer out a fine needle
just six finger-widths long,
and with that needle the skilled one
should pierce a hole in the copper vessel.
Of clear, unclouded water
make the measure sixty palas.
By these palas the nāḍikā is measured;
this is explained for the benefit of people.
That nāḍikā is a brief moment.
By the measures of half a watch and a watch,
and of day and night,
increase in order to half a month and a month.
Two months make a season;
three of these are called a solar course.
Two solar courses
are said to make one year."

Vajraḍāka, supreme in bliss, joined in union with all the ḍākas,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed Vajra Holder, spoke this.

This is the twenty-sixth chapter, on the arising of time, from the great king of tantras, the glorious Vajraḍāka.

"Next, what the classes of the lunar mansions are
I shall explain in brief.
Aśvinī, Mṛgaśiras and Puṣya,
and likewise Punarvasu,
Hasta, and likewise Anurādhā,
Revatī, Svāti, together with Śravaṇa,
are said to be the group of the gods.
Rohiṇī, Pūrvaphalgunī, Pūrvāṣāḍhā,
Pūrvabhādrapadā and Bharaṇī,
Ārdrā, Mṛgaśiras, Uttarāṣāḍhā
and Uttarabhādrapadā are the group of humans.
Kṛttikā and Maghā,
Śatabhiṣā and Dhaniṣṭhā,
Āśleṣā, Citrā, Jyeṣṭhā,
Viśākhā and Mūla:
that these are of the rākṣasa class,
fair-faced one, I explain to you.

Kṛttikā and Puṣya are the goat;
Mṛgaśiras and Rohiṇī are the serpent (nāga).
Ārdrā and Mūla are the dog;
Anurādhā and Punarvasu are of the pig class.
Maghā and Pūrvaphalgunī are the rat;
Āśleṣā is explained as the cat;
Citrā and Viśākhā are the tiger.
Uttaraphalgunī and Pūrvabhādrapadā, both of them,
are explained as the bull;
one who wants strength should know this.
Hasta and Svāti, the two,
should be known as the buffalo, supreme in strength.
Aśvinī and Uttarabhādrapadā, the two,
are known as the horse class.
Pūrvāṣāḍhā and Śravaṇa, the two,
are said to be of the monkey class.
Bharaṇī and Revatī, the two,
are blessed by the elephant class.
Śatabhiṣā and Dhaniṣṭhā
are rightly declared to be of the human class;
of great strength, overpowering others,
goddess, I tell you truly.
Uttarāṣāḍhā and Abhijit
are known as the mongoose class.

What is the use of saying much?
Listen as I explain in brief.
One's own birth star
need not be examined for every activity.
The star is a person's life;
I have not taught it otherwise.
If one takes the reversed portions of the stars,
one will certainly die.

Aries, Libra, Cancer
and Capricorn are said to be movable.
Scorpio, Aquarius, Leo
and Taurus are fixed.
Pisces, Gemini, Sagittarius
and Virgo are the dual signs.
Jupiter (the teacher of the gods), the Sun
and Mars are of male form;
Venus, Rāhu
and the Moon are of female form.
Mercury and Saturn, the two,
are taught to be neuter.
These are the planets that dwell in the wheel of the sky."

Vajraḍāka, supreme in bliss, joined in union with all the ḍākas,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed Vajra Holder, spoke this.

This is the twenty-seventh chapter, on the rite of mastery over the power of the lunar mansions, from the great king of tantras, the glorious Vajraḍāka.

"Goddess, listen as I fully explain
the lunar days, the lunar mansions and the rising of the planets.
On the day of Rohita,
with Kṛttikā and Mars,
it is good for a traveler to set out on the road;
that day brings gain."

On the day of Makara,
when Maghā is joined with Mars,
piercing pains, ruin and the like arise,
so that date should be wholly avoided.
On the day of Svapna,
when Ārdrā is joined with Saturn,
it is to be known as black,
so that date too should be wholly avoided.
On the day of Rohita,
when Maghā is joined with Mercury,
it brings auspiciousness and good fortune.
On the day of Raṇḍa,
when Ārdrā and Mercury
are said to come together,
one of supreme practice should avoid it too.
On the date of the Lord of the Earth,
when Bharaṇī is joined with Mercury,
that too is called a black time.
On the dates of Bhadra,
when Pūrvaphalgunī is joined with Jupiter,
that date brings gain,
so the practitioner should make use of it.
On the date of Mikira,
should Śatabhiṣaj and Jupiter
come together,
it is said without doubt
to exhaust grain and wealth.
On the thirteenth day,
should Āśleṣā and Jupiter
come together, it is black,
and one will be in fear of every misfortune.
On the date of Pṛṣabha,
should the minister of the Mighty One
come together with Pūrvabhādrapadā,
that date bestows increase
and brings about all virtuous companions.
On the day of Vṛddhika,
should Pūrvāṣāḍhā and Venus
come together, there is no doubt
one will be tormented by illness and destroyed.
On the day of Bhūta,
if Bharaṇī and Venus
come together, it is the fang of Yama,
so that date should be wholly avoided.
On the day of Kūrma,
should Kṛttikā and Saturn
come together,
one obtains gain, fame and the like,
and the fruit of every merit.
On the day of Vyākulī,
however Āśleṣā and Saturn
come together, it brings torment,
so on that day too, avoid setting out.
On the full-moon day,
if Maghā is joined with Saturn,
the time of death has come,
so that date should be strictly avoided.

Draw Mikira in the southeast,
Raṇḍa in Yama's quarter, the south,
Kūrma in the quarter of the rākṣasas, the southwest,
Vṛṣabha in the quarter of the waters, the west,
Vyākulī in the quarter of the Terrifying One, the northwest,
Svapana in the quarter of treasure, the north,
Bhadra in the quarter of the Three-Eyed One, the northeast,
and in the east, Vṛṣabha too.
According to the distinction of moon and sun,
they are said to move through these.
From Bhadra to Raṇḍa,
with Vyākulī and Pṛṣabha:
these are the course of the sun,
O you of supreme family, as I explain to you.
Vṛṣabha and Mikira,
Svapana and Kūrma
are explained as the course of the moon;
thus they are stated in order.
At the full moon it abides in the southeast;
at the new moon it abides in the quarter of the wind.
Thus those that abide
in the middle become afflicted.
Because one sees as equal
the lines arising from moon and sun,
the wise therefore avoid them.
Because it aspects both the gentle and the malefic,
a severing of the aspect arises,
and it swiftly brings death.
The times of Rāhu's course
have been summarized in brief.
According to the waxing and waning fortnights,
the intelligent should strive to know them.

Vajraḍāka, in union with all ḍākas,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder, has spoken this.

From the great king of tantras, the glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the twenty-eighth chapter, setting out the determination of lunar dates and stars.

Listen, goddess Mahāmāyā,
you who sport through every illusion,
the consciousness of all embodied beings,
beyond the body and free of stain.
Meru and Kailāsa,
and likewise the Vindhya and the rest,
eastern Videha, Godānīya,
northern Kuru,
together with Jambudvīpa,
the salt ocean, the ocean of milk
and the other bodies of water,
whatever lies below and above:
the three realms without remainder should be dissolved.
The aggregates of form and the rest,
the sense sources and the elements should be dissolved.
Imagining all the world systems of the ten directions,
of the bhūtas and the rest, dissolved,
one should meditate on oneself as well
as the mingling of entity and non-entity.
View the characteristics of form and the rest
as sameness and emptiness.
When everything has dissolved into space,
meditate on a city in a cow's hoofprint.
Following the rite of the yogin,
meditate on the mind as like a lamp flame.
Having gazed with the mind at its center,
examine the signs there.
If it is like a lamp flame, it is pacifying;
if adorned with red, it is increasing;
if it appears like the disk of the sun,
attraction and the like will be accomplished;
if at the end smoke or darkness is seen,
that is known as the fierce rite;
if it is greatly agitated, like the color of wind,
expulsion will follow at once.
By brilliant white rays,
the intelligent yogin should know
that the accomplishment of mantra is achieved.
The auspicious sign shows the path.
By this rite others become clear,
and others' forms and the rest, the central ones;
after that the yoga should be generated.
If, by the mind of wisdom and the rest,
one comes to know emptiness,
the mind is swiftly liberated.
By wisdom examine the knowable;
by the knowable examine beings.
Having killed father and mother,
having seized two pure brahmins,
and having destroyed the king together with his realm,
that person becomes pure, it is said.

Joined with all that is the nature of wisdom,
Vajraḍāka, the supreme of bliss,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder, has spoken this.

From the great king of tantras, the glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the twenty-ninth chapter, setting out the arising of wisdom.

By what distinctive wisdom
are the common wisdoms empowered?
What are the distinguished ones like?
I long to hear the music from the god.

The Blessed One said:
Through the thirty heavy ones
the fruits of virtue and of evil arise.
Empowered by pride,
through the masses of karma the mind has made,
the self-aware mind that has been so cultivated
goes to that destiny.
Ever afraid of the noose of existence,
one does not see where it goes.
With the five kinds of perception
and the five views, one goes beyond.
The wisdom of the gods sees clearly;
the wisdom of the asuras is anger;
the wisdom of the pretas is feeble;
the wisdom of the hells is crushed by fear;
the wisdom of animals is dull;
the stationary ones are without mind.

The wisdom of the cause is of five kinds.
Those veiled by dullness, and the mute,
Those whose knowing is the knowing of the childish,
Sentient beings who are not fully ripened,
And those who disparage the path of the guru:
None of these understand it,
For it is the domain of the buddhas of the great secret.
"Please explain the distinctions
Of this very wisdom," the Great Bliss requested.
The Blessed One (the Buddha) declared:
"Listen well, Mahāmāyā,
To the distinctions of this very wisdom.
Through the distinctions of karma,
The distinctions of wisdom are emanations.
The lowly, the very lowly, and those who share in lowliness;
Yakṣas, asuras, and siddhas;
Nāgas, animals, and guhyakas;
Mahoragas and kinnaras;
Humans, hungry ghosts, and ḍākinīs;
Hell-beings, and whatever else there may be;
Those designated by manifest forms,
And the subtle ones born from eggs;
Water, fire, and wind,
And likewise the element of earth;
All things that are stable, and the rest;
Roots and slender leaves;
Soil, stones, rocks, and so forth:
Because all of them are blessed by wisdom,
All of them appear. Were it otherwise,
Without wisdom they would not come to be.
Over thousands of ten millions of births,
Being urged on by the wisdom of the buddhas,
One should then strive
To generate faith in the treatises of yoga.
Refuting the outer treatises,
Which are like a stage for dancing,
If one follows the treatises of yoga,
One attains the fruit of enjoyment and liberation.
More essential even than the essence, supreme,
Is the treatise of yoga that is to be made known."
Vajraḍāka, supreme in bliss,
Who is united in equality with all the ḍākas,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
The Blessed One, the Vajra Holder, has spoken this.

From the Great King of Tantras, the Glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the thirtieth chapter, on the arising of the magical display of Vajrasattva.

Next I shall properly explain
The characteristics of vajra and bell.
As Vajrasattva and the other deities
They are to be understood with vajra intelligence.
The vajras for pacifying, for enriching,
And for fierce rites, every one of them,
Are to be well forged of gold and the like,
Beautifully adorned throughout with vajras.
Having taken it in three parts,
The girdle of eight parts is well adorned.
The eight of the directions and intermediate directions,
And the central one: these are the nine buddhas.
The nine prongs are the deities themselves;
It is blessed by the nine deities.
Arising from makaras,
The prongs rest upon lotus and moon.
It is called the nine-pronged;
The vajra wisdom delivers beings.
Within the measure of the three parts,
The middle may be somewhat longer;
This is the common measure.
The central one among all the prongs is round;
The prongs of the directions and the rest are set converging.
The central one is not to be made eight-cornered.
The central part, possessed of its proper measure,
Where it rests, is called the hub.
Like the jewel marakata, the emerald,
That too should be made slightly eight-faceted.
The upper, the lower, and the middle
Are each held to be of three parts.
The upper lotus and moon
Should be made round all about, a sixth part.
On them the lion's nose
Should be raised about half a barleycorn.
That which lies at the end of the fangs
Is known as the sixth part.
Of the twenty-seven parts,
Each is held to be nine.
The end of the upper three parts,
Leaving aside the two upper parts,
And the end of the middle, is likewise.
The makara that rests upon the petals
Is just one part,
And the girdle is held to be half of that.
For every vajra, the one possessed of intelligence
Should make the length and width equal.

Next I shall properly explain
The characteristics of the samaya vajra.
Forged of gold and the like,
It should be furnished with the parts and the rest
Just as was taught before.
Resting upon lotus and moon
Is the form of the makara's fangs,
Arising from the lower makara,
With length and width as was taught.
The prongs arise as the nature of wisdom.
At the center of them all is Vairocana;
In the east is Akṣobhya;
In the south Ratnasambhava is shown;
Amitābha is placed in the west;
In the north is Amoghasiddhi.
These are the deities of the four prongs and the rest.
The lotus with the eight limbs of awakening
Should be arranged on its ground accordingly:
Padmapāṇi, Maitreya,
Ākāśagarbha, Samantabhadra,
The Lord of Yakṣas (Vajrapāṇi), Mañjuśrī,
Kṣitigarbha, and likewise Sarvanīvaraṇaviṣkambhin.
These eight deities themselves
Should be visualized on the eight petals accordingly.
At the center the wisdom being
Should be dissolved into the thirteen places.
Furthermore, the eight goddesses of the eight petals
Should be set in their places accordingly.
Vajrāṅkuśī and Pāśī,
Vajrasphoṭī and Ghaṇṭī:
These four goddesses
Rest properly on the lotus petals of the directions.
In the four intermediate directions also,
Once again there are likewise four goddesses:
The Exhorter, Ratnadīpā,
Likewise Vajrasphoṭī,
And likewise Aparājitā.
Arrange them on all the lotus petals.
On the five prongs
Are the five ḍākinīs themselves:
In the east is Vajraḍākinī;
In the north the one called Raudrī;
Vetālī abides in the west;
In the south, likewise, Caṇḍālī;
At the center is the Wisdom Ḍākinī.
Thus one should meditate on the samaya vajra.
For the vicious, a three-pronged vajra,
Its prongs should be spread wide.
Likewise the single-pronged one
Strikes terror into the nāgas.
The wrathful vajra is spread wide;
It subdues all the rest.
For pacifying, it is the peaceful deities themselves,
Distinguished by the ritual of the sequence of families.
Through seeing the ritual of the rites,
One will accomplish every activity.

Next I shall explain further
The characteristics of the secret vajra.
The one possessing the measure of five parts
Is known as the crown vajra.
The earring should be made to the measure of six parts;
The necklace is of a fourth part;
At the heart, a fifth part is to be worn;
At the secret place and on the hands as well
A sixth part is to be worn.
It should be devised so as to be beautiful.
Adorned with vajra ornaments,
The yogin's wisdom increases.
Through this very ritual act,
The knower of yoga should always meditate.

Next I shall explain the length, width, and so forth
Of the characteristics of the bell.
It should be made blazing with various jewels,
With two garlands forming a vajra girdle.
Like a stainless ball of crystal,
It is adorned with nets and half-nets.
On the one possessing the characteristic of three parts,
It is adorned with emblems such as vajras.
The central hollow is deep,
The abode of the essence that delights all.
Then it is adorned with lotuses, jewels,
Utpalas, and kumudas.
In eight parts, in the directions and intermediate directions,
The goddesses and the rest are known as eight.
Above that, the goddesses
Should be imagined arising from eight seed syllables.
In the east is Tārā,
And likewise in the north Pāṇḍaravāsinī.

In the west is the one named Māmakī,
And in the south is the one called Buddhalocanā;
Beautiful Hue, Honey, Lovely Lady,
And likewise Jewel Coil.
In the center of the quarter of space
She abides perfectly, encircled by complete forms,
Shaped like a lotus in full bloom.
In the quarter of formless existence
It is like a vase of the nectar of wisdom;
It is the abode of the nectar of wisdom.
On the seat at its center,
Above, one should visualize a lotus and a vajra
With nine prongs.
The central shaft is primordial wisdom,
She who emanates and gathers back all things;
Like oṃ, she succeeds in granting the supreme;
She fully accomplishes every task.
The bell of the vajra of primordial wisdom
Likewise has nine prongs.
To a measure of twelve fingers,
A garland of vajras is made as its band.
It is adorned with the petals of a supreme lotus
And with nets and half-nets.
As before, it is endowed with every characteristic,
And beautified by the emblem of the goddess.
In the eastern quarter, the lotus
Should be beautified with white color;
In Yama's quarter, a blue-red lotus;
In the western quarter, a red lotus;
In the north one should draw an utpala.
In the center of the abode of nectar
One should draw the single face of the goddess:
She is Prajñāpāramitā,
Who abides perfectly, encircled by beautiful forms.
The lotus and vajra above
Should be visualized as before.
The central shaft of primordial wisdom
Should be beautified with eight facets.
This bell is the great goddess,
Perfect in long life and freedom from illness.

Then comes the bell of the hero,
Furnished with a band of vajra garlands,
And likewise endowed with wisdom
And with a vase of nectar.
It is complete in every characteristic;
Nets and half-nets should hang from it.
It is nine fingers wide,
And its mouth should be made to a measure of five fingers;
It should not be made any larger than that.
Five-pronged or nine-pronged,
It bestows all good fortune,
Sovereignty, and all the fruits
Of the mantras;
It bestows the power and miraculous abilities of the ḍākas,
And likewise all excellence,
And clearly bestows the supreme accomplishment
Of all the tathāgatas.

Now I shall explain the characteristics
Of the bell of the tathāgatas.
Made of bell metal, it gives a sweet sound;
It is hollow within and has a vase.
It bears emblems such as the vajra, jewel,
Lotus, and sword,
And is adorned with nets and half-nets.
Arising from a makara,
Through the purity of the five wisdoms
It has five prongs.
Between each pair of bands
It has two garlands of wheels.
Likewise, between two bands,
For the lotus there are two lotus garlands;
For the jewel there are jewels,
Likewise furnished with bands.
For the one called Amoghasiddhi,
The space between two bands is adorned with swords.
Since all of them have the face of wisdom,
All the heroes become victorious.
For all of them, too, the bell is of bell metal
Or else made from three metals,
And it should have three parts.
For the vajra family there is an ox face,
And the band is encircled by a garland of vajras.
The rim that sits above the vase
Should be made slightly square.
The ox face swiftly
Increases wealth and grain,
Always bestows health and strength,
And takes away all suffering.

Following what was taught before,
The characteristics are to be marked by the vajra.
For the jewel, power, and the rest, and for the skull cup,
One should know each one's own emblem.
Eight fingers, or sixteen;
It should not be made larger than twenty.
As far as one is able, and in keeping with the rite,
The foremost practitioner should make them.
The mouth, too, following the stages of the rite,
Should not be other than five fingers.
Goddess, this is the extensive teaching
Of the sādhana of the bell;
Among all the common tantras
It has been praised by the heroes.

In union equally with all the ḍākas,
Vajraḍāka, supreme in bliss,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
The Blessed One, the Vajra Holder, has spoken this.

This is the thirty-first chapter of the great king of tantras, the glorious Vajraḍāka, which sets out the stages of the rite concerning the characteristics and measurements of the vajra and the bell.

I shall fully explain
The characteristics of the vajra master: goddess, listen!
He is disciplined, of beautiful bearing,
And gives fearlessness to all beings.
He knows the applications of mantra and tantra,
And is skilled in yoga and the arts.
In all things he speaks gently,
And regards all beings as his children.
He always delights in giving,
And strives earnestly in the practice of meditative concentration.
With compassion and a mind intent on benefit,
He speaks the truth and does no harm.
Always cultivating a mind of equanimity,
He is protector and refuge of all beings.
He is skilled in the varieties of beings' dispositions,
And is as loving as kin toward those without a protector.
He looks upon others kindly, with affection.
He is thoroughly practiced in the way of mantra,
And deeply devoted to the conduct of the sacred commitments (samaya).
He understands the application of mudrās and signs,
And teaches the yoginīs, in keeping with the commitments,
The application by means of signs.
He abides indivisibly in nondual union,
And is devoted to the practice of the mantra circle.
From him arise the qualities that are the meaning of empowerment;
His nature is to see the sacred places and the like.
He fully knows the ten principles.
Such a one is declared to be a master.

She asked: "How should one regard such a master?"

He declared:
His body is the nature of all the buddhas;
His limbs are bodhisattvas;
The hairs of his body are arhats;
His crown is the buddhas of the five families;
Worldly beings are the seat beneath his feet;
His rays of light are yakṣas, guhyakas, and the like.
Thus the yogin should always regard
His body as having the nature of good qualities.
When one with wisdom has recognized the marks
And found him to possess such characteristics,
He should diligently rely on such a guru.
But whoever is wrathful and deceitful,
Extremely harsh in speech,
And complacent about his own qualities,
Should not be relied on as a guru.

Further, listen as I explain
The conduct of the disciple and the reality of action.
One who is wrathful and devoted to sophistry,
One who is past the proper age,
Whoever is a monk in his formal standing,
And one bound by clinging and attachment:
To such a one the priceless jewel
Should not be explained.
Whoever is free of pride,
Without deceit, not grasping at a self,
And likewise deeply devoted to the Dharma,
To him the instructions should be given.
Otherwise, one incurs a downfall.
The secrets of the master,
The union with yoginīs,
The practice of the deities' commitments,
And the secret applications of torma and the like:
Whoever makes these the object of curses
Falls swiftly into Avīci,
Accompanied by every terror.
The master's bed and seat,
His shoes, parasol, robes, and the rest,
All his personal belongings,
And his shadow, one should not step over,
And one should not mock him.

If one does these things, at all times
he will be afflicted on every side,
his aims will fail, and he will be far from glory.
He will be tormented by māras and by hatred.
Since this is a sin equal to destroying a stūpa,
one should not even step on the master's shadow.
Whoever reviles the master
will have no accomplishment even in dreams.
Hold to the master's good qualities;
never hold on to his faults.
The practice of mantra, mudrā and the rest
is accomplished by way of his path.
Through the sequence of the maṇḍala rite and so forth,
the training should be given first.

To see things as lacking intrinsic nature
is the stage of awakening,
for one sees as real the phenomena,
pure by nature, that have no essence.
Bodily actions are of three kinds,
those of speech are of four kinds,
and those of mind are of three kinds.
Of these, divided thus into ten,
the white make one white,
and the black likewise yield black fruit.
Therefore those should be abandoned.
Harsh words toward sentient beings
and idle chatter should be abandoned.
With a mind of compassion,
violence and harm should be abandoned.
Better that I myself should die
than kill a living creature.
Since it is like a blazing fire,
one should not touch another's wealth.
The yogin guards against falsehood
and carries out all the guru's commands.
The yogin who is a yogin of yogins
tells no lies of any kind
and speaks of no faults in any sentient being.
Others' wives should be avoided,
and wrong livelihood, gain and the like:
therefore all these should be abandoned.
In the enjoyment of the lord of joy,
and in keeping the samaya with tormas at the proper times and so forth,
one should be moderate in food and drink.
For yogins who desire accomplishment,
accomplishment arises from pleasing sentient beings.

In this, first of all, the disciple should drape the upper robe over one shoulder, place the right kneecap on the ground, join the palms of both hands, and say these words:

In the Buddha, the Dharma and the Saṅgha
I always go for refuge.
In the ḍākinīs of yoga, of the secret and so forth,
in all three vehicles,
in the heroes, the heroines, the goddesses of empowerment,
and the great-souled ones,
and above all in the master,
I always go for refuge.

oṃ pañca ḍākinī pūjopasvānāya ātmānāṃ niryatayāmi hūṃ sarvva ācārya pūjobasvānāya ātmānāṃ niryātayāmi hūṃ sarvva satva paritrāṇa arthe ātmanāṃ niryātayāmi hūṃ

Having blessed this with these words, one should once more say the following:

I shall abandon anger and self-grasping,
harsh words and wounding speech.
I shall abandon evil companions, the taking of life,
theft and robbery.
I shall abandon vast enjoyments
and with gentleness be content.
I shall abandon the treatises of outsiders,
which are like a dancer's stage.

So it is said.

Then I shall properly explain
the unsurpassed rite of the vows.
First, the samaya of the guru:
by obtaining it, cause and result arise.
There is teaching, and likewise definitive teaching.
It has the nature of body, speech and mind.
The teaching is enjoyment;
through definitive teaching comes nirvāṇa.
Eating feces, urine and the like,
the suitable and the unsuitable, and others too:
by the instructions of the mantras,
empower with the name and so on, according to the rite.
After the disciple has meditated on reality,
nirvāṇa should be taught.
"You should kill living beings,
and you should speak falsehood.
You should take what is not given,
and you should resort to others' wives.
You should not perform works at stūpas,
nor should you read the scriptures.
Do not bind mudrās with the hands,
and do not recite the syllables of mantras."
Treatises are easy to find,
but their application is hard to find.
By the means of diligence in serving the guru,
the yogin finds it.
One's own body and the bodies of others:
oneself is supreme bliss, awakened and free.
Without attachment to one's own body,
regard it as a deity, like a reflection and so forth.

Thus spoke Vajraḍāka, supreme in bliss,
united with all the ḍākas,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder.

This concludes the thirty-second chapter, setting out the stages of serving the guru, of the samaya, and of the rite of the vows, from the Great King of Tantras, the Glorious Vajraḍāka.

Then the ḍākinīs,
who sport across the grounds,
sport through the arising of mantras.
Now I shall properly explain.

oṃ kṣa māṃ kṣa māṃ phaṭ phaṭ kara kara bandha bandha paca paca hīṃ hīṃ hāṃ hāṃ daha daha kuru kuru trasaya trasaya bhakṣa bhakṣa hūṃ hūṃ kili kili phasarudhira antramālāvalambina gṛhna gṛhna kṣobhaya kṣobhaya hra hra ākaḍḍhya akaḍḍhya cili cili sili sili sabta phātāla gatā bhujaṃga sarbbaṃ vā tarjjaya tarjjaya hrauṃ hrauṃ pheṃ pheṃ hrīṃ hrīṃ dhili dhili huṃ hūṃ phaṭ

oṃ namo bhagavate vīreśāya mahākkalpāgnisannibhāya jaṭāmukuṭotkaṭāya daṃṣṣakarālograbhiṣaṇamukhāya sahasrabhujabhāsurāya paraśupāśodyataśūlakhaṭvāṃgadhāriṇi vyāghrājināmbaradharāya mahādhūmrānadhakāra vapuṣāya huṃ hūṃ phaṭ

oṃ hūṃ svāhā phaṭ

oṃ śrī vajra he he ru ru kaṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ ḍākinījālasambara svāhā

oṃ vajra vairocanīye svāhā

oṃ hrīḥ h ha huṃ hūṃ phaṭ

oṃ hana mahi svāhā hu vauṣaṭ he huṃ hūṃ ho phaṭ haṃ svāhā

oṃ hīṃ hīṃ hūṃ heṃ heṃ hā

oṃ vaṃ haṃ yoṃ hreṃ moṃ hreṃ hriṃ huṃ hūṃ phaṭ phaṭ

oṃ sarbba buddha vajra varṇaniye vajra ḍākiniye svāhā

oṃ sumbha nisumbha hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ

oṃ gṛhṇa gṛhṇa hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ

oṃ gṛhṇāpaya hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ

oṃ ānaya ho bhagavāṃ vidyārājā hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā

oṃ vajra ḍākinī varṣa varṣapaya hūṃ bhu svāhā

oṃ ghili ghili varṣa varṣāpaya hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ ho ho ho ṭha ṭha ṭha svāhā

oṃ sumbha nisumbha hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ

oṃ kakakṣi kakarudri kaka inbahariṇi amukasya jīvitaṃ śīghraṃ āgaccha kaki yathāsukhaṃ phaṭ

oṃ sarbba tithin kṣatra graha maṃgalebhyaḥ svāhā

oṃ amunikasya inunipasi muni hatha lāṃghale hi phaḍe i amukasya mukha hatha bandhāni svāhā

oṃ ratna vajra vajra hri phaṭ

oṃ gra svāhā

oṃ aṭe baṭe naṭe kunaṭe ṭhake ṭharake taru bitaru benatake lili lulu hile mile tile ṣile sile athe bathe nathe kunthe anapaṭi hanapati kasya kasya ghasya gha mahāghasya mahāghasya svāhā

oṃ piṅgalākṣaya hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā

By this mantra the practitioner
will accomplish all the rites.
Following the mantra ritual exactly,
listen, goddess, and I shall explain.

oṃ namo bhagavati vajravārāhi ārya aparājite trailokyamāte mahāvadyeśvari sarbba bhūta bhayāvahe mahāvajre vajrāsane ajite aparājite vaśyṝkarintrabhramaṇi viṣaśoṣaṇi roṣaṇi krodhani karālini santrasani māraṇi suprabhedani parājaye vijaye jaṃbhani staṃbhani mohani vajravārāhi mvahāyogini kāmeśvari khage tadyathā protaṃge protaṃge hana hana prāṇāṃ kiṃkini khiṃkhini dhuna dhuna vajrahaste śoṣaya śoṣaya khaṭvāṃga kapālamāladhāriṇi mahāpeśitamāṃsāśani manuṣa antra prabritti sannidhya naraśiromālā braṃthita dhāraṇi sumbha nisumbha hana hana praṇāṃ sarbba pāpa satvānāṃ sarbba vaśūnāṃ mahāmāṃsacchedani krodhamūrtti daṃṣṭvā karālini mahāmudre śri heruka devasya gra mahiṣi sahasraśirau sahasravāhave śatasahasrānane jālitatejase jvalāmukhi liṅgalalocane vajraśarīre vajra āsane mili mili timili timili he he ha ha hūṃ hūṃ kha kha dha dhu dhuru dhuru muru muru advaite mahāyoginī paṭhita siddhe draiṃ ṅhaṃ draiṃ dhaṃ graṃ graṃ he he ha ha bhīme hasa hasa vīre ha ha ho ho hūṃ hūṃ trailokya vināsani śatasahasrakoṭi tathāgata parivārite hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ siṃharūpe khaḥ gajarūpe gaḥ trailokya udare savamudra mekhale grasa grasa hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ vīrādvaite hūṃ hūṃ hā hā mahāvaśu mohani yogiśvari tvaṃ ḍākinī sarbba lokānāṃ bandani sadyaḥ pratyaya kāriṇi hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ bhūta trāsani mahāvīre parama siddhe virya yogeśvari phaṭ hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ svāhā

a ā i ī u ū ṛ ṝ li lī e ai o au aṃ aḥ

ka kha ga gha ṅa ca cha ja jha ña ṭa ṭha ḍa ḍha ṇa ta tha da dha na pa pha ba bha ma ya ra la va śa ṣa sa ha kṣaḥ

This is the Blessed One, the deity himself,
the supreme abode of all the gods,
the one who gives birth to all mantras;
and so too are the tantras.

Vajraḍāka, supreme in bliss,
in union with all the ḍākas,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder, has spoken.

This was the thirty-third chapter, setting out the mantra rites, from the great king of tantras, the glorious Vajraḍāka.

Next, what is to be properly explained:
what is hidden in the tantra shall be made certain.
Then, within the source of phenomena of all,
at the center of the variegated vajra, the indestructible and auspicious
is to be drawn; then, following the rite, with mantra
one should gather all phenomena into one.
Having made the vowels and consonants equal,
one should dissolve them into their classes.
Abiding there, the hero,
so as to accomplish every desired aim,
will attain wisdom, perfect enjoyment,
and emanation, without doubt.
He constantly grants the accomplishments below and above;
all my female messengers come,
and those messengers grant every accomplishment.
Seeing, and likewise touching,
kissing and constant embracing,
the very particulars of the state of union,
and however many hosts of yoga there are,
all are declared to bring accomplishment.
All truth is to be given,
and never otherwise.

Mother, sister, daughter,
and wife, abiding as messenger women:
one who constantly bestows the mantra
should perform his own rite in just this way.
The crown ornament of a garland of skulls,
and likewise the crest jewel of the buddhas,
the lotus, the root of the glorious forest:
in the language of signs, these are the seals of the buddhas.
By whichever path of whichever sense faculty,
their intrinsic nature goes to that very thing.
Through union in supreme equipoise,
one oneself becomes all the buddhas.

Vajraḍāka, supreme bliss,
in union with all the ḍākas,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder, has proclaimed.

From the great king of tantras, the glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the thirty-fourth chapter, on the sequence of signs of the vajra empowerment.

Then I shall explain the arising of wisdom
that fully bestows
all the accomplishments of the ḍākinīs.
The lord of yoga in the guise of a donkey
averts by the force of mind.
The ḍākinī, the Beautiful One,
Khaṇḍarohā, and Rūpiṇī, the yoginīs:
these very yoginīs
are taught by way of signs.
Meditative concentration, the seal, the one whose form is desire;
in the guise of a donkey, with a face dripping blood,
a spear tip wound about with snakes,
three-pointed, held in the ḍāka's hand;
holding khaṭvāṅga and skull cup
raised aloft in praise;
seated on a corpse, blazing greatly,
the hair tawny and standing on end,
made beautiful by three eyes,
adorned with every ornament,
holding a copper knife in hand and at play:
meditate on him in a lofty jeweled place
rich with various flowers and fruits.
Short life and death too,
and life and long life,
the yogin of the donkey's way
sees as if they lay in the palm of his hand.

Vajraḍāka, supreme bliss,
in union with all the ḍākas,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder, has proclaimed.

From the great king of tantras, the glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the thirty-fifth chapter, on the sequence of the rite of Heruka's play.

Then I shall rightly explain
the rite of the practitioner just as it is.
Daily, or else monthly,
or likewise yearly,
if one confers the blessing properly,
the accomplishment of the seal comes about.
With the practitioner's left hand
holding the lotus vessel,
and the right hand formed
into the three-flag gesture, one should meditate.
What has arisen from the nature of all the elements,
and likewise from the life-force and the channels,
fashioned and well drawn,
should be given to all the masters.
Fully endowed with divine ornaments,
adorned with the emblems of the seal,
the one born of the vow of buddha yoga
should perform the great dance of good fortune.
If one does not touch the supreme lotus
even for the span of a year,
the practitioner forsakes the samaya;
and having forsaken the samaya,
he should take anew the vow not to do so again.
Otherwise mantra and mudrā
in their sequence become fruitless.

Vajraḍāka, supreme bliss,
in union with all the ḍākas,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder, has proclaimed.

From the great king of tantras, the glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the thirty-sixth chapter, on the sequence of the rite of play by signs, of samaya, and of vows.

The characteristics of all weapons
I shall fully explain; goddess, listen!
Those hurled by a device, those not hurled,
likewise those hurled by hand,
those hurled and then caught back,
and those wielded by hand: five kinds.
Shape, ease, and fitting,
fitting the hollow string, drawing, and speed,
filling, shooting, and clarity;
tautness and a planted stance,
suppleness and steady limbs,
filling broadly, and purity:
the seal of the body, done with ease.
When the body is adorned with this,
fevers, poisons, venoms,
torments of ḍākas, and grahas
can never gain hold.

As though drawing it to one's own heart,
holding the bow with ease,
turning it fully to the right,
if one shoots the arrow, there is accomplishment.
Having straightened the east well
and bent the west a little,
if one draws the bowstring slowly,
all beings are summoned.
This is the seal of summoning all.

Round, as far as beside the ear,
held well in the fitting way,
drawn to the mouth with the syllable oṃ,
for as long as one utters the syllable a,
for that long it should be held.
This is the seal of holding.

Having drawn it in the fitting way
and held it according to the rite,
there one should open it a little;
every filling is accomplished.
This is the seal of filling.

Covering the target with the fist,
one looks with the eyes slightly open;
fixing the gaze upon the fist,
if one shoots swiftly, it is the supreme accomplishment.
This is the seal of victory by bow and arrow.

Square and sharp,
and likewise round on every side,
turning from the right,
above and likewise below,
pressing step by step and pressing fiercely:
by the supreme seal of the ten piercings,
over the great kingdom of the three realms
one conquers with ease and enjoys it.
This is the unsurpassed application of the seal of glorious victory over the three realms.

Vajraḍāka, supreme bliss,
in union with all the ḍākas,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder, has proclaimed.

From the great king of tantras, the glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the thirty-seventh chapter, on the sequence of the armor of wisdom of the signs of all weapons.

Then I shall rightly explain
the means of practice for winning over the messengers.
Ordinary women with bodies of mantra,
and any field-born woman whatever:
the practitioner should bring about bliss
for all of them with supreme benefit.
These are not to be attended by others
with the service of enjoyments.
Fortunate, wealthy, and steadfast,
of auspicious family and conduct of her own;
the marked woman (citriṇī) has a restless mind;
the lotus woman (padminī) has full breasts;
the conch woman (śaṅkhinī) has a navel like a conch:
their order is stated accordingly.

If one carefully places the root of the bhūta tree in the mouth, the mantra practitioner will hold back for as long as he finds bliss with the girl. If one grinds gandusha and the lakṣaṇā herb, cooks them in sesame oil, and anoints the feet, this too is praised by those who indulge in desire.

The bulb of the aśvattha tree, pāṭhā, and pushel tse are mixed in equal parts and combined with the milk of the arka tree. If one rubs the penis with this, it will undoubtedly grow. If one then quickly washes it with warm water, it will become as large as one wishes. If one grinds the anthers of a white lotus together with honey and anoints the navel, even unyielding women will let their fluid flow. White sesame, nāgakesara, and the roots of the plant called Upright Hero are ground, mixed with honey and buffalo butter, and applied to the navel. Then the penis becomes firm, and passionate women are captivated, and

Enjoying desire like a donkey,
giving bliss to those who seek bliss:
from the tantra of unfailing discipline
I have explained the retention.

With the liṅga one should win women;
with the bhaga one should win men.
Liṅga is explained as "sign" for those who seek its meaning;
it is to be joined with the bhaga.
The mudrā is union with the mudrā.
Through its playful display it is taught
to be what is called liṅga;
through its opening it is bhaga.
What was not taught is clearly taught;
its very nature is that of what is indicated.

From the letter a and the letter ba,
generating bhaga and liṅga,
with the bee and the rest, in proper order,
one should accomplish all the ḍākinīs.

Vajraḍāka, supreme in bliss,
in union with the ḍākinī,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder, has spoken.

This is the thirty-eighth chapter, the section on the rite of taming the messengers, from the Great King of Tantras, the Glorious Vajraḍāka.

Then the goddess made offerings to the Blessed One (the Buddha), bowed to him, and said:

"Sentient beings who have no merit
and are unable to rely on mantras:
how will they gain accomplishment?
How power is produced in the powerless,
and how one is protected even without mantras:
please explain this in brief."

"You who display illusion's magic everywhere,
listen, goddess Mahāmāyā!
In order to captivate beings,
I shall explain the divine medicine.
How it is to be taken,
that too I shall explain.
Having made oneself the very nature of the deity,
afterward one should accomplish the seed.
It captivates beings
and likewise performs many kinds of tasks.
From planting seeds and the like,
many kinds of rites arise."

The goddess asked, "What sort of seeds are they?"

The Blessed One said, "They are the seeds of trees such as the castor plant and the golden tree. These and others, when fully ripened, should be accomplished according to the rite.

On the bhūta day of the waning fortnight,
anoint them with menstrual blood and pigment, and charge the lords,
with ash from the charnel ground,
together with torma and protection.
Likewise plant the divine shoots:
pāṭhā and jayā,
aparājitā,
cakrāṅga, and mohana.
They turn back all weapons.
Cakrāṅga is for what is hurled by hand;
pāṭhā for what a machine hurls;
jayā for what is held after being hurled;
aparājitā for what is wielded by hand;
mohana for what is not hurled.
These are explained in coded language.
Binding all of them tightly,
rolling them up toward the left,
if one wears them at the throat or the crown,
they turn back the five weapons.

What is born from the lotus
of a dark-blue caṇḍāla girl,
having been anointed with the five nectars,
should be dried in the heat of the sun.
In the skull of a black dog
filled with charnel-ground earth,
on the eighth or fourteenth of the waning fortnight,
plant it in the forest of the ancestors, the charnel ground.
Then, taking it up with care,
having worshiped the charnel ground, the guru,
and glorious Heruka,
take the seed of accomplishment
and place it in a skull cup.
Consecrate it according to the rite;
if it is tied at the neck, one becomes invisible.
Resting in meditative equipoise, untie the binding.

In the skull of a black crow,
or in the skull of a man
killed by a spear or a weapon,
with the fat of a black snake
anoint, again and again,
the seeds of the golden tree and the rest.
Fill it with earth from an anthill
and with burnt charnel-ground earth,
and in the middle of a crossroads
or on the bank of a river,
the practitioner, in deep meditative equipoise,
should place the skull at evening time.
Giving torma, watering it,
and protecting it should be done every day.
Taking the form of glorious Heruka,
endowed with the khaṭvāṅga and the rest,
holding a skull cup in the left hand,
engaged in the conduct of the vow,
if one cultivates it according to the rite,
piśācas and the like will not interfere.
Having taken the ripened seed,
the wise one, having worshiped as before,
should above all offer to the guru
the first portion of the accomplished substance.
If the practitioner then puts it in his mouth,
he appears in all kinds of forms:
a god, a patron, a yakṣa, and so on,
changing his form just as he wishes,
and with the daughters of the gods
he sports for as long as he likes.

Preceded by the evening torma,
one should duly perform the preparatory consecration,
and the protection and the rest
as given in the extensive rite for growing trees.
Otherwise the flowers, fruits and so on,
being harmed by fierce grahas,
will not come to success.

Then, in the left hand
of a girl with loosened hair, naked,
the practitioner should place
that fruit, if it has ripened.
With the blood of whatever creature it may be,
smear the fruit again and again.
The flesh and other substances
smeared with its blood should be applied,
and it should be grown with its ashes.
Plant it in its skull,
and with its flesh, blood and fat
water it again and again.
Fumigate it with its flesh and the rest,
and anoint it again and again.
Beforehand, perform the torma, the protection and so forth.
When that seed has ripened,
take it according to the rite;
if it is placed inside the mouth,
just as one has imagined through the rite,
one takes on this or that form.
Likewise, by its power
one becomes completely invisible.
Through the strength of the unimpaired seed,
even owls and the like
cannot see so much as a part of one.

Next I shall explain another,
for turning back all weapons.
At the time of a solar eclipse,
the herb called Paṇḍaraka
and the single hero Mahābala:
in the morning, at sunrise,
dig up the root on the northern side,
and take it while in meditative equipoise.
By the sequence of the rite taught before,
it bewilders the moving and the unmoving.
On a dangerous road hard to travel,
against fear of tigers, lions and the like,
fear of kings and thieves and so on,
it is the unsurpassed protection from them all.
Or else, its filaments
should be eaten together with curd.
Until it wells up into the throat,
so long should one keep eating.
As long as it is not digested,
one's body becomes like that of Vajradhara.
Even weapons striking that body,
like drops of water, do it no harm.

Next I shall explain another application
that turns back all weapons.
With torma and the rest, as before,
take the root of aṅkoṭaka,
make a pill from it,
and wrap it in the three metals.
Goddess, if it is placed in the mouth,
all weapons are turned back,
and it cuts off fevers, lung disease, difficult breathing
and other illnesses as well.
Poisons of many kinds,
bodily rot and other afflictions too,
like elephants before a lion,
flee at its mere scent.

Next, another application is explained.
The divine herb called Veti:
on Puṣya and a Sunday,
or else during a lunar eclipse,
the practitioner, sealed with the mudrā,
focusing on the yoga of the deity,
having made the supreme torma and the rest,
should diligently recite the mantra,
and with hair loosened and naked,
deeply in equipoise, should take it.
Through this ritual procedure
it will succeed; of this there is no doubt.
Dividing it into seven parts,
the wise one should wear it on the crown of the head.
By this method, Mahāmāyā,
all weapons are turned back.
Whatever other weapons
there are upon the earth, it turns them back too;
as the sun dispels the darkness,
by a mere glance it turns them back.

Next, another rite is explained.
The great herb Mahākāla:
the wise one, when the planet Sun
is joined with Puṣya,
through the rite of the stūpa and the torma,
deeply in equipoise, should take it.
Afterwards, mix it together
with earth from a bull's horn.
For one year it keeps its potency;
then take another, fresh one.
As long as it remains on the body,
one is not cut or pierced by weapons,
and in that practitioner's body
even poison does not spread.

By the ritual procedure taught before,
take the herb Bhramadanta,
and mix it together
with the mud of a lotus.
By the sequence of the rite taught before,
take Īśvarī,
and, deeply in equipoise, mix it
with earth from a solitary liṅga.
Its virtues are the same as before.

Listen, goddess of great wisdom!
The herb called Kumārī,
dust from a maiden's footprint,
together with earth where she has urinated,
joined with a buffalo horn:
this mud is honored by the Thirty-Three gods.

Next I shall properly explain
the herb called Biṭhṇubi.
By the ritual procedure taught before,
take the forest garland.
Turning it back, diligently take
clay from the spinning wheel
and combine it with that.
If it remains on the body, one is protected.
With one-pointed mind, rest in equipoise!

The herb called Vārāhī,
and mud that has come from a pig's body,
should be placed inside a buffalo horn.
For one year it keeps its potency;
it too works as before.
Earth from a crossroads and a mountain peak,
earth stuck to an elephant's tusk,
together with the root of the Indra tree:
this is called the mud that masters the Thirty-Three.

Next I shall explain Cāmuṇḍā,
unsurpassed, of a single color.
Take the root of Caṇḍālī
and prepare it as taught before.
Combine it with earth from a solitary tree
and a solitary liṅga, and make it into mud.
As long as it remains on the body,
not even the Thirty-Three can destroy one.

Next I shall explain another rite,
which gathers all essences.
One who is the first born from the womb
and has not drunk its mother's milk:
worship Heruka
together with Vajravārāhī,
and having done so, great goddess,
give torma and protect again and again.
Having split the back of its neck,
take the tongue together with its root.
From bhṛṅgirāja, the golden tree,
kagagaja and aparājitā,
together with the divine herb,
make a pill,
and if it is placed and held in the mouth,
all weapons are turned back.
If it turns back even all the weapons
there are upon the earth,
what need is there to speak of a few?
Harm from fevers, poisons and diseases,
from ḍākinīs and grahas,
all of these are turned back;
I do not say otherwise.

Next I shall explain another application.
With its flesh, fat and blood,
together with urine and semen,
smear black turmeric.
On Puṣya and on the day of the bhūtas,
in order to pacify the obstructors,
give torma to all the bhūtas.
In order to accomplish all actions,
the mantrin endowed with the rite,
in the belly of a child's corpse,
on the bhūta day of the waning fortnight,
on the ground of a charnel ground,
should plant it according to the rite and make it grow,
and guard it from jackals and the like.
When its leaves have fallen and it has ripened,
take it according to the earlier rite;
as long as it remains on the body,
one becomes a hero in an instant.
From the child's tongue taught before,
together with black turmeric,
make a pill and place it in the mouth;
if one enters battle, the enemy is bound.

Next I shall explain the thread
that bewilders all armies.
On Puṣya, with great care,
take wild cotton,
smear it with menstrual blood,
and a maiden should diligently spin it,
three-ply and again three-ply.

With the eight-line mantra
one should recite, and tie that many knots.
All of the knots
should be visualized as the eight mātṛkās.
Having thus blessed them
with the eight mantras,
recite one hundred and eight times
over each and every one.
If it is then tied on the left arm,
all weapons, such as arrows,
swords and the rest, will be bound.
This vidyā of Mahāmāyā
should be kept secret with the utmost effort.

Next, another rite is explained.
Take the cotton of the forest śālmali tree
under the asterism Puṣya.
Moisten it with a girl's urine
and mix it with that;
it should be spun by a girl.
On an auspicious planet and asterism,
and when the moon is strong,
perform the tying of the knots,
and for each one separately,
by the procedure given before,
practice in deep meditative equipoise.
Merely by being bound with this,
one cannot be bound even by the Thirty-Three.
By the sight of this ritual act
all weapons become bound.
This is to be accomplished in a cattle pen.

Surakta, khadira,
nimba, or else arka:
under Puṣya and on a Sunday
take two sticks of these.
The great vidyā that intoxicates,
chief of all vidyās,
should be recited a hundred times;
then if the sticks are rubbed against each other,
the enemies will fight among themselves.

Over vajra water
together with blood from the finger
recite the vidyā of the Intoxicating Goddess.
With mantra recitation going first,
if one makes a mark on the forehead,
robbers and thieves,
and any others who do harm,
all people are bewildered.

Vajraḍāka, supreme in bliss,
united with all the ḍākas,
Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One, the Vajra Holder, has spoken this.

From the great king of tantras, the glorious Vajraḍāka, this is the thirty-ninth chapter, the section on the rites for generating power.

Next I shall explain another application
by which the practitioner attains success.
The seeds of the cotton plant
that sit at the tip of the head should be taken.
As before, on the bhūta day of the fortnight of the pretas,
on an auspicious planetary day,
to Heruka, the supreme protector,
together with the ḍākinīs Gaurī and the rest,
anoint them again and again
with a woman's flower.
In the beautiful skull of a woman
who was impaled or slain by a weapon,
and not dragged off by jackals and the like,
one should grow them by that rite.
Moreover, setting it upon
the water-wheel of the she-camels,
take what comes from its top.
The mantrin, having made offerings,
meditates on it as a girl, and carefully
sprinkles the water that stirs it.
Fill it with earth and ash
from a woman's cremation ground,
and in that very skull
grow the cotton seeds.
Reciting intently the mantras
of Vighnāntaka and Yamāntaka,
offer worship and torma,
and guard it as is fitting.
With water over which the pacifying verse mantra
has been recited one hundred and eight times,
water it there and make it grow;
otherwise harm from pretas will arise.
Guard it from insects and the like,
and cultivate the plant as the Veda on trees prescribes.
Then carefully take
the ripened cotton,
anoint it with a maiden's blood,
and together with the hair of the girl from before
let a girl spin it.
If one binds with that thread,
at once a man
becomes a woman tormented by desire;
if it is untied, he returns to normal.

With blood and fat of an impaled corpse
anoint white mustard seed again and again.
In a secret field,
on the eighth day, under Puṣya,
a skull from the road and one of the highest caste,
filled with cremation-ground earth, along with a corpse,
plant it on auspicious days.
Take it when it is fully ripe.
With the oil pressed out by a press as fitting,
the wise one performs all acts such as expulsion,
just as he wishes.
Fully endowed with the fifth earth-boundary,
arising from the seed syllable of wind,
the target is expelled at that very moment
and does not come back.

The corpse of a man killed by impalement
is to be worshipped according to the order of the rite,
and, just as taught before,
all its limbs smeared with that oil.
With the conduct of Heruka,
reciting mantra, remain there throughout.
That great yogin must drive off the obstructors.
At that time the great mantrin
sees obstructors of many kinds
arising from the body of the corpse;
then and there, at that time,
they obstruct the substance of accomplishment.
The practitioner with his attendants
should then bind the mudrā:
binding the womb mudrā
above the forehead, he displays it,
and in wrathful form
displays the heroes' samaya as is fitting.
Give the pretas
a torma with flesh and blood.
The oil that drips from its body
is taken and poured into a flask or the like.
Merely by smearing the body with it
one attains the power to travel through the sky.
Furthermore, the sword, the eye ointment,
the elixir and the rest are fully accomplished.

Anoint white mustard seed
with blood and human fat,
place it in the mouth of a human corpse,
and carefully perform protection and the rest.
Then whoever is struck
by the mustard seed grown as before,
that target is seized by fever
and dies at once.

Anoint sesame with the blood
and fat of a so bird,
and grow it in that bird's skull
filled with cremation ash.
Worship, torma and the rest
are to be done as taught before, as fitting.
If the ripened fruit is placed in the mouth,
a man takes on the form of a bird.
Likewise with other seeds too:
take the fruit that rests within the pod,
and one takes on a form resembling that;
if it is taken out of the mouth, one becomes as before.
The lifespan is equal to that of birds.
Elephant forms and the like are done in the same way.

The seed of vidārī
is to be taken in the waning fortnight, under Puṣya
and on the planetary day of the sun.
The citron, in the waxing fortnight,
is smeared with the fat of a human corpse;
when the moon is seized by the eclipse planet,
it is planted in that one's head,
and it becomes the form of the one seized.
Its bones, marrow,
flesh and so forth are to be grown.
Its skin is adorned on the limbs
with bone ornaments,
and the wise one, as taught before,
makes offerings and scatters ash.
With water charged with mantra
the learned one waters it constantly.
The mantras of Vighnāntaka and Yamāntaka
the mantrin recites constantly.
Give torma to the piśācas;
otherwise they will pull it out and carry it off.
Take that seed when it is ripe;
wrap it with three tendrils,
and if it is placed in the mouth, one becomes invisible.

Even by the sun, the moon and the rest
one will not be seen here in the three worlds.
A single citron (mātuluṅga),
placed in the mouth, turns one into a citron;
when it is taken out, one appears once more.
This is the common rite for cultivating fruits.

Next the mantra rite is explained.
With Locanā's mantra one should plant it;
with Māmakī's mantra, water it;
with Tārā's mantra one should make it grow;
and likewise Pāṇḍaravāsinī protects it.
These are earth and the other elements,
which hold and nourish all things.
Therefore, with every effort,
one should protect it by Locanā and the rest.

Then, at the time of evening,
the seeds of the kubakhande,
smeared with human fat and the like,
are grown in a skull filled with earth from a crossroads.
With it make a mark on the forehead.
The five kinds of food, things to eat, to chew and the rest,
turn bitter merely by being seen.
Then, if the mark is wiped away,
at that very moment they become normal again.

The seed of the olmasé
the mantrin grows in a brahmin's skull
under the constellation Puṣya.
Then it is taken out, and once again
grown in the skull of an ordinary man.
When that seed has been accomplished,
by putting it in the mouth one takes on that form:
man, woman, neuter and so forth,
one becomes as the rite that is performed.
If it is taken from the mouth, once again
one has one's ordinary form, without a doubt.
This application is proven;
I do not teach it anywhere else.

The mantrin grows the medicinal herb bhṛṅgarāja
in the skull of one killed upon an impaling stake,
and should sprinkle it with fat and blood.
Taking its root,
one should mix it with blood from the ring finger
together with fat.
If it is placed on the crown of the head, one becomes victorious
and brings the gods and others under one's power.

Having found the ox-ear plant,
take its seed when it is ripe and put it in the mouth;
if one then enters battle, one will be victorious.

Having taken bhṛṅgarāja,
with the blood of a dark butcher's daughter,
during the waning half of the month,
one should smear it again and again and compound it.
Together with the shoot of the ox-ear plant
grown in a man's skull,
it will bestow a great result.

By properly growing these roots
and fruits and others like them,
all obstructors are destroyed:
so it is taught in the definitive tantra of the buddhas.
There is nothing that cannot be practiced,
and nothing that will not be accomplished.
But apart from the oral instructions,
accomplishment will not appear.

The fruit of the kokanata
is grown in the skull of an elephant.
Bhṛṅga that has sprung up in a charnel ground,
together with its root grown in a skull,
is ground with the five nectars
on the fourteenth day of the waxing and the waning fortnights.
Having made fine pills,
one should dry them in the shade.
By making a mark with them,
all beings come under one's power like servants.

The stone of the mango tree
is smeared with the great butter-fat,
and likewise smeared with the five products of the cow.
Having smeared semen on one's hand,
whoever that fruit is given to
comes under one's power, and not otherwise.

The fruit of the pomegranate,
smeared with blood and planted
in a woman's skull: that ripened fruit,
if given to a lord of men, a king,
brings him forever under one's power.

The seeds of the kubakhande
are smeared with the fruits of the nimba and the like.
One grows the seed in a cracked bowl
holding earth from a cremation ground,
and guards it, resting in deep meditative equipoise.
On a day of the waning fortnight,
at midday, the time when the sun is at its height, one accomplishes it.
By merely pouring it in,
beer and the like will be spoiled, without a doubt.

Next I shall explain something more.
Take one hundred and eight
ripe white mustard seeds, anoint them with fat,
and wrap them well into a bundle.
Place it in the belly of a woman's corpse.
Leave it seven days, then take it out,
and with earth mixed with her liver
fill her skull and grow them.
With torma, protection,
incense, watering and the rest,
with the utmost effort,
perform all the actions as before.
Let them grow until they ripen.
Then, as before, gather them with care
and smear them with a woman's blood.
Leaves, seeds and so on,
any part of it whatever:
whatever woman is struck with it
will follow behind one.

Carefully take the seed of the olsé
and grow it in a tawny skull.
Taking its shoot,
the intelligent one should make an egg.
If beer is poured into it,
the substances held in that vessel,
for as long as they have not run out,
those remaining there will never run out.

Earth from a riverbank, potter's earth,
likewise earth stuck to a plowshare,
earth from an anthill,
and earth of a single kind
are highly praised for all cultivation.
Take earth from a place where someone has urinated
and consecrate it fully with mantra.
From it, mixed with donkey urine,
the skilled one should make a human effigy.
Having purified the golden tree as before,
the mantrin should grow it in the effigy's mouth.
Taking five parts of it,
one should make fine pills.
They will perform all actions,
and especially bring others under one's power.

White mustard and other fruits
are smeared with an elephant's flesh,
blood, bone, fat and so forth,
and with its ashes and the like,
and grown in its skull.
By merely putting this in the mouth
one takes on the form of an elephant.
This is a common rite.
Likewise for horse, buffalo and the rest,
likewise for tiger, hare and the rest:
by growing them in the same way,
and meditating with blood, one becomes a tiger and so forth.

At the time of performing the rite of application,
combine it with the accomplishing planets and so forth.
Delighting always in meditation and recitation,
guarding the conduct of the sacred commitments,
by these one will always succeed;
otherwise there will be no result.

So spoke Vajrasattva, the Tathāgata,
the Blessed One (the Buddha), the Vajra Holder.

This is the fortieth chapter of the Great King of Tantras, the Glorious Vajraḍāka, setting out the stages of artificially produced fruits and of the wisdom of the mudrā.

Then the two, the vowels and consonants:
from them alone everything is generated,
and they are what emanates and gathers back,

the rite of conventional expression,
the common characteristics of mantra.
Listen to these rites just as they are.

The syllable called ā, red in color,
without its head-stroke and its drop,
one should imagine at the target's heart, at the head,
at the secret place and at the navel.
The syllable called ā, endowed with the drop,
colored like vermilion, like olmasé,
and like the kusuma flower,
one imagines on the target's head,
and imagines him intoxicated, bereft of mind,
mute and utterly ruined.
If one meditates that his body
is stirred up and stupefied by one's own seed syllable,
before long he comes under one's control
and will always give everything.

The syllable called ī, like a ladder,
the short one, the third vowel,
one should meditate at the target's heart, at the navel,
and likewise at the throat.
The fourth vowel, called ī,
colored like red lead,
should be placed on the target's forehead.
By placing the supreme third and fourth
on someone's body,
he is made intoxicated, his dress in disarray,
and bereft of mind,
and for as long as he lives
he comes under control like a mute.

Then the fifth vowel,
with light like Indra's weapon,
the one called ū, one should imagine
like a noose at the target's throat.
The supreme sixth vowel,
blazing like lightning,
the one called ū, one should meditate
in the center of the target's heart.
He becomes mute and intoxicated,
his body trembles, he is bereft of mind,
and to wherever the practitioner dwells
he will come as though deranged.
He himself, together with his wealth,
will also come under control.

The syllable called ṛ, the color of smoke,
its forehead parted from its body,
that sacred vowel
one should meditate at the heart and at the secret place.
Then ṝ, the color of darkness,
that long one should be meditated
at the head of the target's body,
and likewise at the feet and the throat.
This seventh and eighth
swiftly drive away.
His mind grown dull and ruined,
he will wander the whole earth,
and in this present life
he will not turn back again.

The syllable called ḷ, the binder of existence,
like a crow's wing,
the practitioner should meditate at the target's eyes,
at the throat and at the secret place.
With the long vowel called ḹ
one imagines the target wrapped round.
Merely by meditating it on the target's body,
it is supreme for causing enmity.
He is reviled by all beings,
scorned by all people,
and like whey from which the butter has been drawn,
he will be cast aside by all people.

Colored like cokla vermilion
and like moist lhambu,
the syllable called e, king of vowels,
is placed at the secret place, the navel and the heart.
The syllable called ai, of great splendor,
colored like red lead and the sun,
one should meditate at the target's eyes,
and likewise at the mouth and throat.
When the supreme practitioner
merely performs this act in his mind,
he sees the three realms in their entirety
as no larger than a thumb.

Face turned directly toward face,
by the portions of those letters
one draws in the line of the breath with the vine.
The portions, the vine: the sacred commitment of control.

The vowel called a,
with the drop and the half-moon,
with the two moons, the visarga:
this is the seal of the supreme syllable
of all the buddhas;
it truly abides as the image of the Buddha.
Thus āli and kāli, the vowels and consonants,
are declared to be "all the elixirs."
Having dissolved them into the principal deity,
one joins them with Vajrasattva and the rest.
After that, thereafter,
all the letters are to be inserted.
It is declared that this gives protection
for as long as appearances exist.
What is called the drop is the mind;
the intrinsic nature of the mind is emptiness.
Truly joined with the two moons,
it is the seal of the supreme immutable.
Therefore the rites of the vowels
of the great-souled practitioner
swiftly bring under control
gods, asuras and humans.

The syllable called oṃ, like the sun,
possessed of a thousand blazing rays,
one should meditate at the target's head,
at the forehead, and especially at the heart.
The syllable called oṃ, ever blazing,
with the splendor of ten million suns,
one should meditate as pervading
the whole of the target's body.
Meditating it pervaded by the vowels
like the mass of fire at the end of the eon,
and meditating the target too
blazing fiercely with the fire of the vowels,
even an enemy who would live a hundred years
will fall at once.

a gathers everything in,
and it also averts harm.
ā is the request to depart,
and it also works the deity as one wishes.
If one imagines it in the womb of any woman,
her fluid will flow.
Especially for others as well,
one should perform the white and red concentration.
If a and the rest and the letters
are placed on the body, there is no need for examination.

Thus, of the rites of the vowels
that I have taught you,
the sixteen virtuous vowels
should be placed on the body as armor.
The syllable called ā is placed on the two feet;
a one imagines on the two calves;
ī is to be placed on the thighs;
the syllable called i abides at the secret place;
u is at the root of the navel;
the syllable called ū at the center of the navel;
ṛ is placed above the navel;
the syllable called ṝ is at the heart;
the syllable called ḷ is placed at the throat;
likewise ḹ at the palate;
the syllable called e at the mouth,
and likewise e at the nose;
o at the ears,
the syllable called au at the forehead;
oṃ is placed on the head,
and the syllable called aḥ is placed on the back.
For subjugation and summoning they are red;
for killing, all are blue.

If one imagines them as like smoke,
expulsion will result, without a doubt.
This blessing by all the vowels
is the clear reality of oneself.
The practitioner, noble in nature,
having thus put on the armor,
after the vowels and the consonants
should then apply the rites with great care.
Then the reality of the vidyā arises.
The rites are to be performed constantly:
the rites that run from ka
to the very end at ma, hear them with great understanding.
Gods and asuras together with humans,
all the three realms,
all of these come under the power
of the practitioner who flourishes greatly.
That which grants the fruits the mind desires,
I shall now explain.

Having already explained to you, Beautiful One,
the sixteen vowels,
I now explain the rites of the letters ka and the rest,
performed according to one's wish.

Goddess, the first letter,
equal in brilliance to the sun,
like a heap of vermilion,
is the great letter endowed with the bindu.
The practitioner should meditate on it at the target's heart,
at the navel, and at the head.
One should imagine it piercing
into the target's body and pervading it.
By it the body is thrown into turmoil;
stupefied and bereft of mind,
as if in a faint, the target comes under one's power.
As long as it remains in the body,
so long does the target remain under one's power,
and for as long as the target lives,
he comes under it, he himself together with his wealth.

And the letter called kha, O you of great diligence,
like glistening moisture,
the color of the rising sun,
endowed with the bindu, of great brilliance,
clear and radiant,
should be placed at the target's heart,
at the head, and at the feet.
Imagine the target pierced by it,
stupefied and bereft of mind.
The best of practitioners should meditate
that his body is thrown into turmoil,
that he is intoxicated and faints.
Through this one becomes utterly victorious,
and because of the greatness of the lord of letters,
for as long as the target lives,
he comes under the power of the great-natured practitioner, together with his wealth.

The great seed called ga
is the vow of one's own deity.
With the visarga, of great brilliance,
the chief one, colored like a rain cloud,
it should be imagined at the target's head, at the throat,
and above all at the heart.
If one imagines the target's body
pierced through and through by the letter,
the body, constantly thrown into turmoil,
trembles and comes under one's power.
All hostile enemies without exception,
with their persons and their wealth,
their households and everything else,
it destroys completely, leaving nothing,
for as long as the sun and moon endure.

The one called gha is Mahāmāyā,
equal in brilliance to the fire at the end of an eon,
adorned above with the bindu,
like the fluid of the kusuma flower.
Merely by imagining it at the target's navel,
secret place, heart, mouth,
and head,
the target, intoxicated with joy,
bereft of mind and enfeebled,
for as long as he lives,
comes wholly under one's power,
he himself together with his wealth.

The one called ṅa, of great brilliance,
stands at the end of its class of letters.
It is the arrow of desire
and brings the three worlds under one's power.
It should be imagined at the target's secret place;
it causes the fluids to flow.
Having entered the secret place,
it pierces everywhere.
Pierced by it in every joint of the limbs,
the target, intoxicated,
together with his wealth and his person,
very swiftly comes under one's power.
As long as he lives,
he will never be free of passion for the practitioner.

With all five letters as well
one should put armor on oneself.
The one called ka is placed at the heart,
the one called kha at the head,
the one called ga at the crown,
the one called gha is made the armor,
and the one called ṅa should be placed
on the practitioner's left hand.
Then apply them to the target for subjugation,
summoning, killing, and the rest.
If the rite is performed in this way,
accomplishment will come, with no need for investigation.

The one called ca is the color of smoke,
the letter of the abode of wind.
The practitioner should imagine it
at the target's heart, head, and feet.
One should imagine the target, borne on the wind,
moving about everywhere,
and, having become bereft of mind,
as though dwelling in the sky,
one should meditate that he is carried by the wind
from one place to another.
Through this yoga of meditative concentration,
even a very steadfast target
will be expelled; of this there is no doubt.
Because the target's body has been cast out,
for as long as he lives
he will not come back again.

The great letter called cha,
clear, the color of black collyrium,
fully endowed with the twofold mark,
should be imagined at the target's head.
His head is pervaded by a wind
like a mass of smoke.
Imagine that in a short while
it encircles the target's body.
Again, imagining cha with the visarga
at the navel,
if one recites it three times,
this letter is supreme for expulsion.
The target is stupefied and bereft of mind;
the enemy, his luster lost,
wanders over the whole face of the earth,
and for as long as he lives
he will not turn back again.

The letter called ja is victorious,
smoke-colored, of great brilliance,
possessed of rays and fiercely blazing,
its particles radiating by the thousand.
One should meditate that the letter enters
the target's head and secret place.
Every joint of his limbs is pierced,
and he remains as if bereft of mind.
Being completely filled by the letter,
he then falls into a faint,
and one should meditate that day and night he is stupefied
and does not awaken.
He will go everywhere
across the earth with its trees and forests.
These rites of expulsion
I have explained to you, Virtuous One.
For as long as he lives
he will not come back again.

The greatly powerful one called jha,
in the heart of the one to be subdued,
blazes like the sun
and radiates bright rays of light.
Adorned with a drop and a half-moon,
one should visualize the letter
upon the head, the heart,
and the navel of the one to be subdued.
The best of practitioners should imagine
the body of the one to be subdued
torn from its vital essence by the blaze.
By this the one to be subdued will faint,
and will be driven away or die,
or else will go mad.
Since he is felled by the letter,
he will never come back again;
across the earth with its forests
he will wander, mad, without a doubt.
By this one called jha
he is swiftly driven away, without a doubt.

The supreme letter called ṇa
has a variegated color.
Blazing with a thousand rays of light
like the palāśa flower,
with a thousand streams of water pouring down,
one should bathe that person's body.
By this, together with his wealth,
he faints and is bereft of his mind,
is stupefied and swiftly brought under control.

The supreme letter called ṭa
has the color of Indra's weapon.
With a thousand blazing rays of light
one should imagine the letter ablaze.
Imagine it on the forehead,
the throat and the heart of the one to be subdued.
In the sky above the one to be subdued,
always imagine above the crown of his head
the drop at the doorway of nectar.
Imagine snow falling
like lightning faintly flickering.
If the practitioner imagines
the one to be subdued trembling in every limb and joint,
fainting, stupefied and intoxicated,
then for as long as the sun and moon endure,
together with his wealth and his very self,
he will be brought wholly under control by this.
Thus I have explained to you
the action of the king ṭa.

Next I shall explain
the supreme action of the second letter.
The letter called ṭha
is held to resemble Mahendra (the great Indra).
Adorned on its crown with a drop of nectar,
likewise adorned with lightning and thunder,
one should meditate on the supreme letter.
With streams of nectar pouring down,
sprinkle the whole body of the one to be subdued.
Imagine it abiding in the head,
the throat and the heart of the one to be subdued.
Piercing the head of the one to be subdued
and pervading his whole body,
the mantra practitioner should constantly meditate with the letter
that the one to be subdued is intoxicated and trembling.
Then, for as long as he lives,
fainting and stupefied, he is brought under control.

Next, the one called ḍa
is the third king of letters.
Its supreme actions
hear with respect and a one-pointed mind.
The one called ḍa, together with a drop,
has a shape like a vajra.
It is green and yellow like an utpala,
and radiates bright rays of light everywhere.
By imagining it on the crown of the one to be subdued,
the joints of the one to be subdued are pierced and split apart.
If one meditates that the eyes of the one to be subdued are swimming,
then, his body trembling all over,
for as long as he lives,
stupefied, he is brought under control.

Next, another supreme action,
which demands great care:
the king of letters called dha,
the maker of great wonders,
has the color of Indra's weapon
and blazes like the sun.
Having imagined the letter in the heart,
the navel and the throat of the one to be subdued,
with the bodhicitta trickling down abundantly
one should bathe his body.
If one meditates that by this he grows dizzy, faints
and falls asleep,
then by this, before long,
he is stupefied and comes under the practitioner's control.
Through the application of this rite
he will never be free of attachment.

Next, the action of the final letter after dha:
hear it diligently, just as you wish.
The one called ṇa has the color of smoke,
faintly blazing and bright;
from the stainless smoke
bright rays of light radiate.
It pervades completely
the whole body of the one to be subdued.
Meditate on rays of light like a thousand flashes of lightning
above the one to be subdued.
Since he is filled with a hundred rays of light,
he becomes intoxicated and faints.
By this, for as long as he lives,
he comes under that one's control.

The supreme letter called ta
is bright like lightning in its rays,
its color like a peacock's throat.
Imagine this letter placed
in the heart, the throat, the secret place
and the navel of the one to be subdued, piercing him.
Since he is stupefied by that letter,
the one to be subdued is bereft of his mind,
and having become stupefied and faint,
for as long as he lives
he is intoxicated and controlled, and it is not otherwise.

The supreme letter called tha,
which stands next to it,
is like the color of lapis
with the radiance of a blue utpala petal.
Place it in the heart, the forehead
and likewise the feet of the one to be subdued.
